# Preface.

I do not venture to publish this periodical in order to increase the number of libraries and journals with which Germany is flooded—and perhaps even plagued; but rather to guide the now-attentive public, and thus also those who do not belong to the <term>Masonic Order<note>Original: *Freymäurerorden*. A fraternal organization that, during the Enlightenment, was often the subject of intense public curiosity and "prejudice" or misunderstanding by those outside its circles.</note></term>, toward more accurate opinions—previously suppressed by prejudice—concerning the ultimate purpose of our endeavors. The task of instruction is more difficult than is commonly believed when it concerns matters which, by their peculiar nature, must re-

<note>The text ends mid-word with 'must re-', which would be completed as 'must remain' on the following page. Original German: 'sol-len'.</note>

a 3

<summary>In this opening of the preface, the author explains that the goal of the new periodical is to correct public misconceptions and prejudices regarding the Masonic Order.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, Enlightenment, public opinion, German periodicals, Masonic Order, preface, instruction</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the Preface from page V. The German text begins with the completion of the word 'sollen' (must/shall) from the previous page. The catchword 'folge' at the bottom indicates that the next page will likely begin with 'folgen' (to follow).</meta>

...main and must. The fundamental truths are, of course, the <term>premises<note>In logic, a premise is a foundational statement used to reach a conclusion. The author is arguing that if the secret foundations of the Order cannot be revealed, the public cannot be expected to understand their results.</note></term>: if these may not be revealed, how can one trust the conclusions? We are not permitted to [reveal them], that is true, and our vows remain sacred to us. 

But we can at least say this much for thinking minds: that they should not take the Order for what common rumor usually takes it to be; we can publicly work upon the <term>literature of Freemasonry<note>Original: *Litteratur der Freymäurerey*. During the 18th century, a massive volume of both pro- and anti-Masonic books were published; the author intends to curate and explain these for a general audience.</note></term>, that is, provide information about printed writings which fall into the hands of other readers just as easily as those of our brothers; we can bring the scattered history into chronological and <term>synchronistic<note>Original: *synchronistische*. This refers to a method of historical writing that compares events happening at the same time in different places or contexts to show their relationship.</note></term> order; we can bring to light the noble-minded intentions which have always been the driving forces of Masonic actions, and also entertain the reader with individual occurrences, with poetic or other clever inventions. If he occasionally encounters mystical passages; then

The catalog <note>Original: *Verzeichniß*.</note> of the writings published by the local Mother Lodge <note>Original: *Mutterloge*. In Freemasonry, this refers to the primary lodge from which other lodges are descended or the administrative center of a specific jurisdiction.</note> on page 157 and following is limited only to the past year. To produce such a list dating back to its first founding was my initial intention. XI

Furthermore, it is my earnest wish that all Freemason lodges <note>Original: *Freymäurerlogen*.</note> be so kind as to share with me a list <note>Original: *Verzeichniß*.</note> of their published works in chronological order, including the full or unabbreviated title, the format, the place of publication, and the page count, sent to the address of the publisher.

Should my efforts succeed in obtaining the same from lodges in other parts of Europe where the Order <term>Orden: the Order of Freemasonry</term> flourishes, whether in secret or in public, then this literary field would benefit immensely; I therefore ask all distant brothers <note>Original: *Brüder*.</note> who maintain a correspondence <note>Original: *Briefwechsel*.</note> with these lodges or their members to be of assistance in this regard. Accurate and interesting reports of remarkable occurrences, of charitable foundations <note>Original: *milden Stiftungen*. In the 18th century, these were often funds established by lodges for the relief of the poor, widows, or the education of orphans.</note>, of celebrated festivities, and so forth, shall also be welcome to me. is dedicated to this, as well as to poems, songs, fruitful observations, and instructive tasks that touch upon the moral principles of the Order <term>Orden: the Order of Freemasonry</term>, and historical discussions—such as the methods of examination used by the ancients <note>A reference to the rigorous initiation trials of ancient mystery schools, which Masons viewed as their precursors.</note>, or the utility of hieroglyphs <note>In this context, "hieroglyphs" refers to the symbolic images and emblems used by the lodge to convey moral lessons.</note> and the symbolic method of teaching.

->Berlin, April 21, 1778.<-

# Preface.

Do not be alarmed by this title, my brothers! It is not at all my intention to draft a history of our Order <note>The author is likely referring to a secret society, such as the Freemasons or the Illuminati, common in late 18th-century German-speaking lands.</note> from authentic documents or from the sacred archives of our society. Such a work, as interesting as it might be, is not for the world—indeed, it is not even for every brother. If the time had come, or if it were even possible to reveal our inner workings to the world, how people would marvel! In a clear, continuous sequence, one would witness the progress of the human spirit and the history of the arts and sciences. Where so-called “philosophical history” <note>In this period, "philosophical history" meant history written to find patterns and progress in human development, often critiquing mere lists of kings and battles.</note> offers us only disjointed rhapsodies, fables, far-fetched conjectures, and plays of the imagination, one would find truth and cohesion, with every circumstance proven by facts <note>original: "Factis"</note>. Furthermore, our history would clearly and distinctly unfold the true causes and the eternal connections of things in those areas where common history leaves us in the dark regarding the most significant revolutions of the human race, or cites childish causes as the driving forces. But I draw a curtain over a subject which even the holiest and wisest approach with fear and trembling. Few eyes are strong enough to behold the light of eternal truth. —

A 2

<summary>The author introduces a preface to the history of a secret order, claiming that while the public history of the world is full of myths, the Order's private archives contain the true, factual history of human progress.</summary>
<keywords>secret societies, historiography, Enlightenment philosophy, human progress, archives, truth</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface. On the previous page, the author distinguished his work from a secret "internal" history of the Order, which he claims remains hidden. Here, he defines his project as a history of public perception.</meta>

My history is nothing more than a collection of the various judgments that those outside our circle <note>original: "die, so ausser uns sind"</note> have passed upon our society in different countries and at different times. It is much like the book by Meursius <note>Johannes Meursius (1579–1639), a Dutch classical scholar who wrote *Eleusinia* (1619) regarding the ancient Greek Eleusinian Mysteries.</note> *On the Eleusinian Mysteries*, which likewise contains nothing of the actual objects, teachings, and intentions of those famous mysteries; instead, it offers indifferent ceremonies, conjectures, gossip, and slanders from the uninitiated—or at least from those who never saw anything more than the mere external ceremonial.

I have adopted the first edition of the English Book of Constitutions <note>This refers to James Anderson's *The Constitutions of the Free-Masons* (1723), which codified the rules of modern "speculative" Freemasonry.</note> as a fixed starting point because, since that time, our society has particularly stirred the attention and curiosity of the world. Everyone was eager to know a secret that was so carefully guarded. Idle and cunning minds took advantage of this public passion, and thus arose the great multitude of writings in which a "secret" was laid bare to the whole world. They were read with the greatest greed; but because they contained nothing but silly trifles, they were forgotten just as quickly, and most now belong to the category of rare books. Therefore, I will always present the essentials of these works in a complete summary, and in this way rescue them once more from oblivion—a fate that their authors certainly would least have expected to receive from a member of the <term>Order<gloss>A common term for a secret society, implying a structured hierarchy and a shared rule of life, similar to a knightly or monastic order.</gloss></term>. At the same time, I will also cite the writings which from time to time

<meta>The text cuts off here. The catchword "von" (from/of) indicates the next page begins with "von..."</meta>

<summary>The author explains that his book is a history of public opinion and "exposures" rather than the society's internal secrets, comparing his work to early scholarship on the ancient Greek mysteries. He chooses the 1723 English Book of Constitutions as his starting point for tracking public curiosity and the "silly trifles" published by those seeking to profit from the society's mystery.</summary>

<keywords>public opinion, Eleusinian Mysteries, Johannes Meursius, Book of Constitutions, 1723, secrets, uninitiated, rare books, summaries</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface. The catchword "Ge-" from the previous page matches the starting word "Gliedern" (members), which concludes a sentence about books defending the society.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>
<sig>A 3</sig>

published by members of the Order, which contain either refutations of such shallow productions <note>original: "seichten Geburten," literally "shallow births," referring to poorly conceived books or ideas</note>, defenses for the Society, or reflections on its true spirit.

But why this work? Besides the amusement I believe I provide to my brothers by presenting them a clear overview of all the follies, absurdities, ridiculous tales, and little stories with which the world has burdened itself at our expense for so many years and in so many different countries; at the same time, I hope to convince the reasonable and fair-minded portion of the public—whose respect we certainly value—that the objects of our pursuits are of a much nobler and more sublime nature. Would our Society still exist if we were held together by no other bond than a mere inclination for pleasure? One would have to be ignorant of human fickleness to claim such a thing. The enthusiastic urge with which people surrender themselves to every new object is a characteristic trait of our nature. However, once this curiosity is satisfied, the matter becomes indifferent and begins to lose its interest. This is why most societies that had nothing but social pleasure as their goal—not even excepting the famous <term>Order of the Pug</term> <note>The *Mopsorden* was a pseudo-masonic society founded in the 1740s by Catholics as an alternative to Freemasonry; they famously used the pug dog as a symbol of loyalty and reliability.</note>—have scarcely survived their founders. Finally, if we occupied ourselves with such trifles as those contained in the writings that claim to have discovered our <term>Secret</term> <note>original: "Geheimniß." In this context, it refers to the ritual secrets and internal knowledge of the Freemasons that "exposure" writers claimed to reveal.</note>: what reasonable man would [remain] in our 

<summary>The author justifies his compilation of anti-Masonic "follies" as a way to amuse his fellow members while proving to the public that the Order is based on noble principles rather than mere social pleasure. He argues that if the Society were only about entertainment, like the short-lived Order of the Pug, it would have long since vanished.</summary>
<keywords>Order of the Pug, public opinion, masonic secrets, institutional longevity, defense of Freemasonry, social societies</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s preface. It concludes the argument that the Society’s survival and the high caliber of its members prove its legitimacy, despite the existence of fraudulent "pseudo-Masonic" groups that have tarnished its reputation. The catchword "Zu-" at the bottom indicates the transition to "Zudem" (Moreover) or "Zuerst" (First) on the following page.</meta>

...remain a society? And yet we still count the noblest and best of the human race among our members.

I do not wish to claim, for that reason, that everything written in these books <note>The author refers to the various "exposure" books or anti-Masonic pamphlets mentioned on the previous page that claimed to reveal Masonic secrets.</note> is merely plucked from thin air or entirely fabricated. It is very possible that societies have existed here and there which, instead of focusing on real matters, amused themselves with such trifles, and which—to entice the ignorant all the more strongly—adopted the name of <term>Freemasons</term>. Wretched, base souls were admitted into such groups—souls to whom nothing is so sacred that they would not trample it underfoot—and these people believed they had discovered the <term>Secret</term> <note>original: "Geheimniß"</note> of the Masons, and subsequently sought to communicate it to the entire world.

It is a stroke of luck for our society that we possess the means to see through such wretched individuals and to keep them away from the thresholds of our <term>Sanctuary</term> <note>original: "Heiligthum." In Masonic rhetoric, this refers to the lodge or the collective spiritual space where the "true" work occurs, inaccessible to the unworthy.</note>. This circumstance deserves to be particularly noted, because it has certainly already given rise to many misunderstandings. And without doubt, this is the reason why various great, learned, and sensible men—who, had they known us, would certainly not have withheld their esteem—have passed judgments on our society that are not the most favorable.

Furthermore, I wish to recount the various fates and persecutions which have affected the external part of our society (for our inner essence is elevated above all the rage and malice of men) in various countries, and at the same time cite the <term>official decrees</term> <note>original: "obrigkeitliche Verordnungen." During the 18th century, Freemasonry faced numerous legal bans from monarchs and the Catholic Church, often due to fears of political subversion or the group's refusal to reveal its secrets to the state.</note> which have been issued against us. To-

7
A 4

At that very time, when a portion of the public scarcely credited us with common sense and looked upon us as children occupied with trifles, the priests of all <term>confessions</term> <note>The various branches of Christianity, such as Catholicism, Lutheranism, and Calvinism.</note> believed that we were declared enemies of the Christian religion, having nothing less than its total overthrow as our aim. The Bishop of Rome <note>The Pope</note> sought to shatter us with thunderbolts of excommunication. The cruel <term>Inquisition</term> <note>The ecclesiastical court system of the Catholic Church intended to suppress heresy.</note> pursued us with the utmost fury, and Protestant clergy condemned us and branded us as heretics from their pulpits. Politicians regarded us as disturbers of public security and enemies of the state; and finally, the fair sex <note>A common historical term for women.</note> accused us, out of vengeance, of the most unnatural vices, because for the most important reasons we could not grant them entry into our <term>Sanctuary</term> <note>original: "Heiligthum." Here referring to the Masonic lodge and its private rituals.</note>. * These times have passed: in most countries, people have finally been convinced of the innocence and purity of our society through experience, and no one believes any longer that (as a certain writer says) a hostile demon is hidden behind a cloud that drops blessings upon the land. In most countries, we enjoy the protection of the laws, and the wisest regents of Europe honor us as the most faithful and obedient of their subjects. The persecution which only recently some of our brothers in Naples endured (and of

<margin>*</margin> In short: in Rome, they accused us of <term>Epicureanism</term> <note>The philosophical pursuit of pleasure and avoidance of pain, often unfairly equated with godless hedonism by critics of the time.</note>; in Florence, of <term>Molinism</term> <note>A theological doctrine concerning free will, named after Luis de Molina.</note>; in Paris, the Jesuits hated us because they took us for <term>Jansenists</term> <note>A Catholic movement emphasizing original sin and divine grace, which was often at odds with the Jesuit order.</note>. In Holland, we were considered followers of the House of Orange; in Protestant Germany, atheists; and in Catholic Germany, it was claimed that through our instigation some unbelievers wished to dispute the "Hail Holy Queen" <note>original: "salve Regina," a traditional Catholic hymn to the Virgin Mary.</note> with Mother Mary.

<summary>The author recounts the various historical persecutions the Freemasons faced from the Catholic Church, Protestant clergy, and political leaders, before asserting that the order is now recognized as innocent and loyal by most European regents.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s preface. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing how European regents honor Freemasons as faithful and obedient subjects. This page begins by addressing the history of persecution that the Order has endured.</meta>

8

(which we hope, to the honor of our enlightened age, will be the last) has turned out to our advantage. The State has realized that it is unjust to suppress those who have bound themselves to the practice of all civil and social virtues, and they now enjoy—after enduring much suffering—peace, security, and the protection of the laws.

Even the priests have reconciled with us, since they have found among our members so many zealous followers of religion. However, as malice can never be idle, it seeks to wound us from another side. Formerly we were considered enemies of religion, and now people are beginning to cry out against us as <term>enthusiasts</term>, <term>fanatics</term>, and <term>visionaries</term> <note>original: "Schwärmer." In the 18th century, this was a highly derogatory term used to describe people who were seen as having lost their rational grip on reality due to religious or mystical obsession.</note>, even though we are everywhere sincere, zealous, and faithful followers and friends of Christianity. But on this point, too, time will justify us.

I have neither copied, reworked, nor used Erhard’s <note>Likely a reference to a contemporary historian of the era who wrote on the origins of the Order.</note> *History of the Illustrious Masonic Order* as a foundation, as my intention is entirely different from his. I have nothing in common with him other than that I have drawn from the very same sources from which he pulled his information, because unfortunately no others are available; however, I have consulted far more writers and also, here and there, where duty permitted, reduced wavering rumors to truths and corrected various events using more reliable sources.

<meta>This page continues the historical introduction. On the previous page, the author discussed how the Masonic Order has transitioned from a period of persecution to one of legal protection and social acceptance, and he distinguished his historical approach from that of earlier writers like Erhard.</meta>

9
A 5

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal rule consisting of a central floral/foliate motif flanked by thick and thin parallel lines ending in small flourishes.</image-desc>

The **Freemasons** had, since time immemorial, a manuscript in their possession which they called the **Book of Constitutions**. This volume contained not only their laws and duties but also the history of the **art of building** <note>original: "Baukunst." In Masonic contexts, this refers to both the physical craft of architecture and the symbolic "Royal Art" of self-improvement.</note> from its origins up to the present day. To save the effort of manual copying, Brother **James Anderson** was commissioned in 1721 by the then Grand Master, the **Duke of Montagu**, to carefully review the old **Gothic Constitutions** <note>original: "Gothischen Constitutionen." These are the "Old Charges," a collection of medieval documents that provided the legendary history and regulations of the stonemasons' craft before the era of modern Grand Lodges.</note>, to prepare an extract from them for the benefit and use of the brethren, and to arrange the whole work in such a way that it would be clear and understandable only to the brethren. He undertook this task, and in 1723, the first edition appeared in London under the following title: *The Constitutions of the Freemasons. Containing the History, Charges, Regulations, etc., of that most ancient and right worshipful Fraternity, for the Use of the Lodges. London. Printed by William Hunter for John Senex at the Globe and John Hooke at the Fleur-de-lis <note>original: "Flower de-luce." A common English variant of the French "fleur-de-lis" used in heraldry and shop signs.</note> across from St. Dunstan's Church, in Fleet Street. In the year of masonry 5732 <note>The Masonic calendar, or Anno Lucis, typically adds 4,000 years to the current year. Though the text here reads 5732, the original 1723 edition of Anderson's book lists the year as 5723.</note>, in the year of our Lord <note>original: "anno Domini."</note> 1723, large quarto <note>original: "4 mj." (quarto maior). A book size where the printing sheet is folded twice to create four leaves.</note>, 13½ sheets.*

<summary>This page describes the creation and publication of James Anderson's 1723 "Book of Constitutions," which modernized the fraternity's ancient hand-written records into a printed regulatory and historical volume.</summary>
<keywords>James Anderson, Duke of Montagu, Book of Constitutions, Gothic Constitutions, London, 1723, printing history, Old Charges, masonry calendar</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the history of the Masonic Order. The previous page detailed the 1723 publication of the "Book of Constitutions" by James Anderson under the commission of the Grand Master, the Duke of Montagu.</meta>

10

The editor says of the intention of this work: All well-ordered societies had their secrets and will continue to have them in the future; the **Freemasons** likewise have theirs, which they have never made known in the manuscript; therefore, one cannot expect to find them in print. Meanwhile, an experienced brother can find many useful instructions by the light which shines through all sides of this book, which an uninitiated person is not capable of finding. That is, he can develop the true history of the Order, which lies hidden under the veil of the history of masonry.

The so-called **radical free-thinking** <note>original: "Starkgeisterey." A contemporary 18th-century term for "strong spirits" or radical Enlightenment thinkers who were often accused of atheism or undermining traditional religious authority.</note>, an eternal blemish on our century in the eyes of posterity, began at that time especially to raise its head against Christianity. The theologians were thus extremely suspicious of every new phenomenon, and just as even zeal for the good can be exaggerated, so it happened here: they found **indifferentism** <note>The theological claim that all religions are equally valid or that religious differences are unimportant.</note> and **naturalism** <note>The philosophical belief that only natural laws and forces operate in the world, excluding spiritual or supernatural explanations.</note> in the most excellent works, which even now bring honor to religion. Anyone who did not blindly profess allegiance to their party was viewed as an enemy. This was especially the fate of those who, following the pattern of Christ and the Apostles, preached tolerance and moderation toward those in error. One can therefore easily imagine from what point of view the theologians of that time viewed our society, since the **Book of Constitutions** makes it our first duty never to act as despots over the conscience and conviction of our brothers.

Benjamin Bieler 1) therefore said in a journal of that time, on the occasion of our Book of Constitutions, regarding our society: “Just as there have always been people found who, like the descendants of the children of Noah, wanted to be free and not allow themselves to be restricted by the laws of God; so too are the **Freemasons**, who, like the ancient **Babylonians**, drag together bricks, stones, and lime, and unite to build a new **tower of religion**, whose height all people admire, and whose peak shall reach into the heavens. These are indeed such workers who build a wall of gutter-mud, and have the learned scoundrels as whitewashers <note>The term "whitewashers" here implies those who cover up the flaws or "filth" of a structure with a thin layer of intellectual justification.</note> among them. They were formerly called **Naturalists**, **Indifferentists**, **Brownists**, **Independents**, **Deists**, and **Libertines**. <note>These were various 17th and 18th-century labels for religious dissenters, free-thinkers, or those perceived as having no fixed religious principles.</note> They emerged around the year 1740 in Holland and England, as can be seen more clearly from Josua Arnd’s *Dictionary of Ecclesiastical Antiquities* <note>original: "Josuæ Arndii Lexico Antiqu. Ecclesiast."</note> p. 502 seqq. and G. Horn’s *Ecclesiastical History* <note>original: "G. Hornii Hist. Eccles."</note> p. 292. A few years ago in England, they took the special name of **Freemasons**, because a mason not only works in the open air, but also always builds on high, and prepares a building according to his own pleasure.”

It is one of the first duties of natural equity to burden no one with something that cannot be proven. Yet can one seek this equity among men who are heated by a **party spirit**? <note>original: "Partheygeist." Could one believe that worse, more malicious, and more unfounded accusations could be imagined? And yet, this man is a very fair and modest judge in comparison to the one whose judgment I will now cite.

Gottfried Kohlreif <margin>2) *Inexpressibly great tribulations, the beginning of which is close at hand for the orthodox,* etc. <note>original: "Unsägliche große Trübsalen deren Anfang den Rechtgläubigen nahe bevorstehet 2c." Are those the—"

According to this sort of hermeneutics <term>Hermeneutics: The science or method of interpreting texts, especially the Bible or legal documents.</term>, one could find the greatest errors in the works of the most orthodox theologians, indeed, even in the Bible itself. What unbiased person with simple common sense would have found anything other than the sublime thought contained within the following passage of our Book of Constitutions: „Whoever rightly understands the whole Art of Masonry will be neither a stupid atheist nor an irreligious freethinker <term>Freethinker: In this era, "Freygeist" was a loaded term for someone who rejected religious authority, often unfairly equated with immorality.</term>, much less act against his conscience,“—who would have found anything in this other than the thought: Everyone who has recognized the Creator in His boundless works will never harbor the nonsensical thought of doubting His existence, nor dishonor his life through vices that are contrary to the ultimate purpose of his high calling.

And yet this is precisely the passage from which our critic proves that we taught nothing less than atheism. How strong is the power of prejudice! How can partiality and the wrong point of view from which a thing is viewed lead even the most honest and upright man astray in his judgment; for our author truly is such a man, notwithstanding his weak intellect. He scolded and slandered us with the honest intention of championing the cause of God and religion. And I am certainly convinced that he would have [made it a matter of] conscience for himself <note>The sentence ends abruptly here, likely continuing on the following page.</note>

The Book of Constitutions <term>Book of Constitutions: The foundational rulebook of Freemasonry, first compiled by James Anderson in 1723, containing the history, charges, and regulations of the fraternity.</term> itself contains, first of all, a history of the art of building <term>Art of building: In German, "Baukunst." In Masonic tradition, this refers to both the history of architecture and the development of the craft from ancient times.</term> from the earliest ages, its origin and progress in various countries, and especially in England; in the subsequent editions, this history has been expanded more and more. I wish to cite only a single passage from this history, because it sheds much light on the origin of our customs and the fundamental constitution of our Order.

„If it served any purpose, it could easily be shown that from these ancient brotherhoods, both religious and secular orders of knighthood <term>Orders of knighthood: Chivalric orders such as the Knights Templar. Many 18th-century Masonic legends claimed that these medieval knights had adopted their secret ceremonies from the Masons.</term> in the following times borrowed many ceremonies; for none of them were better arranged, consecrated, or observed their laws and duties more inviolably than the Masons have done, who, at all times and among all nations, have maintained and propagated their freedom in a quite peculiar and excellent manner, and indeed in such a way that the most cunning and learned could not investigate it, though they have often tried; and this because the Masons of all nations know and love one another without needing the help of language <note>This refers to the "universal language" of Masonic signs and grips that allow members to recognize each other regardless of their native tongue.</note>.“

Following this are the duties of a Mason, which are extracted from old records <term>Old records: These are the "Old Charges," medieval manuscript regulations used by stonemasons' guilds which formed the basis for modern Masonic law.</term> and are read to the new brothers; specifically, duties toward God, the authorities <note>original: "Obrigkeit," meaning the civil government or magistracy.</note>, and those which the brothers must observe toward one another and toward strangers. In all of them, the spirit of true

This refers to religious dogmas or political opinions that Masons believed should not interfere with fraternal harmony.</note>; toward the authorities, he teaches obedience and submission, as well as toward the laws of the land; a Mason must never concern himself with government affairs for which he has no calling. Finally, the duties are prescribed which the brothers must practice among themselves. They are based entirely on the purest brotherly love. Each is bound to assist a worthy brother under all circumstances, and to pursue his affairs with the same zeal as his own. And certainly, the firmly established pillars of our Order will only then waver if we should ever be capable of losing sight of following these wise laws; otherwise, never. I wish to transcribe one more passage in which all these excellent teachings are concentrated.

<meta>This page continues the translation of the charges found in the Book of Constitutions. The previous page ended with the beginning of a sentence regarding the duties of a Mason toward his brothers.</meta>

> “...and render him all services, as far as they are consistent with your own honor and those duties you owe to yourself. Should a brother do you wrong, then turn—according to the ancient and laudable custom of our ancestors among all nations—to the Lodge; and as much as possible, never bring your disputes before a civil court <note>Masonic tradition emphasizes internal arbitration to maintain "harmony" and prevent public legal battles between members.</note>, but let yourselves be reconciled in kindness by your Master. If this were not possible, then even in conflict do not forget that you are brothers, and conduct it without hatred or bitterness, so that those who are outside our circle <note>original: "ausser uns sind." This refers to non-Masons, often termed "the profane" in traditional Masonic language.</note> are compelled to admire the influence of our laws. All honest Masons have done this from the beginning of the world and will continue to do so until the end of time. Amen, so mote it be! <term>So mote it be: The traditional Masonic conclusion to prayers and declarations, an archaic form of "so may it be."</term>”

Following this are the laws to be followed upon the establishment of a new Lodge, which I shall pass over in silence as they are generally known among us.

This is the brief summary of a work <note>This refers to the "Constitutions of the Free-Masons," first published by James Anderson in 1723, which codified the rules of the Premier Grand Lodge of England.</note> which all Masons recognize as the basis of their teachings and laws. I shall note the various editions of this work, as well as the translations produced in several countries, under the specific year in which they were published.

In the aforementioned year <note>Referring to the period of 1723–1724.</note>, on June 24th, the office of Grand Secretary was filled for the first time. The Grand Stewards were proposed, and fixed Masonic toasts <term>Masonic toasts: Formal, ritualized drinking of healths (Gesundheiten) during the festive meal following a meeting.</term> were introduced; and on November 16th, twelve Grand Stewards were appointed annually.

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the chronological history of the Grand Lodge of England, following the summary of Anderson's "Constitutions." It lists the succession of Grand Masters and the administrative milestones of the Order during the 1720s.</meta>

1724. In this year, the Duke of Richmond served as Grand Master. On November 21st, the Earl of Dalkeith made a proposal to establish a fund for distressed <note>original: "unglückliche." In a Masonic context, this refers to "distressed" or "unfortunate" brothers who have fallen into poverty or ill health.</note> Freemasons; and a Grand Treasurer was appointed.

1725. Lord Paisley was Grand Master: on November 27th, a committee for charity <note>original: "Almosendeputation." This committee was formed to manage the relief funds proposed the previous year.</note> was established, and regulations were issued for the distribution of benevolent contributions. The Grand Officers of 49 lodges were present. During this and the preceding year, the Order had already spread beyond England into many countries.

1726. The first Provincial Grand Masters were appointed under Grand Master Charles of Inchiquin. Their office is to serve as the immediate representatives of the Grand Master in their assigned provinces, and they simultaneously possess the power to establish lodges.

1727. The authority of the Grand Lodge of England was generally recognized throughout Europe under the Grand Mastership of Lord Coleraine. A deputation was also sent to Madrid to found a lodge there, which continues its work to this day, independent of the Grand Lodge of England.

1728. During this and the following year, Lord Kingston served as Grand Master. On November 26th, the office of the Stewards <note>The Stewards were responsible for organizing the Grand Festival and managing the logistics of the Grand Lodge meetings.</note> was reinstated and their number set at twelve; and on November 25th of this same year, the aforementioned Grand Master, Lord Kingston, gifted various utensils to the Lodge and also sent the first patent of constitution <note>A formal charter authorizing the legal operation of a Masonic lodge.</note> to the East Indies.

1729. On January 29th, a solemn procession of Masons took place in London. All the brothers went <meta>catchword: themselves (original: "sich")</meta>

When they arrived at the gate of the hall, twelve Stewards stood in two rows with white rods, and the procession moved through the gate in the aforementioned order, with the only difference being that now the Book of Constitutions <note>original: "Constitutionsbuch." The governing book of laws and history of the Grand Lodge.</note> was carried before the old Grand Master on a cushion by the Master of the oldest lodge; before the newly elected Grand Master, however, the Master of his own lodge carried the Sword of State <note>original: "Ordensschwert." A ceremonial sword symbolizing authority.</note> in his hand.

1730. The Duke of Norfolk was Grand Master, and under his Grand Mastership, the first lodges in the East Indies and America were established.

1731. Lord Lovel was Grand Master and granted a patent <note>A formal document authorizing the creation of a new lodge.</note> for the lodge in The Hague <note>original: "Haag"</note>. <meta>This page continues the bibliographic description of Samuel Prichard's famous Masonic exposure, *Masonry Dissected*, which was first mentioned at the end of page 20. The text begins mid-sentence with the name of the printer, William Hunter.</meta>

...William Hunter, for John Senex at the Globe and John Hooke &c. in the year of Masonry 5732, in the year of our Lord <note>original: "anno Domini"</note> 1732, May 4th.

Although this work—as I will show more extensively below—contains nothing but a few questions, mostly concerning the art of building, interwoven with ridiculous trade customs; it was nevertheless read so frequently that many editions had to be produced in a short time. The German translation appeared in 1736 in octavo <note>A book size where each sheet of paper is folded into eight leaves, creating 16 pages.</note> (2 sheets) under the title: *The Guild of Free Masons, or a General and Sincere Description of all its Branches, from their Origin to the Present Time, as an Impartial Report of their Actions in the Acceptance and Initiation of their Members, and the Three Distinct Degrees of Masonry*, published by Samuel Prichard, former member of a guild chamber; this was based on the fifth English edition. In all likelihood, this is the very same work of which the author of *The Shattered Freemasons* <note>original: "der zerschmetterten Freymäurer"</note> says was published in French in Liège <note>original: "Lüttich"</note> in 1736, thereafter translated into English, Italian, and German, and which was intended to contain a sincere discovery of the secrets of the Apprentices and Masters. The author is indeed called "Pachard" <note>A misspelling of Prichard.</note> there, and the French version is presented as the original document; however, since the mangling of names and incorrect dates are not so unusual for a Frenchman, one must not let this lead them astray.

<meta>This page continues from page 21, which discussed the publication and German translation of Samuel Prichard's Masonic exposure. In the book just mentioned, the following anecdote is told regarding the fate of the author: ⁵) “The Freemasons in London supposedly took him by force at night into the Grand Lodge, where many brothers were gathered. Thereupon, they allegedly tore his tongue from his throat, his heart from his body, and hung his corpse on a nail for as long as the lodge meeting lasted. After this, they were said to have burned him, scattered the ashes into the air, and sent news of this occurrence to all the lodges in the world. I, who have no reason to invent anything, says the author, have read these reports myself in Holland, Berlin, and Frankfurt, and have also often heard members of the Order say that this is the customary punishment for all traitors; likewise, that in every lodge there is a book in which the names of all traitors who are to be punished are marked with special characters.” Whether this anecdote is well-founded remains an open question. <note>This gruesome description is a classic example of 18th-century anti-Masonic "scare stories." These tales were intended to portray the society as a dangerous, bloodthirsty group to a public already suspicious of their secret meetings.</note>

In 1733, James Lyon <note>original: "Jacob Lyon"</note>, Earl of Strathmore, was Grand Master. <meta>This page continues the discussion of a scholar’s skepticism regarding Freemasonry, picking up from the previous page's unfinished sentence regarding whether secrets can truly remain hidden.</meta>

—they do not remain hidden." From this reasoning <note>original: "Raisonnement"; the author is critiquing the logic used by the skeptic mentioned on the previous page</note> one learns, if one did not already know it well enough, that a learned man can also speak heartily stupid nonsense. What evidence <note>original: "Data"</note> does this man have to believe that we occupy ourselves with childish trifles? From that which he cites regarding the compatibility and benevolence of our brothers, he could certainly have concluded the opposite. Secrets that are communicated to everyone and without any distinction are not worth the fuss; that is an established truth. Yet was the man too learned for the very natural thought to occur to him, that we—like other sensible people—would handle our secrets with caution, and not throw them at the first person we meet? If so, then his doubts regarding the importance of the secret would have been vanished at once.

Some time later, Antonius Musa <note>A pseudonym for one of the Florentine scholars reporting to Köhler in Göttingen</note> sent him an excerpt from the Book of Constitutions <note>The "Constitutions of the Freemasons," first published by James Anderson in 1723, which outlined the history and regulations of the craft</note>, which he had found with much effort. One will not be surprised at this, because upon its first appearance it was distributed only among brothers, and consequently was not known at all among other scholars. At the same time, he further noted that the Freemasons had secret constitutions besides this one, which were only made known to the brothers, and that they swore no oath, but only pledged silence upon their honor.

"The secret itself," he says, "I consider to be trifles, such as how they recognize one another, or practice acts of charity among themselves: for it is certain that the society has large sums of money, and performs many acts of mercy, just as other smaller societies do in smaller..."

Some time afterward, Antonius Musa <note>Likely a pseudonym; Antonius Musa was the historical physician to the Roman Emperor Augustus, but the name was often adopted by Enlightenment-era writers.</note> sent him an excerpt from the Book of Constitutions <term>Book of Constitutions: the governing rulebook and history of a Masonic Grand Lodge, first published by James Anderson in 1723.</term>, which he had discovered with great effort. --- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues from Page 25, completing the thoughts of the critic Antonius Musa regarding the nature of the Masonic secret, before moving on to the theories of the historian Johann David Köhler.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>

...reveal how things stand. In the accounts, in the science of the distribution and use of funds, I believe, lies the secret, and this must be easy and trivial, because so many know it and learn it so quickly." According to this idea, however flawed it may be in other respects, our secret would be a **trade secret** <term>Handlungsgeheimniß: a business or commercial secret, specifically regarding the "handling" of money and goods</term>. And even assuming it were such a thing, how can Mr. Musa dare to present it as a triviality? Will not every merchant shrug their shoulders with pity at such arrogant ignorance? I do not believe that anyone claiming to be a man of science could still be found who would first need it proven to them that trade has important secrets, and that it is unthinkable without those which constitute, as it were, its very soul. This comparison cannot, therefore, work to our disadvantage in the slightest, however much this Florentine scholar <note>Referring to Antonius Musa, identified here as being from Florence, who was quoted on the previous page</note> believed he could disparage us by it.

Mr. Köhler <note>Johann David Köhler (1684–1755), a prominent German historian and scholar who took a keen interest in the history of the Freemasons</note> then presents his own conjectures about our Order; and although he is fair enough not to take us for children, as his two correspondents do, but rather attributes something substantial to us, his humble thoughts nonetheless contain so much that is adventurous that one cannot read them without laughing. To him, it is especially questionable why specifically the "dirty craft of masonry" <note>original: "dreckigte Mauerhandwerk"; a derogatory reference to the physical labor of stone-cutting compared to the "gentlemanly" nature of speculative Freemasonry</note> was deemed worthy of the honor of being elevated to such a respectable and excellent brotherhood, and according to his opinion, our greatest secret must lie within this choice. "For the great antiquity of **architecture** or **masonry**," he says, "cannot be the cause of this, because, if this..."

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues from Page 26, which discussed Johann David Köhler’s skepticism regarding the Freemasons and his disparagement of masonry as a "dirty craft." The text continues to argue that the guild of tailors has a more ancient and "cleaner" pedigree than that of the masons.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>

...should be taken into consideration, the honorable and clean tailors’ guild certainly deserved the preference. The indecent nakedness discovered by the first humans after their fall <note>Referring to Adam and Eve in the Garden of Eden after eating the forbidden fruit</note> forced them to cover themselves with clothes sooner than provide themselves with houses, in order to protect themselves from frost and heat. If this covering was not sufficient against the harshness of the weather, the shade of thick-leaved trees, or the caves and crevices of the mountains and rocks, could preserve them from all hardship. It was an idea of the Canaanite Enoch <note>Hanoch/Enoch, the son of Cain, traditionally credited in biblical interpretation with building the first city</note> to build the first houses into a city, and thus the origin of the masons is to be sought among the first godless people. All the patriarchs of the Semitic lineage <note>The descendants of Shem, son of Noah, often contrasted in early modern scholarship with the "godless" line of Cain</note> lived in roughly assembled huts made of wood and foliage, and needed no masonry houses; but clothes were indispensable to them. Even today, great lords and ladies look more toward beautiful, well-made clothes than toward palaces. 9) It would therefore have been better if the English, who are inclined toward parties and associations, had chosen the tailors' trade rather than the masons' guild

---

9) This is unfortunately more than true; only we would not, like Mr. Köhler, view it as something laudable, but rather as a consequence of the weak-mindedness of these respective lords and ladies, and thereby remind ourselves what Dr. Smollett <note>Tobias Smollett (1721–1771), a Scottish novelist and surgeon known for his travel writings</note> says on this: The Genoese is thrifty and spends his money on magnificent temples and palaces, in order to leave behind a monument of his taste and godliness for the latest posterity. The Frenchman, on the other hand, squanders it on magnificent clothes and costly meals. After death, his clothes go to the second-hand dealer, the food to the dogs, and the fellow to the devil, and he is forgotten.

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 27 regarding Johann David Köhler’s skeptical view of Freemasonry. It concludes his argument that if the English had organized around the tailors' guild instead of the masons', they would have achieved a more "noble" association, before transitioning to historical records of the Grand Masters in the 1730s.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>

...they would have raised the guild to such a noble brotherhood. For Queen Christina <note>Christina (1626–1689), Queen of Sweden, known for her unconventional behavior and patronage of the arts</note> also ennobled her court tailor, and even gave him the Gothic lion for his coat of arms. ^10) The great secrecy, he [Köhler] continues further, is the least of what I admire about the Freemasons. History does not lack examples where far more important secrets (and how does he know the value of ours? <note>This parenthetical is likely an interjection by the author of this book, defending the Masons against Köhler’s critique</note>) remained hidden for a long time among a multitude of people of various status, age, and disposition; and which, upon their eventual disclosure, overthrew entire empires and republics. As long as everyone remains convinced of the true utility of a secret entrusted to them, they will keep it silent, so as not to rob themselves of an irrecoverable advantage. Finally, he adds: "But this is the most marvelous thing about this society: that, although it does not consist of a few but numbers many thousands, it is said to contain only pious, honorable, sensible, wise, modest, loving, agreeable, and charitable persons—even though among the twelve chosen world-messengers <note>This refers to the twelve Apostles of Jesus</note> of our Savior, who is Eternal Wisdom, there was also a greedy man, a devil, and a traitor."

In 1734, John Lindsay, Earl of Crawford, became Grand Master, and

10) If this was not a satire on the nobility, then this single act proves that Christina, as much as she was superior to most of her sex, nevertheless did not cease to be a woman. <note>The author here reflects 18th-century gender prejudices, suggesting that Christina's decision was an emotional or "feminine" whim rather than a reasoned political act.</note> *You may drive out nature with a pitchfork, yet she will ever return* <note>original Latin: "Naturam expelles furca, tamen usque recurret"</note>, says Horace <note>Quintus Horatius Flaccus (65–8 BCE), a leading Roman lyric poet</note>.

<page-num>29</page-num>

1735, Lord Weymouth. <note>Thomas Thynne, 2nd Viscount Weymouth (1710–1751), served as the Grand Master of the Premier Grand Lodge of England in 1735.</note> Because the Order—owing to the internal excellence of its fundamental laws, which exert an irresistible magic even upon the heart of the most unfeeling person—had extended itself in a short time with a powerful flight across all the lands of Europe and the other parts of the world, and was spreading blessings in all places through its charitable outpourings; this rapid growth excited the suspicion and hatred of all ill-intentioned people. The thicker the mists of stupidity, prejudice, and priestly power <note>The original term *Priestergewalt* refers to the political and social influence of the clergy, which Enlightenment-era Masons often viewed as an obstacle to progress and reason.</note> that envelop a country, the more suspicious and weak the government always is; and all those who jointly undertake something for the happiness of their fellow creatures can surely count on being regarded as enemies of the country and the state; especially when, as with our society, impenetrable clouds veil the inner workings through which so many good, laudable actions—not familiar to ordinary people—are brought about.

Storms gathered from all sides, threatening downfall and ruin. Not only in Catholic states did people seek to exterminate us, but even some enlightened countries allowed themselves to be blinded for a time by general prejudice, accusing our society of destructive intentions; and the first persecution against our Order broke out precisely in a country that is famous for its freedom and gentle, tolerant government.

In Holland, various lodges had already been established from England for some time. In the Hague, there was

...a Dutch and English lodge, and in Amsterdam an English and French lodge, which performed good deeds in secret and corresponded diligently with England. In Amsterdam they were discovered first, and the common people—incited by the clergy—were so embittered that they wanted to burn down one of the lodges. Because a report was inserted into the *Amsterdam French Gazette* on November 3rd stating that a new lodge had been established in The Hague; the government became attentive, especially once it was learned that many noble persons from the court of the Prince of Orange <note>William IV, Prince of Orange (1711–1751). At this time, the Netherlands was in the "Second Stadtholderless Period," and the Prince's supporters (Orangists) were often viewed with suspicion by the ruling Republican regents.</note> were members of the society. Suspicion arose that, under the pretext of Freemasonry, an alliance for the House of Orange could easily be formed which would be detrimental to the state; as public rumor had it that most members were devoted to this party. ¹¹) To shed more light on this matter, an order was given to quietly raid a lodge while it was meeting. People were astonished by the large number of noble individuals who were found there. And because nothing substantial was found except for a few items used for lodge decoration, this had a dual effect. The imagination of the curious sought to decipher the intent and ultimate purpose of our society from these trivial tools and ornaments—which are entirely arbitrary and depend on the taste of each individual lodge—

[24]th Brotherhood of Freemasons, established in the presence of the Grand Master, Mr. Johann Cornelius Rademacher, General Treasurer to His Highness the Prince of Orange, as well as in the presence of Mr. Johann Keune as Viceregent, along with other servants and distinguished members of this society—and on this occasion many new brothers were simultaneously admitted into this Dutch lodge—they <note>The governing authorities.</note> deemed it their duty to obtain precise information regarding the content of this passage <note>original: "Periode"; referring to the specific paragraph in the newspaper report.</note>, and consequently learned that the said passage had been inserted into the aforementioned newspapers at the request of a certain Ludwig Dagran, a cloth merchant in The Hague.

to be <note>Continuing from the previous page: "...to be [admitted]..."</note>, by virtue of the first <note>Referring to the "Constitutions" or articles of the Masonic fraternity mentioned on the previous page.</note>, persons of all religions and sects may be admitted, provided they recognize the rules of morality as valid and otherwise live as honest people—with only atheists and despisers of the laws excepted; by virtue of the second, however, the fellow brethren shall indeed be truly bound to behave as quiet subjects toward the civil authorities; on the other hand, a person convicted of insurrection against the state—if he has no other crimes upon him—shall not be expelled from the brotherhood, notwithstanding that this brotherhood is bound to reject rebellion and give the government no suspicion or cause for mistrust; furthermore, the commissioned counselors <note>The government-appointed investigators.</note> have learned nothing regarding the brotherhood that would be contrary to good order and the duties of good subjects; nevertheless, they cannot leave unmentioned that in the ordinary course and according to the laws, all brotherhoods and <term>collegia</term> <note>Latin for "associations" or "societies." In Roman and Dutch law, private groups not specifically chartered by the state were often viewed as illegal.</note>, except those which the government has authorized, are illicit, and are always regarded as nurseries of factions and debauchery <note>In this context, "debauchen" refers to social disorder or excessive indulgence.</note>, for which reason one can consult **Anton. <meta>This page continues the legal narrative regarding the suppression of Freemasonry in the Netherlands during the 1730s. The previous page established that while the fraternity’s rules preached morality and loyalty, the government viewed any unauthorized private society as a potential breeding ground for political factionalism.</meta>

36

After the matter had been taken into deliberation, it was found good and willed: That the President and the Councilors of the High Court <note>The *Hofgericht* was the provincial court of Holland.</note>, as well as the Mayors and Regents of the cities—respectively including The Hague—be commanded to effectively cause the aforementioned brotherhoods, who assume the name of **Freemasons**, to cease in all places where they might have been introduced or established as a mere inadmissible innovation <note>The term "innovation" (*Neuerung*) in a legal context often implied a dangerous deviation from established religious or social order.</note>. By virtue of this present resolution, the respective officers shall be permitted to proceed against any and all who might dare to refuse to submit to the command of **Their Noble and Grand Mightinesses** <note>original: "Ihro Edlen und Großmögenden"—the formal title for the States of Holland, the governing body of the province.</note>, once such a command has been properly indicated to them. Furthermore, proceedings shall be taken against those who show themselves disobedient to the commands of the sovereign authority, and a proper report shall be rendered in due time to Their Noble and Grand Mightinesses regarding how this has been followed everywhere.

Now, because a Lodge of very upright Freemasons in Amsterdam continued to meet secretly, and the Magistrate received news of it, he initially had the entire Lodge taken into custody. The following day, the Lodge Master <note>original: *Logenmeister*.</note>, along with his two **Wardens** <note>original: *Aufsehern*. These are the two principal officers who assist the Master in governing a Masonic lodge.</note>, was led before the assembled Council, where they publicly confirmed by an oath that the Freemasons were very peaceful subjects, devoted with unchangeable loyalty to their fatherland and the sovereign; that they lived in the greatest harmony, and abhorred hypocrisy and fraud; that their various societies [had] nothing <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the Master's defense of the fraternity's private nature.</note>

...than that they had the performance of philanthropic acts <note>original: "menschenfreundlicher Handlungen"—acts of humanitarianism or charity</note> as their objective, and that the establishment of their Order was very ancient and most venerable. To make known the particular customs and secrets of their Order was indeed not possible for them; yet they assured in the most solemn manner that these were contrary to neither divine nor human laws, and that they would willingly and gladly admit one of the magistrates <note>In this context, the "Magistrat" refers to the city's governing council or high-ranking officials.</note>, who would thereafter likewise confirm this to the others. Thereupon the brothers were set at liberty again, and the City Secretary was dispatched to be admitted into the Order. The brothers fulfilled their promise, and upon his return to the Town Hall, he delivered such a favorable report that shortly thereafter the entire Magistrate had themselves initiated as Freemasons.

In this very same year, Brother Anderson <note>James Anderson (c. 1679–1739) was a Scottish minister and a key figure in the Grand Lodge of England, famous for drafting the first "Constitutions."</note>, author of the *Book of Constitutions*, <gloss>14)</gloss> made a proposal that a new edition of the same was necessary and that he already had the materials in readiness for it. He therefore received an order from the Grand Master and the Grand Lodge to submit his collection to the Grand Officers so that they could report back to the Grand Lodge. At the same time, a work under the title *Freemason’s Vademecum* <note>A "vademecum" (Latin for "go with me") is a handbook or guide intended to be carried for frequent reference.</note> was rejected by the Grand Lodge as a pirated, tasteless, and unauthorized undertaking, and the brothers were warned neither to buy it, nor [to permit] the sale of the same—

<meta>This page continues the history of the Grand Lodge of England and moves into developments in Scotland during the year 1736. It follows the authorization of James Anderson to revise the "Book of Constitutions."</meta>

...to promote. On March 3rd of this very same year, in a new assembly of the Grand Lodge, Brother Anderson <note>James Anderson (c. 1679–1739), a Presbyterian minister whose 1723 "Constitutions" established the governing rules and legendary history of modern Freemasonry.</note> was given the commission—after his papers had been reviewed and approved—to include in the new edition of the *Book of Constitutions* the patrons of ancient Masonry <note>original: "Maurerey"—an archaic term for the craft of Freemasonry.</note>, as many as could be gathered from the beginning of recorded time, as well as the old and new Grand Masters and the names of the Stewards since Grand Master Montagu.

1736. This year's Grand Master was the Earl of Loudoun <note>John Campbell, 4th Earl of Loudoun (1705–1782), was a Scottish nobleman and a prominent military officer.</note>. Under his leadership, a constitutional patent <note>A formal document authorizing the formation of a new lodge.</note> was sent to Africa. Furthermore, several brothers felt offended by the manner in which he filled his Grand Officer positions, which subsequently gave rise to much confusion. <gloss>15)</gloss>

In Scotland <gloss>16)</gloss>, on October 15th, the last hereditary Grand Master of that country, from the family of St. Clair—who was also the last of his line—resigned the office of Grand Master to the brothers and left it to their free choice to appoint another Grand Master for the country. All of the authentic lodges then active in Scotland, thirty-four in total, sent delegates to this election; out of true gratitude, they once again unanimously elected that same William St. Clair of Roslin <note>The St. Clair (Sinclair) family of Rosslyn had a long-standing traditional connection to the stonemasons of Scotland, often associated with Rosslyn Chapel.</note> as their Grand Master on the 30th of this month. He was succeeded the following year by George, Earl of Cromartie.

<gloss>16)</gloss> *Collection for the Free and Accepted Masons in Germany*, p. 15.

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the institutional changes in Scotland (1736) to the expansion of Freemasonry into France and Holland, as well as the initiation of British royalty in 1737.</meta>

In France, our brothers had already been assembling for many years, and under the Grand Mastership of the Scottish Knight Jacob Hector Macleane <note>Sir Hector Maclean (c. 1703–1750), a prominent Jacobite and the first recognized Grand Master of the Grand Lodge of France.</note>, they carried out their labors in complete silence and without being noticed. However, in this year, the Order finally became publicly known and a subject of curiosity for the public. By the end of this year, five lodges had already been established in Paris, and Charles Radcliffe, Earl of Derwentwater <note>Charles Radcliffe (1693–1746), a high-ranking Jacobite who was later executed for his role in the 1745 uprising. He served as Grand Master in France after Maclean.</note>, was elected Grand Master for the following year. Many noble persons entered the Order, and among these preeminently the Marshal of Estrées <note>Victor-Marie d'Estrées (1660–1737), a Marshal of France and diplomat.</note> <note>The text uses a superscript 17 for the footnote reference; see marginal notes.</note> and the lately deceased Prince of Condé <note>Louis Henri de Bourbon (1692–1740), a Prince of the Blood who had served as prime minister to Louis XV.</note>.

The Lieutenant of Police <note>René Hérault, mentioned on the previous page.</note>, who again received news of this through his spies, sent word to several gentlemen of the highest rank: they should cease living in defiance of the King's command, because he had the strictest orders to arrest anyone without regard for their person. 

The members appealed to the example of the English, who were not forbidden from visiting the Lodges <term>Lodge: The basic unit and meeting place of Freemasons.</term>. But to this it was replied that a subject cannot claim a right to that which the government permits a foreigner for specific reasons; and that their meetings would cease of their own accord as soon as they left Paris. Meanwhile, neither the vigilance of the police nor the King's displeasure was able to suppress the Order. All that was achieved was that the brothers arranged their meetings with greater secrecy and caution. <meta>This page continues the "apology" or formal defense of Freemasonry written in verse by the author using the pseudonym "Procopius." ...most base slander? No, it is too hard to endure this insulting thought. Allow everyone to hear my voice; allow me to teach them what a Freemason is. Our members gain respect once they are known, and I hope to inspire in everyone the desire to belong to us. What is a Mason? Recognize his image from these traits! He is a good citizen; a subject full of zeal toward his Prince <note>In this context, the "Prince" refers generally to the sovereign or King of the land.</note>; loyal to the state, and the most perfect friend. Among us, freedom reigns; but it is compatible with modesty, and we experience pleasure without offending God. Even if our joys are secret from the world, the Order nonetheless binds us to the practice of the strictest laws. A Freemason feels neither regret nor remorse. Our intent is to bring Astraea <note>Astraea was the Greek goddess of justice, innocence, and purity. Legend says she was the last of the immortals to leave the earth as it became wicked; her return marks a return to a just world.</note> back to earth and to renew the golden times of Rhea <note>Rhea was the mother of the Olympian gods; her "golden age" refers to a mythical primordial era of peace and abundance.</note> among mankind. We walk on the less-traveled path; our intention is to build, and all our buildings are either prisons for vice or temples of virtue.

Before I close, I wish to justify myself to the beauties <note>The "beauties" (die Schönen) refers to the women of the court and society.</note> who believe they are entitled to punish us because we deny them entry to our midst. It is indeed not permitted for them to cross our thresholds; however, they are wrong to be angry about it; rather, they will praise us once they have heard our reasons. We respect and treasure you, fair children! <note>An archaic and gallant reference to women.</note> But we fear you at the same time, and certainly our fear is well-founded. The first lesson we are given is this: that Adam received the apple from your hands,

All sensible people in Paris read this delightful defense <term>Schutzschrift (a formal written apology or legal defense)</term> with pleasure and were convinced in their very souls of our innocence and good intentions. However, this favorable opinion was too weak to ease our fate; the royal confessor and the King’s mistress <note>Likely referring to the influential Madame de Pompadour and the Jesuit confessors at the court of Louis XV, who were often at odds with Enlightenment societies.</note> believed the opposite, and what can the voice of an entire nation achieve against persons of such importance! The police continued to persecute us. In June, the home of an English brother was searched, and various lodge items <term>Logengeräthe (ritual tools and regalia used during Masonic ceremonies)</term> and other items of minor importance were seized. On September 14, the edict issued to homeowners and innkeepers—forbidding them from permitting Freemason meetings in their houses—was repeated once more, and the harshest punishments were threatened against violators. One innkeeper named Chapelot ²¹) nevertheless employed a clever ruse: he had the regular door to a room bricked up and a secret door cut through the floor. For six whole months, this secret meeting room remained undiscovered; finally, however, it was betrayed, and Mr. Chapelot had to pay one thousand <term>Livres (the primary currency of France before the Revolution)</term> for his ingenious idea.

Meanwhile, the clergy reported these events to Rome, and the Pope <note>Pope Clement XII, who in 1738 issued the first papal bull against Freemasonry, "In eminenti apostolatus."</note>, following the decree of the Congregation of the Holy Office <term>Congregation des heiligen Officii (The Roman Inquisition, responsible for defending the church from heresy)</term>, held a special meeting with Cardinals Ottoboni, Spinola, and Zondedari regarding the means to eradicate our society, which they believed to be so dangerous to the Church. The Father Inquisitor was immediately dispatched to Florence; judicial persecution began, and several prominent persons were taken into custody. However, the Grand Duke intervened <note>original: "schlug sich... ins Mittel"</note>, because he was most thoroughly convinced of the innocence of our society. The prisoners were set free again, a halt was put to the spiritual tyranny, and the lodges were restored. 

The main crime of which we were accused in Florence was Molinism <term>Molinismus (a theological theory regarding the relationship between grace and free will)</term> and Quietism <term>Quietismus (a form of Christian mysticism that emphasizes internal stillness over outward ritual)</term>; for it was believed that our secret resided in the tenets of these systems, which had been condemned by the Inquisition. What kind of human chameleon would one have to be if, based on their behavior, they could be considered both a free-thinker and a Moravian at the same time? And finally, how absurdly would an entire society have to behave if one found in its conduct the data from which it could be safely concluded that it acted according to these diametrically opposed principles? Certainly, this contradicts all common sense, and only the Holy Inquisition and the infallible Vicar of Christ <note>original: "Statthalter Christi," a title for the Pope.</note> on earth were capable of considering such an absurd mixture of principles to be possible.

->Clement XII, etc.<-

> "In the midst of the cares of our apostolic office, and under our constant vigilance to eradicate heresy <note>original: "Ketzerey"</note> and to preserve the vineyard of the Lord in its complete purity, We have learned with pain and sorrow that a certain society, which gives itself out to be a brotherhood of Freemasons, having taken root in various European states, has also spread throughout Italy and even grown considerably. Since We have now noted that the inscrutable secret of this mysterious society is the essential part of its purpose, and, as it were, its very pillar; that various secular powers, to whom they have therefore rightly become suspicious, have banished them from their states; and that the same must be suspicious for even more important reasons to the spiritual power, whose duty it is to watch unceasingly over everything that may concern the salvation of souls;

When the government of Florence received this bull, it was immediately sent to the Grand Duke in Vienna <note>Francis Stephen, the Grand Duke of Tuscany, resided in Vienna as the husband of Maria Theresa of Austria. He was himself rumored to be a Freemason, which explains the lenient response.</note> to obtain instructions on how to proceed. These were issued as follows: while they should accept the bull so as not to directly offend the Papal See <note>original: "päpstlichen Stuhl"</note>, they were not to put it into practice; the government was to completely ignore the Freemasonic lodge as long as it remained calm and quiet.

Since the previous suppression in France had only served to gain the Order more and more followers, a different method was now devised. Better results were expected from this new approach, as it appealed greatly to the French national character. Until now, the matter had been handled seriously; henceforth, an attempt was made to make it look ridiculous. A detailed report was published in the newspapers, which was said to contain the entire secret of the Freemasons, and this discovery was attributed to love. The story is told thus: a famous opera singer <term>Operistin (A prominent female singer in the opera; in the 18th century, these women were often influential social figures)</term> named Carton <note>Mademoiselle Carton was a well-known dancer and singer at the Paris Opera; stories of her using her charms to "unmask" Freemasons were popular satirical fodder at the time.</note> hit upon the idea of discovering the secret of the Freemasons, whatever the cost. [...sing]er was, fell in love with her. Through the most refined artifices of a skilled Phryne <note>original: "Phryne"; a reference to a famous courtesan of ancient Greece, used here to describe a woman who uses her charms for a specific goal.</note>, she first fanned his passion into the most violent flame; and when she was fully convinced that it was no longer in his power to turn back, she suddenly adopted a cold and serious expression. She assured her lover that he would never achieve his ultimate goal unless he first revealed to her the secret of his society. This unexpected demand left the impatient lover completely distraught: for some time, his duty struggled against his love; but what can withstand this almighty deity? He gave his beloved a manuscript in which the entire secret of the Freemasons was supposedly contained, and she did not delay in delivering it to the Lieutenant of Police <note>The "Lieutenant général de police" in Paris, who was responsible for public order and censorship.</note>, in order to make it publicly known as soon as possible. She did this without further concerning herself with the fate of her betrayed lover, whom she exposed by this discovery to the severest vengeance of his enraged brothers. Who could now doubt the truth of a matter that was told with such plausible circumstances? All of Paris paid Mademoiselle Carton <note>Marie-Antoinette "Manon" Carton, a well-known dancer at the Paris Opera who was frequently associated with such scandals in 18th-century gossip.</note> compliments for possessing enough wit to discover such a deeply hidden secret, which had not even been possible for the Argus-eyed police <note>A reference to Argus Panoptes from Greek mythology, a giant with a hundred eyes, signifying constant surveillance.</note>; and the public amused itself at the confusion and shame into which the Freemasons must necessarily fall after this discovery. The "important discovery" itself consisted of nothing more and nothing less than the ridiculous ceremonies and simple-minded trade customs contained in the work by Prichard <note>Samuel Prichard, author of *Masonry Dissected* (1730), one of the earliest and most famous "exposures" of Masonic rituals. It was widely read but its accuracy was heavily debated.</note> cited in the year 1732, which had merely been modified and embellished here and there. After the first rush of novelty had passed, it was declared to be what it truly was: namely, a fairy tale invented with the intent to make the <term>Freemasons</term> <note>A fraternal organization that rose to prominence in the 18th century, often surrounded by secrecy and public suspicion.</note> look ridiculous. Reasonable people could not be persuaded that composed, serious men could occupy themselves with such antics and tasteless ceremonies as those of which Mademoiselle Carton accused them. Furthermore, it was found highly improbable that the Freemasons would [trust] a person who [had] so little...

In the meantime, this story gave rise to one of the best defenses <note>original: "Schutzschriften." In the 18th century, an "apology" or "Schutzschrift" was a formal written defense of a group's principles, not an admission of guilt.</note> ever written for the Order, which was published under the following title: *Historical and Apologetic Account of the Society of Freemasons, by J. G. D. M. F. M. at Dublin, by Patrick Odonoko, 1738. 8.* <note>original: "Relation apologique & historique de la Societé des Franc-maçons..." The Dublin imprint was often a "false imprint" used by continental publishers to bypass local censorship.</note> A German translation of this work can be found in the appendix of the *New Book of Constitutions* (Frankfurt 1762, p. 78); likewise in the book: *Thorough News of the Freemasons* (Frankfurt 1740, p. 89).

With wit and irony, the author demonstrates the ridiculousness and absurdity of those fairy tales which have been foisted upon the public under the name of our "secret," and he then sets before the world’s eyes the true teachings, principles, and intentions of our Society; and since a good cause can never be stated too often, I wish to present them here once more in a brief summary:

> "All things in the world comprise a single whole, and this is found entirely in all things; and that which we call 'All in All' is God, an eternal, immeasurable, most wise Being. In this 'All' we live, move, and have our being; everything is brought forth by it and returns to it again, and it is the ground and purpose of all things. Upon this firm principle <term>Grundsatz (Principle): The foundational philosophical starting point for Masonic logic.</term> the Order builds all its conclusions and judgments. The Brothers—banishing pedantic and stubborn behaviors—investigate with a cheerful countenance, with grace and modesty, and without..."
