<meta>This page continues the block quotation from the "Historical and Apologetic Account" (1738) introduced on page 51. The catchword from the previous page was "Man" (One/They), which leads into the first sentence here regarding the investigation of nature and the soul.</meta>

...[one investigates without] prejudice the first causes of things, and strengthens speculative concepts through significant and convincing experiences. By these means, they maintain their soul in a state of serenity—one which is never stifled by sensual pleasure, nor excessively elevated by external status, nor moved by adversity, nor depressed by sorrow.

They are all equal, and call themselves brothers and friends; never do quarreling, stubbornness, envy, or jealousy arise in their assemblies. In these gatherings, one finds nothing but science, scholarship, and a refined and sincere conduct, which I shall call true **Christian love** <note>The author uses "Christian love" (Charity) here in a universal sense, common in 18th-century Enlightenment thought, where the ethical core of religion was valued as a universal social bond over specific sectarian dogma.</note>, of which they provide so many heroic examples. Everything said and done there is weighed according to the **weight of the sanctuary** <note>original: "Gewicht des Heiligthums." A metaphor for absolute accuracy and divine justice, referencing the biblical "shekel of the sanctuary" (Exodus 30:13) used as a standard for holy offerings.</note> and examined with the utmost rigor. Because they do not allow themselves to be blinded by the reputation of any famous person, living or dead, they never swear by the words of a single master <note>original: "schwören sie nie auf die Worte eines einzigen Meisters." A reference to the Latin phrase "nullius in verba," the motto of the Royal Society, meaning one should not take anyone's word as final authority but rather seek proof.</note>. Truth is their only ultimate goal, and Reason is the guide that accompanies them in their investigations.

Furthermore, they are taught the manner in which one must make use of the goods of this life in order to live wisely, virtuously, and happily. Neither wealth, nor status, nor sensual desires are strong enough to divert them from their prescribed path. They run no risk of rushing toward ruin with the common masses, for they have abandoned the **broad road** <note>A biblical reference to Matthew 7:13: "For wide is the gate and broad is the road that leads to destruction, and many enter through it."</note> and pursue only the true good.

<meta>This page continues the account of the 1739 internal conflict within the Grand Lodge of England under Lord Raymond. The previous page ended by describing "irregular" gatherings of former members as a violation of the ancient laws of the Order.</meta>

...to be recognized in no way, and to proceed according to the laws against all such brothers who attended such <term>clandestine initiations: <note>original: "Winkelaufnahmen"; literally "corner-receptions," referring to irregular or unauthorized Masonic initiations performed outside of a legally constituted lodge.</note></term> in any such illegal assembly or otherwise assisted them. This incensed those brothers even more who had already incurred the censure of the Grand Lodge. Instead of returning to their duty and recognizing their error, they instead persisted in their defiance and publicly refused to obey the Grand Master or follow the regulations of the Grand Lodge. 

With contempt for the ancient, valid laws of the Order, they established an independent power. By taking advantage of the ignorance of their new associates, they falsely claimed to have the same authority as the Grand Lodge to make and promote Freemasons and to open the doors of all lodges to them. Under the feigned sanction of the old York Constitution <note>A reference to the tradition that the first Grand Lodge was held in York in 926 AD. Schismatic groups used this "Ancient" claim to challenge the authority of the "Modern" Grand Lodge in London.</note>, which had completely expired in 1717 during the restoration of the Grand Lodge in London, they usurped the right to establish new lodges. 

This illegal and disorderly conduct forced the brothers of the genuine lodges to take new measures to more reliably identify the imposters and to bar them and their faction from entry and fellowship with <term>just and perfect: A formal Masonic designation for a lodge that is properly chartered, possesses the necessary symbols, and has the required number of members.</term> lodges. To achieve this goal more efficiently, some changes and additions were made to the established ceremonial forms. The old, true <term>landmarks: <note>original: "Gränzlinien"; literally "boundary lines," referring to the fundamental, unchangeable principles and traditions of Freemasonry.</note></term> were nevertheless fully retained, and no change was permitted except those that even the most conscientious person could allow.

In response, the official Grand Lodge introduced new security measures and minor ceremonial changes to protect the integrity of their lodges.</summary>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the history of the 18th-century schism within English Freemasonry. The previous page described how irregular gatherings were declared illegal by the Grand Lodge, leading to a defiant break by certain members who claimed they were the true keepers of tradition.</meta>

...could. This, however, provided a pretext for an excuse which the <term>renegade brothers: <note>original: "abtrünnigen Brüder"; referring to the members who broke away from the premier Grand Lodge to eventually form what became known as the "Ancients."</note></term> eagerly seized. They declared themselves enemies of all innovations; they asserted that they alone were in possession of the ancient customs of the Order, and that the genuine lodges, which they labeled with the title **New Freemasons** <note>This is a historical irony: the breakaway group called themselves "Ancients" and labeled the original Grand Lodge "Moderns" or "New," accusing them of changing the ritual "landmarks."</note>, had adopted new laws and procedures contrary to the Order. This stratagem strengthened their faction. To maintain their usurped power, they took the immediate step of electing a Grand Master from among their own members. They created a new code of laws for their form of government, issued <term>warrants: <note>original: "Patente"; the official charters required to legally constitute a Masonic lodge.</note></term> for the establishment of new lodges, and imposed certain <term>dues: <note>original: "Kontributionen"; fees paid by individual lodges to their governing Grand Lodge.</note></term> upon them, from which they hoped to amass a fund sufficient to sustain their fraudulently obtained status.

They were successful for a time, and they were indeed recognized as genuine Freemasons by many true brothers. Various distinguished persons joined them under this assumption; even some truly genuine Masons were so inexperienced in the laws of the brotherhood that they recognized their authority, attended their meetings, and thereby gave a tacit sanction to their irregular <term>labors: <note>In Masonic context, "labors" refers to the ritual work and official business conducted during a meeting.</note></term>. In recent years, however, they have no longer been so fortunate. The laws of the society are becoming more widely known; the impropriety of attending their meetings and maintaining fraternal fellowship with them has been more clearly and distinctly explained; and not only have their lodges been more generally recognized as irregular, but even their best members have discovered the truth...

<summary>This page describes the strategic ruse used by schismatic Masons to claim "Ancient" status, the establishment of their own rival Grand Lodge, and their eventual loss of influence as members became better educated in Masonic law.</summary>

...[to a better] understanding <note>The word fragment "-heit" likely belongs to "Wahrheit" (truth) or "Einsicht" (insight/understanding), referring to those who realized their error in joining the schism.</note> and have withdrawn. In this manner, one can hope that in a few years all private interest in <term>Masonry: <note>original: "Maurerey"; a common 18th-century German term for the craft.</note></term> will be set aside, a brotherly union effected, and peace and concord restored. The most worthy among the brotherhood will gladly contribute everything possible to bring this union to fruition; and those whom stubborn self-will, or even more unworthy reasons, might prevent from lending a hand to this salutary purpose, will necessarily sink back into their insignificant obscurity and must bear the unpleasant consequences that their actions have justly brought upon them. **Lord Raymond** <note>Robert Raymond, 2nd Baron Raymond, served as Grand Master of the Grand Lodge of England from 1739 to 1740.</note> spared no effort during the conduct of his <term>office as Grand Master: <note>original: "Großmeisteramt."</note></term> to bring about a reconciliation; but both his efforts and those of his successor were fruitless in this regard; meanwhile, these disturbances did not prevent **Freemasonry** from spreading further and further.

In this year, a new **Order of the Mustard Seed** <note>The *Ordre du grain de sénevé* was a lay order founded by Count von Zinzendorf in 1708, characterized by its religious devotion and social equality.</note> arose from the bosom of the <term>Moravians: <note>original: "Herrenhuther"; a Pietist Protestant denomination that settled on the estate of Count von Zinzendorf.</note></term>, about which I will mention only a little, since the members were called **spiritual Freemasons**. <margin>28)</margin> The entire institution was made known in a small Latin treatise: *Account of the illustrious order, which takes its name from the mustard seed* <note>original: "Ratio illustris ordinis, cui a synape nomen est"; published in Emden, 1739.</note>, and **Dr. Vogel**, Professor of Theology at Utrecht, translated it into Dutch in his *Origin and Progress of the False Mystical Theology* <note>original: "Oorsprong en Voortgang de valsche mysticke Godtgeleerheit."</note> with several annotations. <meta>This page continues the description of the Order of the Mustard Seed (Ordre du grain de sénevé), detailing its symbols and organization, before transitioning to a historical account of the Roman Catholic Church's opposition to Freemasonry in Italy.</meta>

and purpose of the Order is borrowed from the passage in Mark 4, verses 30 to 32. The members are drawn from all Christian denominations, and their main purpose is the expansion of the Kingdom of Christ throughout the entire world. The Order’s insignia is a mustard plant, along with a golden ring, with the inscription: **none of us lives to himself.** <note>original: "unser keiner lebt ihm selber"; a reference to Romans 14:7.</note> This mustard plant is located in the center of a golden cross, which is worn on a green ribbon, and bears the inscription: **what was previously nothing.** <note>original: "quod fuit ante nihil." This likely refers to the humble beginnings of the mustard seed as a metaphor for the growth of faith.</note> The brothers are distributed across all provinces and kingdoms of the world (which they call "ours" in the aforementioned writing). Annually, they hold a solemn assembly in the castle chapel at <term>Gnadenstadt: <note>Literally "City of Grace." In Moravian context, this likely refers to Herrnhut or a similar Pietist settlement.</note></term> and furthermore celebrate March 15th and April 16th as primary feast days. From all these arrangements, it is very clearly evident that this Order is intended to be an imitation of the formerly famous society of the <term>Rosicrucians: <note>original: "Rosenkreuzer"; a secret philosophical and brotherly society that emerged in early 17th-century Germany.</note></term>.

In Florence, our sworn enemies, the clergy, did not fail to employ every means for our ruin. The Holy Inquisition regarded it as an insult that its murderous claw was bound, and that it was unable to unleash its fury upon us. Through various intrigues, during the absence of the Grand Duke, it finally obtained a surreptitious order to proceed legally against the Freemasons according to the contents of the papal bull. Thus, on May 19th, <term>Dr. Crudeli: <note>Tommaso Crudeli (1694–1745), a Florentine poet and scholar who was the first individual imprisoned by the Inquisition for being a Freemason.</note></term> was unexpectedly ambushed in his house, taken into custody, and [sent] to the prison

**1740.** In this year, the Earl of Kintore was Grand Master in England. Above, I have already mentioned the founding of the first lodge in Hamburg, as well as some persecutions it endured; now I wish to relate its further history. ³¹) The first lodge remained unnoticed for a time: but as several people joined who made it known, and the reputation of certain special secrets of the <term>Freemasons: <note>A fraternal organization that rose to prominence in the 18th century, often meeting with suspicion from both church and state due to its private rituals.</note></term> spread very quickly through the entire world, this society became known here as well in a manner that was not entirely favorable; for prejudice sought to blame the entire society for the vices and excesses of a few unworthy members. There were even some gatherings under the name of "Freemasons" which were not actually such; and this also gave rise to the Council’s prohibition mentioned on page 59. The true local Freemasons therefore received a <term>Provincial Grand Master: <note>A regional leader appointed to oversee Masonic activities in a specific territory—in this case, Hamburg and Lower Saxony.</note></term> of Hamburg and Lower Saxony, who was appointed by the above-mentioned Grand Master on October 30th of this year through a formal <term>patent: <note>An official document granting authority or confirming a high-ranking appointment.</note></term> from London. He then established the Grand Lodge of Hamburg and constituted the <term>Absalon Lodge: <note>Founded in 1737, Loge Absalon is one of the oldest Masonic lodges in Germany and still exists today.</note></term>. In Spain, the <term>Inquisition: <note>The Holy Office of the Catholic Church, a powerful tribunal tasked with suppressing heresy and enforcing religious orthodoxy.</note></term> now also discovered the lodge in Madrid, the founding of which was mentioned above; everyone against whom they held even the slightest suspicion was arrested, regardless of their social standing. I shall only mention that most, because they would reveal nothing, were condemned to life imprisonment, the <term>galleys: <note>A form of severe punishment where prisoners were forced to row large naval vessels as manual labor.</note></term>, and some even to the fire. ³²)

In Salzburg, our Order became entangled in a very heated theological dispute through the stupidity of the monks, a matter in which the Order was entirely innocent; this dispute, as fundamentally ridiculous as it was, almost broke out into public riots. The matter was as follows: ³³) A few young Salzburg scholars and officials of the state were accustomed to meeting in a friendly manner after their return from Italy to converse about subjects of history and antiquity. <meta>This page continues the narrative of a theological dispute in Salzburg. The previous page introduced a group of young scholars and officials who had recently returned from Italy and were meeting to discuss history and antiquity.</meta>

of the reigning Prince-Archbishop, and they formed a kind of learned society. For some time already, several members of this society had expressed in private conversations their displeasure with the excessive teachings of the monks: namely, the claim that God and Mary had divided the realm of grace and justice between them, and that without the service of Mary, no salvation was to be hoped for. To this was added the fact that, through their recommendation, the work of the famous Muratori, *On the Moderation of Talent in Religious Matters* <note>original Latin: "de ingeniorum moderatione in religionis negotio"</note>, was frequently imported and read in Salzburg.

All this embittered the monks to the extreme, and since they had heard a great deal about <term>Freemasonry: <note>A fraternal organization that became a target for religious and political authorities during the Enlightenment due to its secret rituals and perceived liberal ideals.</note></term>, they were led astray by the Italian name for our society, *Franchi Muratori* <note>Italian for "Free Masons." The monks confused the surname of the scholar Muratori with the term for "Masons."</note>, into believing that Muratori was the founder of Freemasonry and that their opponents were members of it. Everywhere they denounced these individuals as the most dangerous of people, and asserted that the Masonic society was founded solely for the destruction of the Catholic religion. They did not only hide behind the noble, pious women, throwing them into the most wretched anxiety over this encroaching heresy; but sermons were also preached against it from every pulpit, and the "heresy" of Freemasonry was condemned with the utmost zeal. They did not stop there, but also spread rumors throughout all of Catholic Germany, and even as far as Rome through letters, that religion was at an end in Salzburg; since not only most of the ministers and <term>nephews: <note>Referring to the practice of nepotism, where the Prince-Archbishop's relatives were appointed to influential positions.</note></term> of the Prince-Archbishop, but even the <term>Consistorial Councilors: <note>High-ranking administrative and legal advisors who sat on the governing council of the Archbishopric.</note></term>

<summary>This page details a theological conflict in Salzburg where local monks, fueled by religious zeal, mistakenly identified the followers of the scholar Muratori as Freemasons due to a linguistic misunderstanding. This led to a widespread campaign of paranoia, pulpit condemnations, and reports of heresy sent to Rome.</summary>

...and that the Archbishop himself was a devotee of the sect of the Freemasons, and that things had already reached such a point that the tutor of the princely pages had forbidden them from praying the *Hail Holy Queen* <note>original Latin: "Salve Regina," a traditional Catholic hymn to the Virgin Mary.</note>.

The common people were extremely embittered by this outcry from the monks and the wailing of the women (for even the maids in the streets were howling about the imminent downfall of religion). When one of the suspected <term>consistorials <note>Members of a consistory, an administrative council that assists a bishop in church government.</note></term> walked out of the cathedral with the Most Holy Sacrament on the Feast of the Assumption of Mary, the people did not follow him but instead abandoned him and shouted: "His Mass is worthless; he is a Freemason!" The Archbishop sought to curb the evil and had some of the monks' inflammatory sermons confiscated; however, this only made the situation worse. At night, seditious <term>pasquils <note>Anonymous lampoons or satires, often posted in public places to criticize authorities.</note></term> were put up, in which the death of several alleged Freemasons was demanded, and the people and the university were called upon to avenge the insulted honor of Mary.

With great effort, the heated tempers were finally calmed, and the government had to use the utmost prudence to ensure that this monkish dispute did not end in a violent bloodbath. Muratori finally learned from a traveling German about the strange error to which his name had given rise. To save his honor, he wrote a bitter letter to the Rector of the Salzburg Academy, Georg Horner. Since the rest of this dispute does not interest us in the least, I will include only the passage from this letter...

> “I was most amazed when I heard that pious and learned men had conspired against me and, to support their slanders, devised the most ridiculous accusations and fables. <meta>This page continues the text of a defense written by Swiss Freemasons, originally published in "The Brachmann." It follows the previous page's introduction where the author addresses "philosophers" or enlightened thinkers, asking them to use their reason to judge the society fairly.</meta>

<page-num>72</page-num>

...the things of this world, judge all matters soundly, and allow justice to be done to every person. Allow us, then, to speak with you a little about the prejudices that are harbored against us in the world, and particularly in Switzerland.

I ask you, gentlemen: reveal to me the cause of why people in so many corners of the earth slander the <term>Freemasons</term> <note>The term "Freemason" (Freymäurer) refers to members of a fraternal organization that evolved from medieval stonemasons' guilds, emphasizing moral improvement, secrecy, and brotherly love.</note>? No mortal ever had cause to complain about a member of our society; no one can step forward and say: "this or that Freemason has done me harm, or treated me unjustly." No one has been able to accuse our ancient and well-established society of possessing any laws or customs by which religion, divine worship, civil laws, or public decency might be violated. And yet, people cry out every manner of evil against us with N. N. <note>The text notes: "here some names from the manuscript are presumably omitted." N.N. is a traditional placeholder for names.</note>. One cannot say that from our society, which has grown to over twenty thousand members throughout the whole world, anyone has ever been arrested and punished for a wicked or shameful deed; and yet people cry: "Exterminate these people from the face of the earth, so that they may be remembered no more!" And even if such a thing had occurred—which, however, no one will be able to prove—what evil could that prove against our society as a whole? Kings and princes, great regents, bishops, and others have been led to the <term>scaffold</term> <note>Original German: "Richtstätte." A place of public execution.</note>; were others of the same office and name therefore considered evil and profligate people? Only regarding the Freemasons is no [one]—

[no one can] prove that in all the long time the society has existed, even one person has been punished for vice. Is this not a testimony that we do not admit vicious men into our Order, and that the rules and laws of the <term>Freemasons</term> <note>Members of a fraternal organization focused on moral self-improvement and charity.</note> must be so constituted that honor, justice, and other duties of the <term>law of nature</term> <note>A system of ethics believed to be inherent in human nature and discoverable by reason.</note> are cultivated and observed?

But we pay no heed to these malicious rumors: we know all too well that people in general are inclined toward malice, envy, and anger, even regarding the most innocent of activities. If all of Athens raged against <term>Socrates</term> <note>The Athenian philosopher (c. 470–399 BCE) sentenced to death for allegedly corrupting the youth and impiety.</note>, whose teachings they did not understand, and condemned him with the most violent fury to the cup of poison, can one say because of this that he was a wicked, shameful, and vicious citizen of Athens, who was therefore rightly removed from society? If soon after the death of <term>Pythagoras</term> <note>Greek philosopher and mathematician whose followers in Magna Graecia (Southern Italy) faced political suppression and violent attacks.</note> in the southernmost part of Italy such a violent rage and persecution arose against his innocent students and followers, can one say because of this that these <term>philosophers</term> <note>original: "Weltweisen", literally "world-wise ones."</note> were people whose teachings and principles conflicted with the welfare of society, and who therefore had to be killed, persecuted, and exterminated? You will, hopefully, conclude with us that prejudice brought these good people to their deaths. We testify to you, gentlemen, that there is nothing in our laws by which God or the <term>sovereigns</term> <note>Refers to kings, princes, or governing authorities.</note> of this earth are offended. Justice, love for one's neighbor, and sincerity are the laws,

In all guesthouses and coffeehouses, indeed in all large inns, there were people who were meant to keep a watchful eye on the Freemasons. Bounties were offered for the heads of innocent people, and what was gained by it? Has a single one been discovered? No, we have remained as safe since that time as before, because we initiate nothing evil; we have our friends in the Inquisition, in the venerable <term>Society for the Propagation of the Faith</term> <note>original: "Gesellschaft der Fortpflanzung des Glaubens" — referring to the *Congregatio de Propaganda Fide*, the Vatican department responsible for missionary work and spreading Catholicism.</note>, in the religious orders, and especially among those whom the world knows to possess more learning and an enlightened spirit than others. We are seen in the confessionals, in the pulpits, before the altar, and in other places. We are friends of the government and the laws, and from this one can conclude that our society has no laws that run contrary to the ordinances and the religion of the land; and we hope the new <term>Head of the Church</term> <note>The Pope; specifically likely referring to Pope Benedict XIV, who succeeded Clement XII in 1740.</note> will allow himself to be influenced against us neither by anger, nor much less by prejudice.

It is further objected that there must certainly be a sufficient reason why we are not tolerated in France. Who denies this? We also have no complaint to make regarding the <term>King's decree</term> <note>likely referring to the 1737 ban on Masonic assemblies by King Louis XV.</note>, and one can claim with certainty that the curiosity of the French, more than anything else, was the cause for a law being issued against us.

Another objection is also made against us: why do the Freemasons keep their law hidden if there is nothing evil behind it? This objection is very simple-minded. Can one with any justice conclude that, because a society does not wish to blabber its orders and laws to the whole foolish world, there are things contained therein which conflict with the peace of human society, the state, and religion? We can leave the judgment of that to all wise and rational men.

But perhaps, gentlemen, you yourselves are eager to know what to make of the Freemasons? We wish to tell it to you and the whole world, quietly and in confidence. A Freemason is a *human being*, who submits himself to the laws of the land in every place where he lives. We share a close friendship with one another, without the confessions of religion separating us: for just as a husband and wife of different faiths can live well and peacefully together, so too can the diversity of religion have no dangerous influence among us. In Constantinople, we let the Mufti <note>A Muslim legal expert and religious leader.</note> profess and spread the doctrines of Muhammad unhindered. In Rome, one can ring all the bells, cover all the altars with tapestries, hold processions, carry around the bones of the saints, and the like; none of this disturbs a Freemason’s peace and contentment; he does not view this as a matter against which he must contend. Even though in various places people have no better concept of us than that of a sorcerer <note>original: *Hexenmeister*; literally a "master of witches" or warlock.</note>, we are nevertheless without danger. This is what we ask to be made known to the world concerning the Freemasons.

<page-num>79</page-num>

The conclusion of this apology shows more than sufficiently in what light the Freemasons must have been viewed in various Swiss localities. The authors of *The Brachmans* <note>original: *Brachmanns*; likely referring to *Der Brachmann*, a moralizing weekly periodical published in the mid-18th century.</note> themselves say: they hesitated for a long time before incorporating this essay into their pages, and only upon repeated requests did they finally decide to make it public. The author treated his subject with much warmth, sincerity, and zeal for the good cause, even if certain parts were occasionally placed in too brilliant a light. Despite all this, this defense was not sufficient to scatter the mists of prejudice, or to bring those who were biased against us to better thoughts. Here too, as I shall mention in the sequel, our Order had to endure persecution and suppression, and be proven through suffering.

In Holland <note>The historical term for the Netherlands.</note>, the government was indeed convinced of our good intentions; however, the clergy nevertheless persecuted us to the extreme and were firmly determined to eradicate this most charitable institution; and it was certainly not their fault that all Dutch lodges were not torn down. They attacked our society not only from the pulpits, but in their blind zeal they even usurped the right to exclude all our members from the communion of the church. Out of respect for a venerable profession <note>The author refers here to the clergy, maintaining a polite tone despite their hostility.</note>, I shall pass over these violent controversies—which certainly do not redound to their honor—in silence, and content myself with the following anecdote:

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the clergy’s opposition to Freemasonry in the Netherlands, specifically following an anecdote about the refusal of church certificates.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>

anecdote, ⁴⁰) which is more than sufficient to demonstrate how far the clergy allowed themselves to be carried away by their blind zeal.

Two young officers of very good family, both Freemasons, asked the preacher of their parish to examine them in their doctrines of faith according to the Heidelberg Catechism <note>The *Heidelberg Catechismus* is a Protestant statement of faith used by the Reformed Church to ensure congregants understood Christian doctrine before taking communion.</note>, and then to grant them a certificate stating that they might be admitted to the Lord’s Table <note>The "Lord's Table" refers to participation in Holy Communion or the Eucharist.</note>. After the examination was over, and the preacher was satisfied with their confession of faith, he asked them if they were Freemasons? When they answered in the affirmative, he refused them the requested certificate. In the same way, another person was also rejected, simply because he would not promise the preacher never to become a Freemason. This caused a great sensation, and a multitude of writings appeared for and against the Order, which I shall pass over in silence for the reasons already mentioned. Finally, the assembled States-General <note>The *Generalstaaten* was the governing body of the Dutch Republic.</note> themselves took up the matter and commanded: that in the future, no clergyman, in his official duties, should ask his penitents such questions concerning Freemasonry. And that very same clergyman who had examined the two young officers was ordered to issue them the requested certificates immediately. This ruling, granted in favor of Freemasonry, contributed immensely to the expansion of the good cause in Holland. In

...give an account of it. The entire work consists of a brief preface, and the remainder is composed of questions and answers. In the former, the author traces the origin of Masonry and its secrets back to the building of the Tower of Babel. It was brought to Egypt; Hiram, a Grand Master of the Masons and builder of Solomon’s Temple, brought it to Jerusalem. One of the most excellent and learned Masons, <note>original: "Mannon Gräcus"; likely a reference to the legendary figure Naymus Graecus found in the "Old Charges" of Masonry.</note> Mannon the Greek, instructed <note>original: "Carolus Martellus"</note> Charles Martel in the art, and the latter brought it to Europe. Under King Athelstan <note>original: "Athelstone"</note>, it came to England, and annual meetings were held in York. The initiation took place in the following manner: one of the elders held the book, and the initiate placed his hands upon it while the laws were being read aloud. The first quarterly lodges were established in 1691, and shortly before this time, the expression "free and accepted Masons" also came into use. From the accepted Masons arose the actual Masons, and from both came the Gormogons <note>original: "Gomogons," a satirical rival society to the Freemasons in the 1720s that claimed ancient Chinese origins.</note>, whose Grand Master, Volgi, traces his origin to China. The freest and most public society is that of the Grand Khaibar <note>original: "Groß-Kaihebar," another rival society to the Grand Lodge of England that mocked the Freemasons' claims of antiquity.</note>, which consists of the most select and learned men.

The catechisms <note>original: "Fragstücke," literally "question pieces," referring to the ritualized Q&A used to teach Masonic degrees.</note> are based for the most part on geometry and architecture; here and there, traits shine through which refer to various customs and practices of antiquity; likewise to the Pythagorean philosophy and the Cabala <term>Cabala: a mystical Jewish tradition often integrated into esoteric Western thought and high-degree Masonry.</term> of the Hebrews. On the island of Malta, the Order had also taken root; however, the brethren could only carry out their work under the veil of the deepest night. Six knights were discovered to be Freemasons; at the prompting of the Inquisition, they were immediately banished from the island by the Grand Master, with the express command never to set foot on this land again. <margin>42) *Secrets of the Freemasons*, by Z. 1768. page 19.</margin> Several writings concerning Masonry also came to light, namely:

1) An unaltered reprint of the French translation of the **Book of Constitutions** in small octavo <note>original: "klein 8."; a standard book size format where the paper sheet is folded three times.</note>. give a report thereof. The entire work consists of a brief preface, and the remainder consists of questions and answers. In the former, the author traces the origin of Masonry and its secrets back to the building of the Tower of Babel. It was brought to Egypt; Hiram, a Grand Master of the Masons and architect of the Solomonic Temple, brought it to Jerusalem. One of the most excellent and learned Masons, Mannon the Greek <note>original: "Mannon Gräcus"; a legendary figure in Masonic mythology sometimes identified with Naymus Graecus.</note>, instructed Charles Martel <note>original: "Carolus Martellus"; the Frankish statesman and ruler.</note> in the craft, and the latter brought it to Europe. Under King Athelstan <note>original: "Athelstone"; the 10th-century King of the Anglo-Saxons.</note>, it came to England, and annual assemblies were held in York. The initiation took place in the following manner: one of the elders held the book, and the initiate placed his hands upon it while the laws were read aloud. The first quarterly lodges were established in 1691, and shortly before this time, the phrase "Free and Accepted Masons" also appeared. From the Accepted Masons arose the operative <note>original: "würklichen"; literally "actual" or "working" Masons, distinguished here from the "Accepted" or speculative members.</note> Masons, and from both the Gormogons <note>original: "Gomogons"; a reference to the "Ancient Noble Order of the Gormogons," a 18th-century society that parodied Freemasonry.</note>, whose Grand Master Volgi traces his origin to China. The freest and most public society is that of the Grand Khaibar <note>original: "Groß-Kaihebar"; the "Ode to the Grand Khaibar" was a 1726 satirical publication by a group that opposed the Freemasons.</note>, which consists of the most select and learned men.

The catechisms <note>original: "Fragstücke"; literally "question-pieces."</note> are based mostly on geometry and architecture; here and there, traces shine through which have a connection to various customs and practices of antiquity, as well as to Pythagorean philosophy and the Kabbalah <note>original: "Cabala"</note> of the Hebrews. The refutation bears the following title: **Defense of Freemasonry, prompted by a short work: Masonry Dissected.** From a letter attached to this work—written by a Freemason under the name of Brother Euclid to Brother Anderson <note>James Anderson (c. 1679–1739), the Scottish minister best known for authoring 'The Constitutions of the Free-Masons' in 1723.</note>, in which various satires and accusations are answered that appeared at the time in newspapers and other public journals against our society—one can see that the author of this defense is not a Freemason; thus, his testimony is all the more above suspicion. He proves, by citing the most rigorous casuists <note>original: "Casuisten"; these were specialists in moral theology who resolved "cases of conscience" by applying general principles to specific instances.</note>, that Freemasons can administer an oath to their new initiates without infringing upon religion or civil authority in any way, and that this oath is indeed binding before God and man. This is a matter that has been denied to us nearly always and in all countries, and for which we have been charged as if it were something criminal. In the following pages, I will have another opportunity to touch upon this point. Furthermore, he shows that even if everything making up the basis of these questions were true—setting aside minor details—it would not be in the least detrimental to Freemasons to occupy themselves with matters of this kind. For besides what I have already mentioned regarding the scientific <term>scientific part</term> <note>In 18th-century Masonic contexts, "scientific" often referred to the "liberal arts and sciences," particularly geometry and architecture.</note>, which glimmers through here and there, everything else is based on these moral principles: Seek to conquer your passions, strive

4) **Letter from a Mason to My Lord Robert Truell, a member of the Society of Babblers** <note>original: "Gesellschaft der Plauderer"; likely a reference to a satirical or social club, common in 18th-century London coffee-house culture.</note>, **concerning the exclusion of the fair sex from the Order of Freemasons, translated from the English, octavo, 1741.**

Since this question will be raised very frequently in the following sections, I will say only a little here to answer it, so as to pass over it with silence forever afterward. It is almost not worth the effort to respond to it, and our opponents have raised this objection more with the intention of harassing us and putting forward something amusing, rather than because they considered it a matter of significance themselves.

In the **Book of Constitutions** <gloss>43)</gloss> the important reason is cited: because a woman <note>original: "Frauenzimmer"; a term for a woman that, in this legal context, refers to her social and domestic status.</note> is never free, but stands either under the authority of her parents, relatives, or her husband, she can never provide assurance regarding her **<term>freedom</term>** <note>original: "Freyheit"; in 18th-century masonry, "freedom" was a technical requirement meaning one was "free-born" and legally their own master, not subject to the will of another.</note>, and thus cannot become a member of our society. This has indeed been answered; but the strength of this reason itself has not been weakened, because truth cannot simultaneously be a lie.

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 88 regarding the exclusion of women from Freemasonry. It transitions from legalistic arguments about a woman's "freedom" (legal status) to historical and theological justifications based on ancient traditions.</meta>

<page-num>89</page-num>
<sig>F 5</sig>

In another writing by one of our members, it is said regarding this point: <gloss>44)</gloss> true **Masonry** has nothing in common with women, and the true reason for this is found in the **Second Epistle to Timothy, Chapter II, Verse 4**: *“No soldier in service entangles himself in the affairs of everyday life, so that he may please the one who enlisted him.”* <note>The author uses this biblical verse to suggest that just as a soldier must remain focused on his duty without distraction, a Mason must remain focused on the "work" of the lodge, implying women were seen as a domestic distraction or a "worldly affair."</note> I could further add that, since the origin of all our teachings and customs is lost in the most remote antiquity, the exclusion of the fair sex—for which our ancestors had their good reasons—has also been maintained. Among the **Egyptians**, no woman was instructed in the sacred language, <gloss>45)</gloss> the **hieroglyphs**, or the teachings of wisdom; they were never permitted to read the first ten **Hermetic books**, <note>A collection of ancient texts attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, which formed the basis of Egyptian philosophy and alchemy as understood in the 18th century.</note> and indeed they could not, because these were written in hieroglyphic characters. They were, therefore, also unfit for government, because kings were chosen from the **priesthood**, which was experienced in the very sciences mentioned above. In **Greece**, it is true that women were admitted to the **Lesser Mysteries**, in which the knowledge of one true God was taught; however, they could never be admitted to the **Inner Mysteries**. In **China**, even to this day, women are excluded from government by virtue of a fundamental law, because they cannot perform the **Great Sacrifice**, which the Emperor, in his role as **Sacrificial Priest**, must perform four times a year.

<meta>This page continues the defense of the exclusion of women from Freemasonry, shifting from historical precedents to a comparison with monastic orders. It then transitions into a historical chronicle of the Grand Masters of England, specifically Lord Ward's tenure starting in 1742.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>

Since we, moreover, never intend to found a particular <term>sect</term> <note>In the 18th century, a "sect" often referred to a breakaway religious group; the author is clarifying that Masonry is not a new religion seeking converts.</note>, and are therefore little concerned with a large and rapid increase in members—as our ultimate purpose concerns more **scientific subjects** <note>original: "wissenschaftliche Gegenstände." Here, "scientific" refers to the broader Enlightenment-era definition of "Wissenschaft," meaning any systematic study or branch of knowledge, including philosophy and architecture.</note>—it is equally ridiculous that people treat our exclusion of the **female sex** as a crime. Could one blame the **Order of Saint Benedict**, or that of any other saint, because its members do not live in a community with women in their monasteries?

Our current author has viewed this accusation from a ridiculous side; and since writings of this kind please only because of the manner of their delivery, and necessarily lose all their appeal in a dry summary, I refer anyone who desires to read his reasons to the writing itself; yet one will lose very little if one leaves them entirely unread.

**1742.** In this year, **Lord Ward** was <term>Grand Master</term> <note>The highest-ranking officer of a Grand Lodge.</note>, and he held his office until 1745. He understood the nature and the laws of the **Fraternity** from the ground up. <gloss>46)</gloss> There was no office in **Masonry** that he had not held. From the position of a **Secretary** in a regular **Lodge**, he had gradually risen to the position of a Grand Master. Under such an experienced director, the privileges and laws—

**Germany** was now, after **England**, the only country where the Order could raise its head and show itself in its full glory. Wise princes not only granted it their protection, but many entered into its bosom and thereafter promoted with all their might the expansion of an institution which they knew from their own conviction contributed so much to **human happiness**.

Another German prince, Duke Ernest Augustus of Saxe-Weimar <note>Ernest Augustus I (1688–1748) was a ruler known for his interest in both the military and the building of grand estates like Belvedere Castle.</note>, although he was not a member of our society, nevertheless did not fail to recognize our noble intentions. What intentions this highly esteemed society actually has is still known to no one outside of them, and the general secrecy of the brothers on this point has been admired more than once. For our part, we believe that in our critical times, God perhaps intends to accomplish something extraordinary through this society—which, however, must remain a deep mystery in an astonishing way for the time being, into which we cannot look until God and time reveal it. Nevertheless, we hope that this highly renowned brotherhood may indeed have God's glory and a beneficial purpose as its end.”

The following writings concerning Freemasonry appeared in this year:

1) *The Deliberate Freemason* <note>original: *Der bedächtliche Freymaurer*</note>; a weekly publication, octavo <note>the '8' indicates the book format 'octavo', where a sheet is folded into eight leaves</note>, Hamburg, 1742. It is not much better than the one mentioned above on page 58.

2) *The Secret of the Freemasons Revealed in a Play* <note>original: *Das Geheimniß der Freymäurer in einem Schauspiel eröfnet*</note>, octavo, Frankfurt, 1742. This is a tasteless thing in itself, which is furthermore almost unreadable due to the extremely wretched translation.

A young, wealthy widow, Lucilia, is curious to the point of absurdity to know the secret of the Freemasons. She promises a flighty young man that she will marry him if he allows himself to be initiated and then reveals the secret to her: however, he is rejected, and his servant—who is essentially just as bad as his master—is initiated instead. The servant resists not only all the threats, pleas, and promises of his master, but also the temptations of the chambermaid, whom he nevertheless loves. The chambermaid then disguises herself as a man, but is discovered and rejected by her lover, the servant. This steadfastness moves the widow;

The author confesses that he did not allow himself to be deterred by the malice and slander of the common rabble from joining a society which he nevertheless holds in high esteem, and that after his entry, he found his good opinion was not mistaken. He had promised his friend to give him a faithful account of this society as he found it, and this is the purpose of the present letter. He demonstrates that the preservation of the secret—which rouses nearly the entire public against us—is not exactly the main duty of the society, even though an honorable man would never reveal it; rather, the secret consists in the order by which the society is governed, which is the true seal of wisdom. The practice of all duties prescribed by the law of nature and the teachings of Christianity are the primary object of the Masonic— <note>The word "maureri-" is a catchword for the next page, likely completing the word "Masonic duties" or "Masonry."</note>

4) *Apology for the Order of Freemasons, by Mr. N. Member of the Order etc. at The Hague, 1742.* <note>original French: *Apologie pour l'Ordre des Francs-maçons, par Mr. N. Membre de l'Ordre &c. à la Haye, 1742*</note>

In the preface, the German translator says that he undertook this translation primarily because the newly established lodges in Lisbon, Vienna, and Brussels had recently been disturbed by the inquisitiveness, curiosity, and vengefulness of women; therefore, he seeks to make this writing more widely available to the public in order to, if possible, convince the opponents of the Order of their injustice.

The author demonstrates in the most enlightening manner—and so clearly that one can essentially raise no objection against it—that the Order intends nothing against religion in general, much less is it its intention to [undermine] the one...

to elevate one Christian religious faction <note>original: "Religionsparthey"; in the 18th century, this term referred to different denominations or sects</note> by suppressing others: That the Order cannot be suspicious to the governing authorities <note>original: "Obrigkeit"</note> in any way, neither in the respect that it itself might be capable of undertaking anything against the peace and security of the state, nor that restless minds could gather undisturbed under the pretext of Freemason societies <note>original: "Freymäurergesellschaften"</note> and hatch evil. 

He shows how ridiculous the claim of some outsiders is—as if our entire secret <note>original: "Geheimniß"</note> consisted of ridiculous ceremonies <note>original: "Ceremonien"</note> and rituals, and was only kept secret because sensible men would be ashamed to openly confess their own foolishness. Nothing can be proven from the bad behavior of some members—even those who have spoken of the Order <note>original: "Orden"</note> itself in the least favorable terms—for in the entire world, the Christian Church <note>original: "christliche Kirche"</note> not excepted, there is not a single society to be found that could not also be subject to this same reproach.

The author then poses the following question to our opponents: How is it possible that the secret has been preserved, even if it were truly pledged under oath <note>original: "Eid"</note>, when there are so many examples of perjured scoundrels who are deterred by neither eternal nor temporal punishments (which we, moreover, cannot carry out) from blabbing what has been entrusted to them? Furthermore, if we—as others say—have nothing as our object, which consists merely of signs and a secret language by which members recognize one another, and which are not worth the trouble of binding someone to by oath—

...to recognize one another, why have these seemingly trivial things not been betrayed long ago? Since this has not happened until now—regardless of whether an oath <note>original: "Eid"</note> was taken or not—because the Order could no longer exist without the secret, which is its soul; others have suspected that this stubborn secrecy <note>original: "Verschwiegenheit"</note> was caused merely by the fear of being secretly murdered. One often finds people who have perished in forests and rivers, and those murdered at night in cities: could there not be many among these who were sacrificed for the sake of general security by their embittered brothers because of betrayal <note>original: "Verrätherey"</note>? Our author refutes this hateful accusation with strong reasons as well, and finally explains that the ultimate purpose of the Order consists of: a brotherly union of the adherents of all sects and religious parties <note>original: "Religionspartheyen"</note>; an effort to restore natural equality <note>original: "Gleichheit"</note> among men; reverence and submission toward the authorities <note>original: "Obrigkeit"</note> and laws; the helpful support of the brothers among one another, and charity <note>original: "Wohlthätigkeit"</note> toward all people. In short, the Order is the great bond that unites all social classes, nations, and tongues for the collective practice of social virtues and duties.

<meta>This page continues the summary of a 1742 work on Masonic history and statutes by Brother de la Tirce, picking up from the description of its various sections.</meta>

98

II.

...[In the fir]st section, the founding of the old lodges and how Masonry <note>original: "Maurerey"</note> spread across the surface of the entire globe is recounted. The author has made a particular effort to cite only those historical facts that were passed over in silence in the history written by Brother Anderson <note>James Anderson (c. 1679–1739), whose 1723 "Constitutions" established the framework for modern Freemasonry</note>. One can therefore view this work as a supplement and correction to the former. The first section itself recounts the ancient history of Masonry, its growth, and its gradual perfection. The preface to the second section tells how the Royal Art <note>original: "königliche Kunst"; a term used by Masons to refer to architecture and the craft of building</note>, after lying in darkness for many centuries, rose from its ruins and rediscovered the true measures, proportions, and first basic lines within the remains of the oldest monuments of art; it then showed itself in perfect brilliance once more during the building of the Temple in Jerusalem. In the treatise itself, the history of the art under the Romans is recounted—under the kings and consuls, as well as under the emperors up until Constantine the Great. The third section contains the duties, statutes, and ordinances of the Order unchanged, just as they are established in the Book of Constitutions <note>original: "Constitutionsbuche"</note> as a norm and rule for all brothers scattered across the entire surface of the earth. Finally, the fourth section contains the specific history of Masonry in England, and the entire work concludes with several appended Masonic hymns.

->The continuation follows in the future.<-

98

II.

...In the first section, the founding of the old lodges and how Masonry <note>original: "Maurerey"</note> spread across the surface of the entire globe is recounted. The author has made a particular effort to cite only those historical facts that were passed over in silence in the history written by Brother Anderson <note>James Anderson (c. 1679–1739), a Scottish minister whose 1723 "Constitutions" established the administrative and historical framework for modern speculative Freemasonry</note>. One can therefore view this work as a supplement and correction to the former. The first section itself recounts the ancient history of Masonry, its growth, and its gradual perfection. The preface to the second section tells how the Royal Art <note>original: "königliche Kunst"; a traditional term used by Masons to refer to architecture and the craft of building, which they viewed as the highest form of human endeavor</note>, after lying in darkness for many centuries, rose from its ruins and rediscovered the true measures, proportions, and first basic lines within the remains of the oldest monuments of art; it then showed itself in perfect brilliance once more during the building of the Temple in Jerusalem. In the treatise itself, the history of the art under the Romans is recounted—under the kings and consuls, as well as under the emperors up until Constantine the Great. The third section contains the duties, statutes, and ordinances of the Order unchanged, just as they are established in the Book of Constitutions <note>original: "Constitutionsbuche"</note> as a norm and rule for all brothers scattered across the entire surface of the earth. Finally, the fourth section contains the specific history of Masonry in England, and the entire work concludes with several appended Masonic hymns.

->The continuation follows hereafter.<-

# Notice.

The present *Essay* first appeared in 1771 in Paris, although "The Hague" was listed on the title page. <note>original: "Haag"; it was a common practice in the 18th century to list a false place of publication, such as The Hague or Amsterdam, to bypass local censorship or legal restrictions in France.</note> In 1776, it was reprinted anew in Amsterdam with several improvements, and the current translation has been prepared according to this edition.

Undisputedly, this small essay deserves an excellent place among those writings in which a clear, sufficient, and rational idea of Masonry <term>Masonry: Short for Freemasonry, a fraternal organization that became highly influential—and controversial—during the Enlightenment.</term> is given; perhaps it can contribute something toward dispelling the prejudices and poisonous slanders with which the Order <note>The "Order" refers to the society of Freemasons.</note> is still being attacked.

<margin>
* *Essay on the Mysteries and the True Object of the Brotherhood of Freemasons*, second edition, revised and corrected at Amsterdam, octavo. 40 pages. <note>original: "Essai sur les mysteres & le véritable objet de la confrérie des Francs-maçons, seconde Edition, revue & corrigée à Amsterd, 8. 40 Seiten."</note>
</margin>

<summary>This page serves as an editorial preface, identifying the source material for the translation as a 1776 Amsterdam edition of a French essay on Freemasonry. It frames the text as a rational defense of the Masonic Order against contemporary public slander.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, publishing history, The Hague, Amsterdam, Enlightenment, counter-propaganda, translation notice</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 101. The catchword "Wäre" from the previous page matches the first word of this text. The catchword at the bottom of this page is "Schrei-", likely referring to the word "Schreiben" (Writing/Letter) on the following page.</meta>

If it were ever possible to lead those princes—who still view the Order from a dangerous perspective—into its inner workings, and to present to them all the beneficent, magnanimous, noble, and philanthropic plans and designs for the betterment of the human race with the greatest clarity: they would surely become just as zealous protectors of it as so many other great and enlightened princes of Europe, who, being familiar with its innermost secrets, place themselves at our head and most actively promote all projects useful to humanity. <note>During the 18th century, many European monarchs were suspicious of secret societies like the Freemasons, fearing they were breeding grounds for revolution or subversion. The author is arguing that if these "Princes" (sovereign rulers) truly understood the Order’s charitable goals, they would support it rather than fear it.</note>

Perhaps this fortunate moment is near; it will certainly come, be it sooner or later, because it is not probable that an institution <term>institution: The original German uses "Institut," referring here to the structured organization of Freemasonry.</term> guided by sound reason—which makes men virtuous and thus happy—will remain eternally shrouded in darkness. —

### Letter to Mr. H***.

Yes, my dear sir! I am a Mason <note>original: "Maurer"; short for "Freimaurer" or Freemason.</note>, and I thank you for the confidence you have shown in desiring to know my thoughts regarding our Order—a group to which you very inappropriately apply the name of a "sect" <note>In the 18th century, the term "sect" often carried a negative connotation of a divisive, heretical, or secretive political faction. The author is defending the Order's reputation as a universal and noble institution.</note>. I am well aware of everything that the distorted fantasies of weak and suspicious minds have dreamed up regarding the most solemn and venerable society that has ever existed on Earth. And when one is familiar with the errors of their ways, what reflections must one not make on the deception of human thoughts and the judgments of the uninitiated masses <note>original: "ungeweihten Haufens"; refers to the "profane" or those outside the mysteries of the craft.</note>! I joyfully seize this opportunity to lead you out of your error by attempting to provide you with a true and rational understanding of our Order.

The Enlightenment <term>Enlightenment: "Aufklärung," the intellectual movement of the 18th century emphasizing reason, science, and individual rights over dogma.</term> of our century teaches us sufficiently that man is corrupted, and that the reason for this is to be found in the state of society. Faults in early education, the staggering disparity of wealth <note>original: "Glücksgüter," literally "goods of fortune," referring to inherited wealth and social status.</note>, and furthermore, the passions generated by power and prestige, have corrupted the original sentiments of Nature within the human heart; they have thereby made it unhappy and

<vocab>Mason, Order, Sect, Enlightenment, Society, Nature, Education</vocab>

<summary>In this letter, the author identifies himself as a Freemason and defends the Order against being labeled a "sect." He argues that human corruption is caused by social inequality and poor education rather than innate nature, framing the Order as a rational solution to these societal ills.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, Enlightenment, social critique, inequality, human nature, defense of the Order, education</keywords>

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 103. The sentence fragmented on the previous page ("Faults in education have made man...") is completed here with "pitiable." The catchword "gen" at the bottom matches the start of the word "gegenüber" (toward) expected on the following page.</meta>

made him pitiable. A certain type of metaphysics <term>Metaphysics: In this 18th-century context, the author refers to overly abstract or speculative philosophical systems that he believes distance humanity from practical truth.</term>, which was considered all the more sublime and profound the more it overflowed with sophisms <term>Sophisms: Clever but fallacious arguments used to deceive or mislead.</term> and errors, confused human understanding from yet another side. The founders of Masonry <note>original: "Maurerey"</note> therefore made it their primary goal to lead men back to their original, natural goodness, and to allow the laws of nature to germinate again within their hearts in the greatest perfection. This was also the goal of religion, and it is precisely what the civil laws of every possible form of government seek to achieve. Perhaps Freemasonry alone knew the true means to reach it.

The intent of this society is thus to cultivate man to be rational, gentle, and virtuous; in this regard, it strove to dispel his errors, to soften his customs through the innocent pleasures of life, and to stand by him and support him in his needs.

All members of this society are brothers, and neither languages, nor clothing, nor opinions, nor honors, status, or possessions make the slightest difference among them. Equality is their first law. According to this system, the entire world is viewed as a republic, as it were; every individual nation is a family, and every individual member is a son of that family. Since all individual members of this society are brothers—brothers who have resolved to act rationally and virtuously—it is their duty to love and help one another; also toward

It outlines the Order’s vision of a global brotherhood where social status and nationality are secondary to a shared commitment to virtue and equality.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, Enlightenment philosophy, natural law, equality, cosmopolitanism, social ethics, metaphysics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from page 104. The sentence fragmented on the previous page regarding the duties of a Mason—specifically the duty to love and help one another—concludes here by extending those duties to the broader public and the state.</meta>

toward other people, to behave with integrity and decency, and to be good, faithful citizens of the state. 1)

Since the Order possesses no external means of coercion, it must take its refuge solely and exclusively in the power of morality, and it must therefore strive to make its members sensitive <note>The German *empfindsam* refers to the 18th-century "Age of Sensibility," which emphasized emotional receptivity and moral refinement as a way to achieve virtue.</note> and virtuous. From this source spring all other laws, whose objects are unity, order, harmony, decency of customs, and peace of mind. To maintain these in their strength and to prevent disruptions, it was necessary to exclude the fair sex <note>A common 18th-century idiom for women.</note>, without valuing them any less because of it. 2) For this very reason, it is also expressly forbidden in the lodges

---

1) The Masons <note>original: "Maurer"</note> therefore swear in all their degrees to be faithful and obedient to their sovereign authority.

2) It is the custom to present every newly admitted brother with a pair of women’s gloves, to be given to the woman he most esteems. Indeed, for some time now, women have been initiated into the secrets of Masonry through imitation. Yet these are different from those shared with the male sex. The women's degrees are taken from the first book of Moses <note>The Book of Genesis.</note>; the symbols and images relate to the virtues of their sex, and during the initiation, the lodge represents the Garden of Eden. The purpose of this female order <note>This refers to "Adoptive Masonry" or *Mopsorden*, which allowed women to participate in quasi-masonic rituals during the Enlightenment.</note> is the teaching that woman was created by God to be the helper <note>original: "Gehülfin"</note> of man, and that this union, as a gift of His benevolent hand, should be a sanctuary of pleasure, peace, and innocence: But that curiosity on the one side, and the sweetness of seduction on the other, caused the fall of the entire human race. At the conclusion, the initiates are exhorted to repair this damage through their virtues, through which alone they can strengthen the bond of union and allow us to feel the pure and true joys of life.

<summary>This page explains that Freemasonry relies on moral persuasion rather than force to guide its members toward being good citizens. It also justifies the exclusion of women from main lodges while describing the "Adoptive" lodges created for them, which used the Garden of Eden as a symbolic framework.</summary>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 105. The previous page ended with the statement that it is expressly forbidden in Masonic lodges to engage in certain behaviors; this page begins by specifying that it is forbidden to argue about religion or politics.</meta>

to argue about theological and political subjects; indeed, one is not even permitted to speak of them, and just as little to engage in immoral or indecent conversations.

Truth, justice, prudence <note>original: "Klugheit"—in the 18th century, this often referred to the cardinal virtue of Prudence, or practical wisdom.</note>, temperance, loyalty, faith, and compassion are the virtues that a Mason <note>original: "Maurer"</note> must practice. Generally, you may judge from this that the sole ultimate purpose of this venerable society is to improve the human being and to perfect morality.

Even in the best-ordered civil societies <note>original: "bürgerlichen Gesellschaften"</note>, you will find not a single law through which individual members are supported in an appropriate manner or assisted in their needs. One sees all too often how the virtuous man and the man of merit sighs under the weight of oppression and misery. But the Order of Masons takes it as a fundamental rule that every member finds a secure and effective support not only within himself, but also in the esteem of his fellow brothers. An unfortunate man, who is oppressed by violence, slander, or hatred, is for the most part lost without rescue; but if he is a Mason, it will be very easy for him to find a fatherland, brothers, and protectors among all nations—and not infrequently, his fortune.

Humanity <note>original: "Menschlichkeit"—this carries the Enlightenment sense of "philanthropy" or "love for mankind."</note>, this beautiful and noble virtue which encompasses all others, which is the object of sound philosophy and the foundation of Christianity, is the very soul of the Masons.

Divine, peaceful friendship—this beloved passion of the wise and virtuous, in which alone

in which the true happiness of life consists, is for almost all people only a mask <note>original: "Larve"—suggesting a facade or a hollow appearance.</note>, but for the Masons it is a duty. ³) It unites them as a whole through the gentle bonds of charity <note>original: "Wohlthätigkeit"—in the Enlightenment sense of active philanthropy.</note> and decent pleasures; those happy moments are nothing but permissible recreations, where they withdraw from official business and the malice of the world to spend a few hours of the night in innocent labors and at joyful banquets with civil and cheerful friends.

One must judge the society of Masons neither by their secrets, nor by their languages, customs, and imagery. Humans are sentient beings <note>original: "empfindsame Wesen"—reflecting the 18th-century "Age of Sensibility" where emotion and empathy were highly valued.</note> who allow themselves to be guided more easily by vivid impressions of the imagination than by the cold calculations of reason. Only the rational person feels the strength of logical conclusions; if they do not simultaneously nourish the imagination, they are all too often dry and without charm. Indeed, one must not infrequently take refuge in those wholesome prejudices <note>original: "heilsamen Vorurtheilen"—here meaning beneficial social habits or inherited wisdom that guide behavior before reason kicks in.</note> which have such power over the human heart that they even take the place of laws and virtues. The ancient priests of Isis <note>The Egyptian goddess whose mysteries were a common reference point for Enlightenment-era secret societies.</note> and the Eleusinian Ceres <note>Demeter, the goddess of the harvest; the Eleusinian Mysteries were the most famous secret religious rites of ancient Greece.</note> made use of—

> ³) This must be understood with some qualification. The friendship of the Freemasons is a feeling based entirely on the noble ultimate purpose of actively and permanently practicing peace, harmony, and kindness toward one another. For to be happy is the sole goal of Masonry. It seems to me that the kind of friendship founded on intimacy and frankness, combined with unfeigned sincerity, can only be a virtue of the Masons.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ancient mystery traditions (such as those of Isis and Ceres) mentioned on page 107. The sentence fragment at the beginning of this page completes a thought about how those ancient groups used ritual and imagery to influence behavior.</meta>

...[made use] of images, figures, and superstitious ceremonies in order to correct vices and abuses.

A society that possesses no means of coercion other than morality must therefore use these symbols and mysteries with wisdom, because through them—better than by any other means—it can awaken in the hearts and minds of its members a love and inclination for their work and duty. In short, these symbols and mysteries are the most excellent nourishment for the human spirit. The fool is blinded by them; the indiscreetly curious is thrown into confusion; and only the wise man finds in them, with inner satisfaction, the expression of his principles and concepts. Even if these mysteries occasionally produce fanatical enthusiasts <note>original: "fanatische Enthusiasten"—in the 18th century, "enthusiasm" often referred to religious mania or irrational zealotry rather than simple excitement.</note>, the practiced and thinking man nonetheless enjoys the benefits of Masonry in silence.

Masonic mysteries therefore aim for the upliftment of the spirit, the preservation of harmony, and the cultivation of the heart. The allegory of the society itself is ingenious and entertaining. One sets out to build a temple, and this is the Temple of Virtue. The tools for this construction are symbols of the <term>architecture of the heart</term> <note>A central Masonic metaphor where the process of building a stone structure represents the internal labor of building a moral character.</note>. The square, the triangle, and the compass represent equity, justice, and integrity. The light is an allusion to virtue. Man—before he is admitted to work on this sublime temple, that is, before he enters the path of virtue—is an unfortunate soul wandering in darkness; if he wishes to be admitted to this temple, he must first purify himself of his vices, and therefore

<meta>This page continues the description of the Masonic initiation process and the symbolic meaning of the candidate's attire. It follows the previous page's discussion on the "Temple of Virtue" and the internal preparation required of a new member.</meta>

...must provide sufficiently strong proofs of his persistence and his good will. The gloves and the white apron, with which a newly initiated brother is clothed, represent the purity and innocence of morals; it is through these that a true Mason should distinguish himself.

The Masons recognize no other distinction than that which virtue provides. 4) Birth, rank, and worldly possessions are placed on a level <note>original: "wagerecht gesetzt"—literally "set horizontally," referring to the Masonic "level," a tool used to ensure surfaces are flat, symbolizing that all members are equal regardless of social status.</note> as soon as the first degree is conferred. — All degrees have a mystical meaning: but few Masons are capable of unveiling their allegory.

You will not find a single good Mason—that is, a rational and upright man—who will reveal to you the secrets of his society. It has always been observed that even those who were very indiscreet in all other matters nevertheless remained impenetrable in this regard. If our society is slandered because it guards its secrets most sacredly, it is just as if one were to criticize the regularity of a building’s design. Secrets of this kind are only honorable when they are kept secret under the seal of an oath; if they are spread universally, they cease to be secrets.

<meta>This page continues the defense of the Masonic order against common prejudices. The previous page concluded by arguing that secrets are honorable when kept under oath and that only the uninitiated—the "profane"—view the society with suspicion. This page moves from the defense of secrecy to a defense of the Masonic concept of equality.</meta>

When the Christian religion, in its first centuries, was practiced only under the nocturnal veil of mystery <note>The author refers to the *disciplina arcani* (discipline of the secret), a historical practice in the early Church where certain rituals and doctrines were hidden from non-believers to avoid persecution and desecration.</note>, those initiated into it were accused of a thousand kinds of shameful deeds and the most abominable crimes; and it was only this inviolable secrecy that gave rise to these horrific slanders.

The Christians were accused of the notion that only illicit and shameful things tend to wrap themselves in darkness, while good and useful things never shun the light. In short, it has always been the fate of all secret societies to be maligned and persecuted by the masses <note>original: "großen Haufen"—literally "the big pile," a common 18th-century idiom for the uneducated populace or the common rabble.</note>: but if the gift of keeping a secret is one of the most necessary virtues in civil life, will not the best school of morality undoubtedly be the one where men are taught to be silent?

People who take everything quite literally consider Freemasonry to be detrimental to the state because its system is founded on **equality** and **freedom**. They interpret this to mean a perfect equality of status, rank, and lifestyle. Indeed, it would be very useful if the nobility and the wealthy, who set the tone in social gatherings, were pleased to visit our lodges; but nonetheless, nothing is more nonsensical and chimeric than a perfect equality in the sense mentioned above. Men are never perfectly equal, neither in strength, talents, nor stature. Moreover, everyone possesses that terrible innate inclination to rule over others, and it would be impossible to maintain a complete 

<summary>The author defends Masonic secrecy by comparing it to the early Christian Church and argues that Masonic "equality" is a moral concept rather than a literal dissolution of social classes.</summary>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of the Masonic order against accusations that it promotes radical social upheaval. It specifically addresses the concepts of "equality" and "freedom," arguing that these are moral and spiritual ideals rather than calls for political anarchy or the dissolution of social ranks.</meta>

...to introduce individual equality. The equality of the Freemasons consists in the fact that they all regard one another as brothers, and practice among themselves the duties of charity <note>original: "Wohlthätigkeit" — benevolence or charitable works.</note> and compassion. Pure morality is founded upon this equality; even Christian charity is based upon it, ⁵) and every good political constitution is a moral system which relies more than one might think on subordination <term>Subordination: the 18th-century concept of social hierarchy and the orderly structure of a state where citizens fulfill different roles.</term>.

The judgment regarding our **freedom** is just as unjust. It is assumed that the Masons teach a total independence from the laws, and that they view civil subordination as a yoke that dishonors humanity. Perhaps no word has been more misused than the word **freedom**. The metaphysicians <note>Philosophers who study the fundamental nature of reality and existence.</note> are still quarreling over the various concepts that should be associated with it. It is assumed, entirely without reason, that all actions are guided by commanding reason; there are always small incidental circumstances that are the determining factor of even the most indifferent actions: and the philosophers, by pushing these principles too far, simultaneously destroyed every kind of freedom. In morality, another meaning is attached to the word freedom. From his birth, man is subject to the laws of nature; consequently, he must also obey, in social life, that which the common—

---

⁵) There shall be among you neither high nor low. <note>original: "Vornehmer noch Geringer."</note> Matthew 20 <note>A reference to Matthew 20:25-28, where Jesus instructs his disciples that leadership should be through service rather than lordly authority.</note>. The hallmark of this equality was that all Christians called one another brothers.

<summary>The author clarifies that Masonic equality is a spiritual brotherhood based on charity rather than a rejection of social rank, and argues that Masonic freedom does not mean lawlessness but rather moral self-governance within the framework of nature and society.</summary>
