--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 111, completing the sentence regarding how man, in social life, must obey that which the communal interest dictates.</meta>

communal interest commands: for everyone finds their security, their personal advantage, and their happiness in this dependence. Only he is in fact free who is rational and virtuous, or who obeys the laws and fulfills his duties: from this it follows that freedom and equality are synonymous expressions in morality. Every just and moderate form of government is also founded upon freedom, because its actual ultimate purpose is to secure for every citizen the free and peaceful use of their property <note>original: "Vermögen" — this refers to one’s assets, property, and personal faculties.</note>: and in this sense, freedom is a right which all people received from nature equally at birth, in such a way that everyone is permitted to freely make use of all rights, provided they fulfill all social duties. Every sentiment, up to and including love, is neither perfect nor real if it is not free. The system of Masonry is entirely opposed to irregularity and licentiousness <note>original: "Zügellosigkeit" — literally "bridle-less," referring to unrestrained or chaotic behavior.</note>, and it allows no other freedom than moral freedom. Only in this respect do the Lodges call themselves free, because virtue and love must embrace one another, and because the former can never be authentic if it is not practiced freely and without coercion. This is precisely the reason why the workers at the Temple of Virtue call themselves *free masons* <note>original: "liberi Muratori" (Italian).</note> in Italy. In France, the word *freedom* <note>original: "Franchise" (French) — implies not just liberty, but also sincerity and openness of heart.</note> was adopted; a term that even more precisely designates the freedom of the heart, and from this, the descriptors *Freemasonry* <note>original: "Franche-maçonnerie."</note> and *Freemason* <note>original: "Franc-maçon."</note> are derived.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the terminology of Freemasonry and transitions into a philosophical defense of the Order's character, defining it as a pursuit of wisdom rather than a political threat.</meta>

In order to form a correct idea of the Freemasons, we should consider them as a society of mystical philosophers. If you have even slightly studied the character of those who seek to refine their intellect, then you must also know that such people can in no way be harmful to the human race. It is possible that they occupy themselves with speculations <note>original: "Spekulationen" — in this context, theoretical or philosophical contemplation rather than financial guesswork.</note>, that they judge useful plans uselessly, that they argue about unknown things they do not comprehend; but as long as they are wise, they maintain social pleasure and the charms of friendship. The general character of philosophers is simplicity, moderation, kindness, and tranquility. You will often have seen that people, out of an inclination for philosophy, have renounced the most brilliant positions of honor to devote themselves to the sciences in quiet solitude.

Neither judgment nor the enlightenment of modest and calm worldly wisdom <note>original: "Weltweisheit" — an 18th-century German term for philosophy.</note> are the driving forces of human actions; it is the passions that set everything in motion. The experience of all centuries teaches that the state has nothing to fear from those who only seek to expand their knowledge. But those who are dangerous are the ones who know how to stir up the foolish and wretched passions of the common mob. Far from the masonic lodges ever being capable of producing such ruinous disorders, they are, on the contrary, very useful and fruitful schools for correcting faults and forming good citizens. The constitution of their Order aims only at the happiness of its members; but since it [has] no

Innocent and decent joys of life, the comforts of equality and friendship, harmony, prosperity, peace, and a graceful, virtuous freedom—all these are things that not only satisfy the taste of the whole world, but also provide the liveliest and most real pleasures. Among the Freemasons, these are also at the same time the secret causes through which their society sustains and expands itself.

They call upon God as the **Grand Architect of the Worlds** <note>original: "großen Baumeister der Welten" — a traditional Masonic title for God, emphasizing the orderly, mathematical creation of the universe.</note>, and this expression is noble and sublime. He who can believe that a society that teaches godlessness and has shameful customs could exist for a long time knows very little of the human heart. authorized societies, which must not be tolerated in any well-ordered state, he argues that the government must make a general exception regarding the Freemasons.

The clearest and most convincing proof that nothing occurs in a Masonic assembly which could disturb the peace of the state is surely this: that for many centuries it has spread not only through all the provinces of Europe, but also into many Asian regions and almost the entirety of America <note>In the 18th century, "America" usually referred to the European colonies in the New World.</note> inhabited by Europeans; yet, it has never incited the slightest disorder, but rather has constantly distinguished itself through works of charity and mercy. And even if they have not been tolerated in some states due to unfounded suspicion, it cannot be denied that, on the contrary, they have been protected and encouraged in many others. ⁹)

> ...not count the Freemason lodges. This order has spread across the entire earth; it has maintained itself for many centuries in the most civilized <note>original: "poliziertesten" — referring to states with highly developed systems of administration, law, and social order.</note> countries: it has never meddled in state affairs; it has always striven to do good for the state and its fellow citizens. There are so many ruling princes, so many great men, magistrates, and clergy who are members of this society that the state certainly has nothing to fear from their meetings; on the contrary, many subjects of the state, and especially the poor, can expect much that is beneficial from them.
>
> 9) In the states of the King of Prussia, in Sweden, Holland, Courland <note>A historical region in present-day Latvia.</note>, and England, the government publicly protects the Order of the Freemasons. Public processions are often seen in London and Stockholm where the Masons are dressed in their clothing, ornaments, and symbols. Throughout Germany, the society is held in the highest esteem, and in all French provinces, there are a multitude of Masonic lodges which are by no means hidden from the government.

<summary>This page argues that the global expansion and historical longevity of Freemasonry prove its peaceful nature, noting that many European monarchs and officials are members. It highlights specific countries, such as Prussia and England, where the order is publicly protected and respected.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument for the legitimacy of Freemasonry, specifically citing its acceptance by European nobility and the initiation of Frederick the Great to prove its compatibility with state authority. Note this also, my dear sir, that the defining characteristic of dangerous societies is that they hide from the eyes of honest people, and especially from the authorities. The lodges of the Masons, on the other hand, are reserved toward no one except the common populace <note>original: "Pöbel" — used here to refer to the uneducated or uninitiated masses, reflecting the era's class-based view of secret knowledge.</note>. While they close their doors to the weak, the malicious, and the vicious, they open them without distinction to men of merit and status, and especially to the virtuous. Indeed, it is one of their fundamental principles that they strive to welcome officials, civil servants, and even rulers into their midst. And in general, how many reigning princes, how many worthy clergymen, how many men distinguished by their status, by brilliant qualities, and the purity of their morals, are there not among the number of their brothers? 10)

<margin>
10) Baron von Bielfeld <note>Jakob Friedrich von Bielfeld (1717–1770) was a Prussian writer and diplomat whose "Familiar Letters" are a primary source for the history of Freemasonry in Prussia.</note> relates in his friendly letters (Vol. 1, Letter 4) that the late King of Prussia, while still Crown Prince, was initiated as a Freemason in Brunswick on the night of August 15, 1738, according to all the usual customs, along with a captain from the King’s regiment. This same writer relates in the 13th letter, dated June 20, 1740, while he was occupied with the funeral of King Frederick William: "The King has publicly declared himself a Freemason, and His Majesty held a very grand lodge a few days ago. I had made all the arrangements; His Majesty himself took the Master’s chair <note>term: "Meisterstuhl" — the ceremonial seat of the Worshipful Master who leads the lodge.</note>, and I assisted as Senior Warden <note>term: "Vorsteher" — a high-ranking officer in a Masonic lodge who assists the Master.</note>. The curiosity of the court was thereby strained to the utmost. We initiated Prince William, Margrave Karl, and the Duke of Holstein as Freemasons, and they expressed the greatest satisfaction at being granted admission to our Order."
</margin>

--- Page 148 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of Freemasonry by situating it within a long historical tradition of private, moral societies. The previous page discussed King Frederick the Great’s initiation and the society's acceptance by the nobility; here, the author connects Masonic practice to ancient groups like the Essenes and the Eleusinian Mysteries.</meta>

Peace-loving and philanthropic men have always withdrawn into small societies in order to live far from the unrest, rebellions, and adversities that devastated their homelands. Of this kind were the <term>Gymnosophists</term> <note>Ancient Indian philosophers who lived a life of asceticism and were known as "naked wise men."</note> in India, the <term>Essenes</term> <note>A Jewish sect known for communal living and strict moral discipline.</note> among the Hebrews, the <term>Pythagoreans</term> <note>Followers of the philosopher Pythagoras, who lived in a communal, secretive society in Southern Italy.</note> in Italy, and the various philosophical schools in Greece. All these societies had this in common: they kept themselves hidden and were united by the bonds of brotherhood, selflessness, strict morality, and through works of charity and compassion. Even if their principles were sometimes excessive or ridiculous, their virtues nonetheless earned the respect and admiration of other men. In general, it seems to be a natural instinct of honest people to separate themselves from their vice-ridden and corrupt contemporaries. But among all these sects, the wisest were those who also exerted themselves to the utmost to lead others back to sound reason and good ethics through symbolic and figurative works. The <term>Mysteries of Eleusinian Ceres</term> <note>Better known as the Eleusinian Mysteries, these were the most famous of the secret religious rites of ancient Greece, dedicated to the goddess Demeter (Ceres).</note> deserve, in this regard, a particular attention above all other institutions. These Mysteries, which the most remote antiquity so greatly praised and valued, <gloss>11)</gloss> and which even Plato, Cicero, and Plutarch honored with praise, had many similarities to the <term>Masonic secrets</term>.

---

It is believed that Osiris in Egypt, Zoroaster in Persia, Orpheus in Thrace, Minos in Crete, and Cadmus or, according to others, Erechtheus in Greece, founded and established them. <note>Referring to the "Mysteries" discussed on the previous page.</note> ¹²) What can be asserted with certainty about them is that they were held in the highest regard by half of the then-known world. Although we do not know exactly what these mysterious assemblies consisted of, and the ancients have left us little reliable information on this, we can nevertheless assume, based on the unanimous judgment of the most famous writers of antiquity, that these very mysteries contributed to the perfection of social life, to the refinement of the barbaric customs of the first inhabitants of Europe, and to the spread of the light of truth. ¹³) In them, one sought only to improve morals, to sweeten the evils of life, and to enlighten the spirit, which was then lost in the most ridiculous and tasteless superstition in which the entire human race was submerged. crimes, and forgave errors by imposing certain penances on the guilty and having them vow by the most holy oaths to lead a better life. They proclaimed in a symbolic manner the most important truths of the unity of God, the immortality of the soul, and the punishments and rewards of the future. ¹⁴) All these mysteries <term>Mysteries: Secret religious rites of the ancient world, such as those at Eleusis, which were open only to initiated members.</term> contained the greatness and sublimity of the religion of the ancients. Judging by what the ancients have left us regarding them, it seems they were established not only so that we might live with pleasure, but also so that we might die with a better hope.

...it was so, that all mysteries were hidden under the veil of the deepest secrecy, in order to instill in the initiates a more profound respect for them; thus, they were never celebrated except during the holy awe of the night. The most respectful silence was observed throughout; the sight was grand and beautiful, and indeed, as we know from Maximus Tyrius <gloss>15) Maximus Tyrius, *Dissertation 33*.</gloss> <note>Maximus of Tyre was a 2nd-century Greek philosopher who wrote on various religious and philosophical topics.</note>, it was of the kind that afforded a completely new and delightful pleasure. The Greeks called admission to participation in these mysteries *teletē* <note>original: τελετη</note>, which means "perfection," and the Romans called them *Initia*, because these mysteries contained the fundamental rules of a peaceful and happy life. One had to pay a certain sum for the initiation, and the candidates were subjected to a rigorous examination. They were tested through three dangerous journeys; they were purified by water, by blood, and by fire; a thousand confused and terrifying faces were made to appear before them, and they were made to hear extraordinary voices. From the most terrible darkness, they were led into a bright and pleasant place; this light was an image of the truth. The initiates had their signs and words by which they recognized one another, and the Greeks called these "symbols." They took the oath of silence and pledged to keep all mysteries inviolably secret. Anyone who was so incautious as to reveal them was regarded as a blasphemer, a traitor, and a monster; he was banished from society,

Among the Greeks and Romans, these initiations were in general use, and the Athenians even endeavored to dedicate their children to them. One may read further on this in Meursius in the work just cited. Diogenes Laertius <note>A 3rd-century biographer of Greek philosophers.</note> (in Book 7, No. 39) tells of Diogenes the Cynic, that when asked by the Athenians if he did not wish to be initiated—since the initiated held the first rank in the next life—he replied: it was ridiculous to believe that Agesilaus and Epaminondas <note>Renowned Greek generals known for their virtue and leadership.</note> should reside in a worse place, while any wretch, provided he were initiated, should dwell in the fields of the blessed. No Epicurean <note>Followers of Epicurus, who were often viewed by later societies as atheistic or purely materialistic.</note> was admitted, and following this example, the **Masons** <term>Maurer</term> <note>A common shorthand for Freemasons.</note> also deny **atheists** entry to their lodges. The mysteries were communicated only from degree to degree; each degree had its particular customs, and only after lengthy trials of character and behavior did one finally attain full **initiation**. Various hymns were sung, and almost all sources agree that neither the true meaning of the mysteries nor the symbols were clearly understood. Nevertheless, it is evident that the overall spirit of these beneficial institutions consisted of educating the human race, comforting it in times of misfortune, providing assistance through charitable works, and instructing it in the arts necessary for its survival. ²⁰)

There is good reason to suspect that a portion of the mythology of ancient peoples is a consequence of the images and allegories of these mysteries; at the very least, it is certain that they served one purpose for the common people and another for the initiated, and that a great distinction was made between the **theology of the wise** and the errors of the masses, between secret worship and public ceremonies. How many important discoveries could be made regarding the religion of the ancients if one could obtain precise knowledge of their mysteries!

*History of the Egyptian King Sethos. Part One. Translated from the French by Matthias Claudius, 1777. 1 alphabet, 6 sheets, octavo. Breslau, published by Gottlieb Löwe.*

(From the Königsberg Learned and Political Newspaper, issues 85 and 86 of the year 1777.)

The original of this translation can be viewed from a threefold perspective. First, as a political novel, which <term>Terrasson</term> <note>Jean Terrasson (1670–1750) was a French sub-dean and scholar; his novel *Sethos* (1731) was presented as a translation of an ancient Greek manuscript.</note> (for he is its author) wrote with the same intention as <term>Fénelon</term> <note>François Fénelon (1651–1715) wrote *The Adventures of Telemachus*, a famous novel intended to educate a prince in the virtues of a wise ruler.</note>; thereafter as a commentary on the manners, customs, antiquities, laws, and even the geography and history of ancient Egypt; and finally as a contribution to the history of <term>Freemasonry</term> <note>A fraternal organization that often looked to ancient Egyptian initiation rituals for its symbolic heritage.</note> in the most ancient times. The latter word does not appear in the entire book; however, without prejudice to the word, it contains the subject itself; and I hope to earn the gratitude of the readers of this paper if I share with them a short <term>précis</term> <note>A concise summary or abstract.</note> of what they can read in greater detail, certainly not without pleasure, on page 145 onwards of the book itself. — The largest of the Egyptian pyramids was actually—if I may express myself so—the dome of an immensely expansive subterranean temple. Once one had worked their way into the center of it, one found oneself at a deep, terrifying-looking well, down which one [descended] by means of hammered iron

[iron] rungs, sixty steps deep. Once a person had reached that point, they found themselves next to a window which served as the entrance to a quite comfortable path. This path, carved into the **living rock** and constantly winding, descended for 125 feet. At this point, one reached the bottom of the **well** and simultaneously the entrance to a seemingly endless corridor, from which many side-passages branched off to the right and left. Through these passages, all the pyramids, as well as the most prominent of the **Egyptian temples**, were connected to one another. They led into vast, spacious, subterranean halls that were perpetually illuminated by the glow of many thousands of torches and lamps, serving as the residence for many **priests** and priestesses. 

The **candidate** who sought **initiation** <note>In this context, initiation refers to the secret rituals required to enter the Egyptian priesthood, a popular theme in 18th-century literature and Freemasonry.</note> was forced to descend into this well in the manner described above. Once at the entrance of the long path, it was up to him whether he wished to turn back and climb out, or to continue onward. Assuming he chose to proceed—though one should know that the structure was so cleverly designed that the priests were immediately notified if a candidate had the courage to descend to the very bottom—he would find the following **inscription** on the **attic** <term>attic: in classical architecture, a decorative wall or square story placed above the main cornice of a building or arch.</term> of the archway he was to enter: 

"Whoever travels this path alone and without looking back, will be purified by **fire**, by **water**, and by **air**; and if he can overcome the dread of death, he will [emerge] from the womb..."

"of the earth again and see the light once more, and

The **candidate**, who was not deterred by these conditions, entered this **archway** <note>The original German "Schwibbogen" refers to a curved arch or flying buttress, here describing a subterranean vaulted passage.</note> and continued for a good mile without encountering anything strange or adventurous. 
However, he then found himself at a small, iron, and firmly locked gate in the wall, beside which stood three men. Their heads were covered with helmets adorned with the **head of Anubis** <note>Anubis was the ancient Egyptian god of the dead, typically depicted with the head of a jackal. In these initiation rituals, he represents a guardian of the threshold.</note>. 
These men said to him: "We do not stand here to delay you on your path; continue, if the gods have granted you the courage. But if you are unfortunate enough to return by this same way, we shall stop you on your return. You may still turn back; but one moment further, and you will never again be able to leave these places, unless you exit immediately by the path you create ahead of you, without turning your head or retreating." 
If the candidate was not dissuaded and proceeded further, he had to pass between two burning **pyres**, between which a glowing **iron grate**, thirty feet long and eight feet wide, was positioned so that one could only with great difficulty place one's foot between the bars of the grate. In this way, he had to cross over it and pass through the glowing pyres; these were so artfully constructed that from a distance they appeared as nothing other than a continuous fire, through which one

[the water through which he would have] had to swim, reached [the level where it could be] scooped; and he had, to his greatest astonishment, passed through the earth and found himself in the sanctuary of the great **Memphite Temple** <note>Memphis was the ancient capital of Lower Egypt. Its great temple was dedicated to Ptah, the creator god. In the context of 18th-century mystery literature, these temples were viewed as sites of elaborate mechanical and psychological initiations.</note>. Here he now remained for a long time in the hands of the **priests**, who now performed the purification of the soul with him, just as that which had happened until now was called the purification of the body. Once this soul-purification—linked with much fasting, prayer, sacrifices, and other religious ceremonies—was also over, the candidate was finally admitted to the **mysteries**. And what was revealed to him? On this point, my author—who remains only with the external—does not venture. I will, however, supplement him in this regard and say in what manner these mysteries came to Greece, to Italy, and to the other western lands.

People have wondered at all times how it came to be that a people as wise and clever as the Egyptians claimed to be—and truly were—adopted a religion which, at first glance, seems the simplest of all and the most repulsive. Since I have read nothing on this matter that has fully satisfied me, I undertake here to discover the true reason. The **animal worship** <term>Thierdienst: The veneration of animals as sacred beings or manifestations of deities.</term> of the Egyptians had neither physical nor political causes at its root; it was the fruit of a very enlightened and correct philosophy and was based on the principle generally recognized as true—at least generally recognized by the wise—that God has no corporeal form,

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the origins of religious symbolism and the transition from abstract monotheism to idolatry, following the previous page's introduction to Egyptian animal worship.</meta>

consequently [God] can also not be depicted under any image, nor any likeness. The wise of all nations, the Jews as well as the Heathens, possessed this correct knowledge; however, they feared that this was *strong meat* <note>original: "starke Speise." A reference to Hebrews 5:12-14, where "strong meat" or "solid food" represents advanced spiritual teachings that the "unskilful" cannot digest.</note> which the common people would not be able to digest, as they required a physical object to focus their devotion. Thus the problem arose: what might be the most suitable symbol for the worship of the **Divinity**? And here, every nation chose the works of His omnipotence; sometimes fire, sometimes water, and most commonly, the first and most excellent of His creatures: the **human being** created by Him. As soon as one desired to represent God under a symbol, one could naturally choose nothing other than a thing created by Him to serve as that symbol; and it then seemed most fitting to choose either the most magnificent of His creatures—the Sun, Moon, and all the host of heaven—or else the most rational: the human being, created in His image (that is, rational like Him), to be the external object of veneration. Since it would have been dangerous to take a living person for this symbol—who would certainly not have neglected (as indeed actually happened in **Tibet** and **Japan**) to derive a benefit from his status as a symbol that would be detrimental to the ruler of the state—it was necessary to take recourse in statues. Thus, gods were worshipped who were the work of human hands, who "had eyes and saw not, ears and heard not," etc. <note>This is a paraphrase of Psalm 115:4-6, a common biblical polemic against idolatry.</note>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on why ancient peoples chose specific physical symbols for the divine, moving from the human form (which led to anthropomorphism) to the animal worship of Egypt.</meta>

Apart from the fact that this behavior often brought bitter, though fundamentally undeserved, mockery upon the **idolaters**; it also had the additional harm of encouraging **anthropomorphism** <note>The practice of attributing human characteristics, forms, or personality traits to God.</note>: for the worshippers of idols gradually grew accustomed to imagining the Supreme Being in the form of an immensely large human. — Is it any wonder, then, if a brooding **philosopher**—who likely possessed more speculation than knowledge of human nature—hit upon the idea of worshipping God not in the most magnificent or rational of His creatures, but in the most hideous and irrational ones: in **cats** and **crocodiles**? 

This thought was, fundamentally, quite philosophically sound: everything that God has created is good, and His supreme wisdom and omnipotence can be recognized in the smallest and most contemptible of His creatures; it can be perceived in leeks and onions just as well as in the sun and the human being. The philosopher added: this wisdom can be recognized more vividly and intuitively from a living creature than from even the most artistically crafted statue; and the more irrational and senseless this living creature is, the less we have to fear finding a **Dalai Lama** <note>A reference to the previous page's warning about living humans (like the spiritual leaders in Tibet) who might exploit their status as divine symbols for political power.</note> within it. — Thus reasoned the speculative philosopher, and **Egypt** erected temples to the **crocodile**, and folded its hands in prayer for cats!

Just as the worship of God through human symbols gave birth to anthropomorphism, so did this strange symbol of Egyptian speculation—supported by various circumstances and physical utility—degenerate into one of the most repulsive <note>original: "rebütantesten," derived from the French *rebutant*, meaning disgusting or off-putting.</note> and crassest forms of **idolatry** <note>The worship of physical objects or "idols" as gods.</note> that ever infected the earth. And here, fortunately

Orpheus had the good fortune to partake in this initiation during his stay in Egypt. Since a poet usually views the things of this world through very different eyes than a common mortal <note>literally "clod of earth," implying someone mundane or unrefined.</note>, Orpheus utilized the mysterious ceremonies of initiation—which the Egyptians themselves kept so sacredly secret—quite differently than the Memphite priests. He described them in songs, and through imagery borrowed from these rites, the state of the soul after death was presented! — What new vistas open before us! Tartarus and Elysium, which knows its own sun and has its own stars; Ixion, Cerberus, the Danaids! All stand before us in light and clarity! Johann Jacob Moser, Royal Danish State Councilor, *On the Tolerance of Freemason Societies; especially with regard to the Peace of Westphalia.* <note>The 1648 Peace of Westphalia established the legal status of various religious denominations within the Holy Roman Empire.</note> 1776. 44 pages in octavo <term>8.</term> <note>A standard book size where the printed sheet is folded three times to create eight leaves.</note>

To him, the tolerance and admission of all religious parties into the society—even Jews—seem to prove this; for he regards the claim made in the *Pocketbook for Freemasons* of 1776—that only Christians, and among them only those who specifically pledge themselves to the propagation of this religion, can attain complete perfection—as nothing but an opium to lull to sleep those who are zealously devoted to the Christian Church, so that they might not hinder the progress of Masonry before the mine <note>A common 18th-century metaphor for a secret plot or an explosive charge set to undermine a foundation.</note> has been properly laid. The Order consists of a society of men, divided into various grades, who keep their constitution and actual purpose hidden from all curious eyes. Everything that has been said from time to time about <term>women's lodges<gloss>original: "Weiberlogen." While some unofficial branches of "Adoption Masonry" allowed women, regular 18th-century Freemasonry was strictly male; the author here dismisses mixed-gender groups as fraudulent.</gloss></term> belongs to the playthings of spurious lodges; true Masonry has nothing to do with them. In the 18th century, this often referred to a scholarly journal or a series of collected essays rather than a physical room of books.</gloss></term>) has brought forward in favor of these lodges to flatter the ladies, it remains nothing more than a silly dalliance, with which a genuine lodge will never concern itself, as Masonry certainly has a far more sublime ultimate purpose than the improvement of women.

Since our constitutions are unknown, everyone believes themselves entitled to judge us according to their own various dispositions. A good-natured youth, perhaps hearing of some of our magnanimous traits, considers us an elite selection of the human race; a settled man of worldly knowledge considers us a harmless society; a dark, reclusive philosopher considers us a purposeless assembly of fools and madmen; an anxious politician considers us citizens dangerous to the state; a vapid wit and fashionable philosopher (and such boys who have become clever before their time now drown out almost everything with their talkative ignorance, so that sensible people are almost ashamed to speak) for

Anyone who attempted to reason with such logic in other cases would surely draw universal laughter upon themselves.

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 150 regarding the limitations of standard medicine. This page argues that alchemical remedies are uniquely suited to address the "primary causes" of illness by interacting with the body's vital life force.</meta>

Remedies prepared in this way are intended to act upon the primary causes of the diseases themselves. These causes are woven so deeply into the physical mechanism that they not only completely elude our senses, but it is also—given the way we currently proceed with physical investigations—quite impossible to ever discover them. In our entire **materia medica** <term>materia medica: the body of collected knowledge about the therapeutic properties of substances used for healing</term>, we have not, until now, possessed a single remedy that acts quite so directly upon these primary causes.

Since it is now certain that all diseases which do not arise specifically from a structural flaw in the body's **organization** <term>organization: the biological arrangement of the body's physical parts and systems</term> are instead caused by such an unharmonious action of the **life forces**—for instance, most types of fevers and nervous diseases—one can certainly conceive, to some extent, of a kind of **universal medicine** <note>Also known as a Panacea; in alchemy, this refers to a "perfect" medicine capable of restoring balance to the entire system rather than just treating symptoms</note>. Furthermore, the original physical forces—**sensibility**, **irritability** <term>sensibility and irritability: 18th-century physiological terms; "sensibility" was the power of the nerves to feel, while "irritability" was the power of the muscles to respond</term>, vegetative, and other forces, of which usually nothing but the name and a few outward signs are mentioned in physiology—are likely an extremely delicate, fine, and independent physical essence. This has already been made very plausible by **Medicus** <note>Friedrich Casimir Medicus (1736–1808), a prominent physician and vitalist who believed a "life force" was distinct from mechanical laws</note> in his treatise on the life force and by other modern **Stahlians** <note>Followers of Georg Ernst Stahl (1659–1734), who argued that a "vital soul" governed the living body and prevented its decay</note>.

Consequently, what the **alchemists** say about the astonishing effects of a spiritual, fiery medicine—one consisting of the **primordial origins** <note>original: "Uranfängen"</note> and being of the same nature as this life force—acquires a very high degree of probability. But this is mentioned only in passing. —

...that one cannot decompose gold—which, because it consists of the most delicate and purest parts, could certainly offer the most effective remedies—into its original elements and apply it for medicinal use. Many modern chemists therefore consider gold to be a body entirely useless for medicinal use, and declare everything that the alchemists say about it to be mere dreaming. However, the reviewer can contradict this from his own experience; he himself knows of a **diuretic** <term>diuretic: a substance that encourages the body to release excess water through urination</term> remedy made from gold, which is extremely effective in cases of **dropsy** <term>dropsy: historically known as "Wassersucht," this is the painful swelling of the body caused by the accumulation of fluid, now called edema</term> and infinitely surpasses all other known diuretic remedies, and he has the reading of alchemical writings to thank for this.

From what the author has now said of this philosophy, he concludes: that since it leads through the closer contemplation of the great universe and its active forces to a more intimate and pure knowledge of the **Father of Light** <note>A poetic and Enlightenment-era reference to God as the source of all wisdom and creation</note>, a true **Mason** <note>Refers here to a Freemason</note> must not only be a true worshiper of God and religion, but also a good subject and a philanthropist. He now refutes the accusations which have been made against us, and seeks to demonstrate their groundlessness. From this, we wish only to highlight here and there our author's own thoughts, and pass over everything that many others before him have already said about this very subject. On page 63, he addresses the objection which is so often made: namely, why a good thing, if it does not need to shun the light, is conducted in the dark and not made known to the whole world? This has happened, and happens

...This still happens daily, the author replies. The writings of **Arnaldus de Villa Nova** <note>A famous 13th-century physician and alchemist</note>, **Basilius Valentinus** <note>The pseudonym of a legendary 15th-century alchemist known for his works on antimony</note>, **Paracelsus**, **Bacon** <note>Referring to Francis Bacon, the philosopher of the scientific method</note>, **Helmont** <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont, a 17th-century chemist and physician</note>, and others—who wrote under their usual names and still seek daily to enlighten the world with the most useful knowledge, but without boastfulness—can confirm this.

The **Masonic oath** <note>A reference to the "maurerische Eid"</note> is more of a vow made to the Lord to perform laudable actions pleasing to Him than a formal civil oath. Since many passages in Holy Scripture expressly command that such vows made to the Lord be kept most strictly, it can in no way be considered unlawful.

There is no reason to fear that the Order will ever create a **state within a state** <note>original: "Statum in Statu"; a common 18th-century political fear that secret societies might function as independent political bodies outside of government control</note>. This is because we acknowledge no sovereign other than the authorities of whichever land we live in, under whose protection we reside, and we have joined together for no worldly or political purpose, but merely for **scientific** <term>scientific: the original "wissenschaftlich" here refers to "Wissenschaft" in the older sense—the systematic pursuit of knowledge, including natural philosophy, alchemy, and ethics</term> ends.

The author now explains that he wrote for the most important reasons and by order of his superiors, as I have already mentioned above, and he simultaneously addresses our **constitution** <note>The internal rules and organizational structure of the Masonic Order</note>. From the very beginning of the Order, the members were divided into classes and taught according to the measure of their abilities and their more or less useful qualities. Those who were unfit were kept busy with useful secondary matters and held back from the Truth. No one can be admitted to the number of the chosen teachers and gain insight into the innermost...

...basic rules of the **sciences** <term>sciences: translated from "Wissenschaften," which in the 18th century encompassed all systematic knowledge, including natural philosophy, alchemy, and ethics</term> be admitted until he has survived the unavoidable and necessary prescribed trials. Once this has occurred, one proceeds to the initiation with greater tests, and only then to that which must precede admission to our innermost circle.

To make useful knowledge more beneficial to the public, to spread the arts and sciences, and to alleviate the misery of our fellow men is the sole purpose of all our most secret works. The author concludes from this that the State should favor us in this regard rather than persecute us, and that **Herr von Moser** <note>Karl Friedrich von Moser (1723–1798), a prominent German statesman and social critic who wrote extensively about the influence of secret societies</note> has viewed us from a entirely false perspective. We honor every worldly government and take no part in political revolutions, because we are **cosmopolitans** <note>original: "Cosmopoliten"; individuals who view themselves as citizens of the world rather than of a single nation-state</note>. We know how to cautiously avoid the superior strength of tyrannical power, but we will never offer resistance. We yield to all force, and our secret is the only thing that no power of man can wrest from us. God gives it to whom He wills, and has subjected it neither to any man’s will nor to earthly power. All our true brothers who fall into the claws of tyranny willingly surrender themselves to death and the most terrible tortures in order to keep the secret sacred; the Lord Himself grants them steadfastness, because the great work is only useful as long as it remains enclosed within a small circle, and would immediately have the most detrimental consequences as soon as it became known to the **children of Belial** <note>A biblical term (Hebrew: "worthless") used here to describe the uninitiated, the wicked, or those who would abuse the Order's spiritual and scientific knowledge</note>.

<meta>This page continues the text's exploration of esoteric knowledge, transitioning from the philosophical defense of a secret society to a review of a specific alchemical preparation—Astral Powder—and a bibliography of contemporary Freemason lodges. The attached **Astral Powder** is prepared from *Tremella Nostoch* <term>Tremella Nostoch: commonly known as "Star Jelly," this is a gelatinous cyanobacterium that appears on the ground after rain; historically, it was believed to be remnants of shooting stars and possessed magical or medicinal properties</term>, and the method of preparation can be read in the book itself. The author attributes great healing powers to this remedy: whether it achieves all it claims, the reviewer does not wish to decide, as he is not at all accustomed to rejecting anything based on mere probabilities. Who knows all the inner properties of bodies! And how often do we find that the very body which, according to common theory for researching healing powers, should be trusted with almost nothing, is in fact the most effective. Experience alone must decide here. Meanwhile, Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), the Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine</note> in various parts of his works (such as *On the Long Life* <note>original Latin: "de vita longa"</note>, *Treatise on Philosophy* <note>original Latin: "Tract. de Philosophia"</note>), Helmont <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644), a chemist and physician who bridges the gap between alchemy and modern chemistry</note>, Borelli <note>Giovanni Alfonso Borelli (1608–1679), an Italian scientist; the text cites his "Observations and Histories," Century III, Observation 41</note>, and other **chemical physicians** <note>original: "chymische Aerzte"; these were practitioners of iatrochemistry who favored mineral and chemical remedies over traditional herbal Galenic medicine</note> have already attributed great healing powers to this plant. It is also remarkable that, as the reviewer knows from his own experience, it arises almost only after a warm thunderstorm rain. The entire air is at this time impregnated with electrical matter; perhaps this plant absorbs a large portion of it, and from this, its healing power might perhaps also be explained.

# Directory
### of the Freemasonic writings published in the year 1777 by the Mother Lodge of the Three Globes.

---

### I.
->Freemasonic Toasts and Well-Wishes for the Year 1777.<-
->Berlin, published by G. J. Decker.<-

The headings are: 1) To the King. 2) To the Crown Prince. 3) To Duke Ferdinand as Grand Master of the United Lodges in Germany <note>Duke Ferdinand of Brunswick-Wolfenbüttel (1721–1792), a Prussian field marshal and a prominent leader in European Freemasonry</note>. 4) To Prince Frederick of Brunswick, as Grand Master of the United Lodges in the Prussian States. 5) To the Fathers of the Order. 6) To the Mother Lodge and its branches. 7) To the Masters of the Chair <term>Master of the Chair: the presiding officer or "Worshipful Master" of a Masonic lodge</term>. 8) To the officers. 9) To all Masons. 10) To the martyrs of the Order. 11) To those seeking knowledge. 12) To those who are erring. 13) To the profanes <term>Profane: a Masonic term used to describe those who have not been initiated into the secrets of the Order</term>. 14) To the fair sex <note>original: "An die Schönen," literally "To the beautiful ones," a traditional toast to women who were not members but were often honored at Masonic banquets</note>.

The toasts are primarily intended for use at table lodges <term>Table Lodge: a formal ritualistic banquet where toasts are drunk according to a strict ceremonial sequence</term>.

IV.

A speech by the local Grand Master B * * <note>Likely referring to a high-ranking official like Carl Friedrich von Benda, who was active in the lodge at this time</note>, full of the warmth of patriotism and a vivid portrayal of the happiness of the Prussian States under the scepter of their wise and just King, for which the motto selected from his *Miscellaneous Poems* <note>original: "Poësies diverses," a collection of poetry written by Frederick the Great himself</note> is perfectly suited:

> Such for his subjects is a tender and good Monarch,
> Humane in his counsels, humane in his projects;
> He extends for them the thread of the Fates <note>In mythology, the Parcae or Fates spin the thread of life; the King is described here as lengthening his subjects' lives through his care</note>.
> He counts all his days by as many acts of kindness.

This speech was later translated into the German language (the language in which the first draft was also composed and read), under the title: *Speech on the Birthday of the King, delivered in the Old and Just Lodge of the Three Globes*, 22 pages in octavo <term>Octavo: a book size resulting from folding a sheet of paper three times to create eight leaves</term>, published by Decker.

### III.

->*Historical Abridgment concerning the Mother and Primitive Lodge of Freemasons established in Berlin under the name of the Three Globes, translated from German. This is a translation, albeit somewhat expanded, of the "Short Historical Account" published in 1775, which was cited in the preceding speech and sent to His Majesty. His Majesty most graciously expressed his pleasure in a formal letter read aloud in the Lodge.

Apollos; the Greeks are proud of their Hector and Achilles; the Romans are proud of their Scipios, Pompeys, and Caesars. Your pride is far more justified, Germania! From patriotic and honest blood you brought forth, and from the unique strengths of the art of war you formed, your own protective deity. ->Here Ferdinand was victorious!<-

Even now, the sons of Tuisto <note>original: "Thuiskon," a mythical ancestor of the Germanic peoples; the phrase "Tuisto's sons" refers to the German people.</note> sing of his deeds here in festive choral songs and in Olympic games. Here the white-haired old man still renews for his listening grandson the terrifying memory of when Germany's enemies, armored in serpent scales, heated with desire for plunder and slaughter, approached in countless swarms like thunderclouds in a black whirlwind. It was then, says the old man, that Ferdinand wielded the bloody scourge; where the murderous lead <note>A poetic reference to musket balls and bullets.</note> rained around and upon him, and he fought against the threatening danger with the most cheerful, the most undaunted expression; where he charged into entire squadrons with the courage of a lion and, as an instrument of Providence, completed the great plan to save our fatherland. Yes, he is the savior of German liberty. Let ever-blooming laurel crown his temples;

temples: His name will one day, even after many centuries, shine in starry rays upon the monuments of immortality. His victories are inscribed in golden letters into the history of our age.

But now, my brothers! Now mix with the formidable, majestic features of a hero the gentler ones of a philanthropist. <note>original: "Menschenfreund," a key Enlightenment term for someone who loves and serves humanity.</note> — In the midst of his martial career, he did not merely strive for the giant stature of a victor and conqueror: he was magnanimous toward the vanquished; filled with noble compassion, he looked upon the corpses that the death-pregnant artillery had sown across the battlefield; he saw the mournful ruins of torched dwellings, the trampled crops, the wringing hands of the unfortunate; he heard the gasping hunger of the exhausted farmer and the whimpering of the wounded. He helped as often and as soon as he could. And this help alone was balm for those who had to endure the devastating wrath of war.

Since the Goddess of Peace has once again established her blessed, feud-free seat in our region, he calculates his days by the greater sum of charity and nobility. His Tusculanum <note>original: "Tuskulanum," a reference to the Roman statesman Cicero’s famous villa at Tusculum, used here to describe the Duke's country estate, Schloss Vechelde, as a place of intellectual retreat.</note>, whose simple yet tasteful splendor even a Roman Sulla <note>original: "Sylla," referring to the Roman dictator Lucius Cornelius Sulla, who famously resigned his power to live in rural retirement.</note> would envy, provides him with wise rest and occupation. Walking beneath the shade of the olive tree and the myrtles, he arranges plow and seed, plants and flowers, just as he previously, as a commander, commanded legions of foreign and domestic peoples. Now <note>original: "Ist," likely an archaic variant of "itzt" (now).</note> his thinking spirit feels the full dignity of man, and milder

### VIII.

### The Golden Pythagorean Sayings. 4. S. gr. 8. <note>This refers to the book format, likely "Small Great Octavo."</note>

These were distributed to the brothers on St. John's Day <note>June 24th, the feast of St. John the Baptist, is the primary festival day for Freemasons.</note>, and we believe we are doing the Masonic public no unpleasant service by making them known here once more, e) and leaving it to every reader to compare them with Gleim's translation. f) <note>Johann Wilhelm Ludwig Gleim (1719–1803) was a famous German poet of the Enlightenment.</note> The translator has followed primarily the commented Glandorff edition. g)

> First serve the immortal gods, as it is commanded,
> And do not profane your oath: Then praise the deified heroes,
> And the protective spirits <note>original: "Schutzdämonen," referring to the *daemons* or guardian spirits of classical antiquity</note> with sacrifices of hallowed duties.
> Honor also your parents and those related to you by blood.
> From others, win as a friend the one best in virtue.
> Let yourself be guided by friendly words and charitable deeds,
> Without hating the friend for the sake of a small offense,
> As much as you possibly can; for capability borders closely on necessity <note>original: "denn Können gränzt nahe bey Müssen." A philosophical point that if one has the power to do good, it becomes a duty.</note>.
> Fulfill these things then. Strive to become a master
> Over the impulse to indulge, over sleep, and lust,
> And anger. Never do anything shameful, neither with others,
> Nor with yourself; rather, let yourself be most worthy of honor in your own eyes.

e) They are also included word-for-word in the 3rd volume of the *Dessau Pedagogical Negotiations*.
f) See Wieland’s *German Mercury*, May 1775. <note>Christoph Martin Wieland was a highly influential Enlightenment writer and editor.</note>
g) *Works of certain gnomic <note>Gnomic refers to aphorisms or moral maxims.</note> poets* <note>original Latin: "Sententiarum quorundam gnomicorum poetarum opera."</note>. 8. Leipzig 1776.

--- Page 199 ---
<meta>This page continues the translation of the "Golden Pythagorean Sayings" from page 166. The text shifts from the initial duties toward the gods and family to practical moral conduct and the nightly self-examination required of a Pythagorean seeker.</meta>

<sig>L 4</sig>

Further, practice justice in both words and deeds,
And accustom yourself never to act without thought;
Instead, remember that no one escapes the judgment of death:
Yet people are wont to gather goods now, only to lose them soon.
Whatever evils are assigned to you by divine decree <note>original: "Rathschluß," referring to a predetermined counsel or plan of God</note>, which oppress
Mankind, endure them with a gentle spirit and do not murmur against them;
But to alleviate them according to your ability is your duty: For consider,
That Heaven does not burden good souls too heavily.
Among men, both good and bad rumors are often known
To circulate: do not let yourself be frightened by them, nor let your settled
Purpose be shaken. If there are lies among them, remain silent
And unperturbed; but above all, take heed of the following teaching:
Let no one ever deceive you through words or actions
To do or say anything to your own detriment.
Deliberate before you undertake anything, to avoid folly;
(What a wretched fool is he who speaks and acts without thought!)
Do that which will not cause you regret soon thereafter.
Do not undertake a business that you do not understand, but learn
What serves you; then you will live through the most delightful days.
Sey not negligent in caring for the health of your body;
Rather, be moderate in eating, in drinking, and in exercise of the body:
I call that moderation which causes you no distress.
Be diligent in cleanliness, yet without living luxuriously.
Be temperate in all things that might stir up envy.
Do not be extravagant in your expenses, and stay within the bounds of honor,
Without being stingy: For measure is the best in all things.
Do what you cannot regret; therefore, examine your undertaking beforehand.

Beware that sleep does not close your eyes,
Until you have precisely examined the entire business of the day:
In what did I err? What did I do? What kind of duties did I neglect?

Review the series of your actions from beginning to end;
If you have done evil, then rebuke yourself; if good, rejoice;
Occupy yourself with this; let this be your diligence, your whole endeavor;
This will lead you into the footsteps of divine virtues:
(By him I swear, who taught us the fourfold number <note>The "fourfold number" refers to the *Tetractys*, a triangular figure of ten points arranged in four rows (1, 2, 3, and 4), which Pythagoreans revered as a symbol of the organization of the universe.</note>, as the source
Of eternal nature.) Work, proceed to your labor,

### X.

->*Two Masonic Discourses by Brother de G\*\*, Member of the Military Lodge of the Blazing Star, in Berlin, 1777. Published by Decker. 47 pages, large octavo.*<-

The short dedication is addressed to His Serene Highness Prince Frederick. The first discourse deals with the importance of Masonry, delivered on April 9, 1776, in the French Lodge of the Three Seraphim. It defines the fundamental rules of a true Mason and requires of him the duties of piety, charity, temperance, diligence, modesty (discretion), and zeal. The author next goes back to the mystical hieroglyphs of antiquity and shows the ancient forms of initiation and the sacred customs associated with them. The second discourse deals with the danger of a bad example; delivered on May 6, 1777, on the occasion of the initiation <note>original: "Aufnahme," literally 'reception' or 'acceptance' into the order</note> of the author’s biological brother. It is rich in practical maxims that can serve as a guide for the conduct of an aspiring Mason.

<summary>This page concludes a review of a Masonic songbook and summarizes two 1777 lectures by a member of the Military Lodge of the Blazing Star regarding the duties and history of Freemasonry.</summary>

### XI.
## Frederick.

->May you love to be called Father and Prince here.<-
<note>original Latin: "Hic ames dici Pater atque Princeps." From Horace, Odes, Book I, Ode 2, line 50.</note>
->Horace.<-

---

->8 pages in large octavo.<-

Read on July 18, 1777, during a solemn Masonic assembly <note>original: "Freymäurerversammlung"</note> arranged for the introduction of the Royal portrait <note>likely an official portrait of Frederick the Great</note> by the Mother Lodge of the Three Globes <note>original: "Mutterloge zu den drey Weltkugeln"</note> in Berlin. During this meeting, the name *Frederick* echoed at the end of every stanza; the opening motto from Horace refers to this, cited here as being from the first book, Ode 12, verses 1–4 <note>The quote "Hic ames..." is actually from Ode 2, but Ode 12 begins by asking "What man or hero...?" which fits the context of the speech.</note>. A title copperplate and two fitting vignettes by Brother C. L. Stahlbaum provide a special decoration to these pages. This speech can be found, albeit with orthographical errors, printed in the *Latest Diversities* <note>original: "Neuesten Mannichfaltigkeiten"</note>, 31st week of 1777. Here it is:

### I.

Heroic glory shines into eternity. Its threefold echo resounds on countless tongues. It flows through the hearts of all nations like water roaring down from the steepest cliffs into the surrounding valley fields. Bronze <note>original: "Erz," often referring to monumental brass or bronze</note> and marble carry it in indelible letters. The elderly stammer it, while youths and maidens <note>original: "Rosenmädchen," literally 'rose-maidens'</note> celebrate it. Spirited songs of bards pass it on to the coming world. Heroic names shine in the high starry vault: Who has gloriously won it?

->Frederick.<-

<vocab>Frederick, Masonic assembly, Mother Lodge of the Three Globes, Berlin, Horace, C. L. Stahlbaum, Heroic glory</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces a formal Masonic oration delivered in 1777 at the Mother Lodge "The Three Globes" in Berlin to honor King Frederick the Great. The text describes the ceremonial context and the poetic, celebratory language used to praise the monarch's fame.</summary>
<keywords>Frederick the Great, Mother Lodge of the Three Globes, Berlin, Freemasonry, Horace, C. L. Stahlbaum, Masonic oration, Enlightenment panegyric</keywords>

--- Page 203 ---
<meta>This page continues the text of a speech delivered in 1777 at the Mother Lodge of the Three Globes in Berlin, honoring King Frederick II (the Great). The previous page ended mid-sentence, describing how heroic names shine.</meta>

### II.

Great are the Scales of Europe <note>original: "Wage von Europa." This refers to the "Balance of Power," a central concept in 18th-century diplomacy</note>: their beam stretches from the Atlantic Ocean to the Arctic Sea. Their pans are two halves of the earth's poles. Great is the weight they carry. In one pan lie thick black clouds of human blood, crowns and scepters, sighs of entire nations heavy with curses, the slavish chains of freedom, plagues, swords, cannons, and murderous arrows. In the other lies the olive tree, untroubled peace, the blessing of fertile soil, the shield against malice, revenge, and rebellion. And who is the Earthly Zeus <note>original: "Erdenzeus." A classical allusion comparing the King to the king of the gods</note> who guides the colossal tongue of the scales toward equilibrium with Herculean strength?

->Frederick.<-

### III.

Paternal duty is sweet, yet bitter. Only giant shoulders are capable of the burden of a Father of the Country <term>Landesvater: a traditional German term for a sovereign, emphasizing his role as a protective father to his subjects</term>. Now a terrifying sternness thunders down upon the criminal; now tender hands reach out to catch the stumbling. Here is command; there, leniency. There is punishment to curb vice; here, rewards to crown merit. Busy, never-weary care for the good, active, and loyal subject; ever-watchful eyes for order and law; the unraveling of intertwined court intrigues; the rapid extinguishing of the smoldering sparks of neighboring unrest; divine compassion for provinces in need; the unlocking of the grain gates <note>original: "Kornthore." Frederick the Great was famous for his state-controlled granaries, which he opened during famines to keep bread prices low</note> for the cry of hunger; mercy for the harvest-less farmer; the building and beautification of cities; the cultivation of a new humanity <note>likely referring to Frederick’s policies of internal colonization and education</note>; the fertilization of the wastelands; public care for wounded veterans, for orphans, for the sick and the confused; and finally, the protection to be sealed with his own princely blood—the protection of the state; O Heaven, what duties! And

<summary>This page contains a highly metaphorical and laudatory speech comparing Frederick the Great to a divine figure who maintains the Balance of Power in Europe and fulfills the heavy paternal duties of a sovereign.</summary>
<keywords>Frederick the Great, Balance of Power, Sovereignty, Paternalism, Enlightenment Monarchy, Grain Granaries, Court Intrigues</keywords>

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>This page continues the speech honoring Frederick the Great. The previous page ended with a list of the King's duties, specifically his efforts to provide for his people during times of famine.</meta>

who has fulfilled them for an epoch of thirteen thousand five hundred days of rule?

->Frederick.<-

---

### IV.

The sciences and fine arts are the foster-mothers of national morality, the driving wheels of industry, and the electric tinder for geniuses. The spirit of budding youth is prepared in the schools <note>original: "Pflanzschulen," literally "plant-nurseries," a metaphor for educational institutions</note>: it bears buds and blossoms, and finally from it ripen men for the art of arms, for the judicial benches, for the administration of the state, for the church, natural science, and history. Inventive power catches the outlines of creative nature and imitates it in paintings, in sounds, and in likenesses. Thalia <note>The Muse of comedy</note> teaches one to joke with Attic salt <note>A classical term for refined, sophisticated wit</note>; Melpomene <note>The Muse of tragedy</note> to weep nobly; Polymnia <note>The Muse of sacred song and rhetoric</note> teaches singing, Terpsichore <note>The Muse of dance</note> the finer dance, and Urania <note>The Muse of astronomy</note> the path of the stars. And who is the head of the German Aonides <note>A poetic name for the Muses</note>? Who is the Apollo who himself thinks with Platonic depth, himself tunes the Orpheic lyre <note>A reference to Frederick's well-known talent as a flute player and composer</note>, himself pragmatically examines the documents of antiquity, himself tests the scholars of the first magnitude, and himself orders the higher taste of the stage and the choral dances?

->Frederick.<-

---

### V.

Alliances, formed in close circles, strengthened with powerful oaths, and destined for the warmest feeling of philanthropy and the highest intentions, are hated by the short-sighted mob and envious curiosity: hatred breeds persecution; persecution undermines the dam of freedom of conscience. The Masonic order <term>maurerische Bund: refers to Freemasonry, a fraternal organization Frederick the Great famously joined and protected</term> possesses internal strength, but it must lean upon the arm of the powerful on earth. Its magnetic chain, propagated for millennia, is indestructible; but mighty links must protect it against external attacks. Which ruler of Teutonia <note>A poetic or Latinized name for Germany</note> has his

<summary>The speaker praises Frederick the Great as a modern Apollo who fosters the arts and sciences, while also identifying him as the essential protector of the Freemasons against public prejudice.</summary>
<keywords>Frederick the Great, Enlightenment, Patronage, Muses, Education, Freemasonry, Freedom of Conscience, Apollo</keywords>

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>This page continues the rhetorical tribute to Frederick the Great's virtues and his relationship with Freemasonry, picking up from the previous page's list of his accomplishments as a ruler and patron of the arts.</meta>

...exchanged the diadem <note>A royal crown or headband</note> for the apron <note>The ceremonial apron worn by Freemasons to symbolize their craft and labor</note>, wielded the consecrated hammer <note>The gavel, a symbolic tool used by the leader of a Masonic lodge</note> as a brother, and felt that equality which reigned in the Elysian <note>In Greek mythology, Elysium was a paradise for the virtuous; here it refers to a "golden age"</note> world-age? Which monarch has declared our brotherhood to be a Society of the Wise (truly the most beautiful and most valid title of honor!) and dedicated his image to it—an ideal that glows like phosphorus <note>A substance known for its ability to shine in the dark</note> in all patriotic hearts?

->Frederick the Great.<-

Yes, the greatest king the world ever saw; the most sublime man, whom perhaps a hundred Olympiads <note>A period of four years; a hundred Olympiads suggests a span of four centuries</note> could not produce; the founder and the protective patron of our Lodge. Long may he live!

Reflections on Wisdom. ### XIV.

To His Serene Highness Prince Frederick Augustus of Brunswick-Lüneburg. Berlin, November 3, 1777. Large octavo <term>octavo: A standard book size where the printed sheet is folded to create eight leaves</term>. With a title page engraved in copper by Brother C. L. Stahlbaum and two vignettes <note>Decorative illustrations or ornaments often found on the title page or at the ends of chapters</note>.

This celebration marks the Lustrum <term>Lustrum: A period of five years; here referring to a five-year anniversary</term> of the installation of this most venerable Grand Master of all the united lodges in the Prussian States, which took place on November 2, 1772. We share here this ode, which until now has been known to only a few brothers:

> At high noon, the sun does not dazzle
> The strongly tested eyes of the Mason: He
> Sees, even through dark caverns of imagery,
> Eternal truth in a purple glow.
>
> He climbs the great ladder of creation,
> And contemplates the primordial seed of all that is created;
> And his power is like a fire,
> That does not destroy, but only warms and nourishes.

->And take the palm that adorns the victor<-
->In the temple of immortality.<-

### XV.

At the close of the year 1777, in a solemn assembly of the Lodge of the Three Globes <term>Lodge of the Three Globes: The "Zu den drei Weltkugeln" lodge in Berlin, the oldest Masonic Grand Lodge in Prussia, founded in 1740</term> in Berlin. 8 pages, large octavo <term>octavo: A book size resulting from folding a printed sheet into eight leaves</term>.

## Here is this short speech:

Soon — soon — soon the midnight pulse of returning nature strikes! The second, pregnant with meaning, approaches; the last of twelve great hours of a long solar year <note>The speaker uses the metaphor of a single day to describe the entire passing year</note>. Then the sun, that fiery finger of the almighty hand, steps upon the point where, as a golden hand on the boundless clock of the firmament, it rolls into a new cycle of time.

Time! What a precious, never-purchasable gift of the Infinite, the un-originated Father, who through the Word created space, in space worlds, and in worlds an indivisible chain of causes and effects. Time! What a

<vocab>sanctuary, wisdom, mastery, Lodge of the Three Globes, Berlin, speech, nature, sun, clock of the firmament, time</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes a poetic tribute to Prince Frederick Augustus and introduces a philosophical Masonic oration regarding the nature of time and creation at the end of 1777.</summary>
<keywords>Prince Frederick Augustus of Brunswick-Lüneburg, Lodge of the Three Globes, Berlin, Masonic oration, philosophy of time, nature, Grand Master</keywords>

--- Page 208 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical oration delivered at the Lodge of the Three Globes at the end of 1777, picking up mid-sentence from the previous page's meditation on the nature of time.</meta>

terrible image of what we were, are, and will be. Like ebb and flow, moved by the ever-surging world-soul <term>world-soul: Or "Anima Mundi," an ancient philosophical concept that an intrinsic spirit infuses all living things on earth</term>, it rushes down and back again annually. And we, we swirl along as tiny droplets of the sea; finally, it throws us upon the dark shore of death—and whither?

Shuddering thought! Whither? Thither, where in the sum of centuries already counted and yet to come, no digit stands; where the longest epoch is but the swift passage of striking lightning. — Either Elysium <note>Elysium: In Greek mythology, the paradise reserved for the heroic and the virtuous</note>, or swallowing Orcus <note>Orcus: A Roman name for the underworld or the god of the dead, often associated with punishment in the afterlife</note>, or a middle state between interrogation and judgment. A re-germination—to the divine primal destiny; a ripe or uncertain bloom, or to wither and yet not be able to fade away. Laugh then, O freethinker <note>original: "Freygeist" — a term often used in the 18th century to describe deists, atheists, or those who rejected traditional religious dogma</note>, and gnash your teeth, and stagger, and spin yourself a fabric of comfort out of Voltaire's <note>original: "Voltärschen" — Referring to the French philosopher Voltaire (1694–1778), who was a symbol of Enlightenment skepticism. The speaker here views his influence as a "poisonous thread"</note> poisonous threads: nevertheless, shortly before the tomb, your conscience shall rattle; your failing eye looks out into eternity with frozen dread, and the invisible witness of life walks with you!

Annals of human age, what are they? Childhood is an inactive, slumbering dawn; youth, like the laughing red of morning, and dallying like the children of spring; manhood, a scorching noon in the heat of boiling passions; further on, the evening full of care, and finally the gray night, veiling all the splendor of worldly joys.

To think of our existence as purposeless is a rebellion against the Creator. To act without intent would be shameful folly even for thinking creatures. Should the All-Wise have planted us in the most beautiful fields of the universe without expecting fruit from us? No! That He could not, He would not. Trust, love, and glorification are blessed fruits; but parched idleness, spongy sensuality, and thorny vice are deadly weeds. Ask yourself,

The speaker warns against the skepticism of freethinkers like Voltaire, urging the audience to bear "blessed fruit" rather than "deadly weeds."</summary>
<keywords>Time, Death, Afterlife, World-soul, Voltaire, Freethinker, Morality, Virtue vs. Vice, 18th-century Oratory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical oration from the Lodge of the Three Globes. ...you, youth, man, or old man! Were your days fruitfully blessed, or, through your own fault, without a harvest?

Examination, my brothers! This duty-heavy word echoes a thousand times from the Master’s threshold <term>Master’s threshold: The symbolic entrance into the higher degrees of Masonic knowledge and responsibility</term> into your ears. To settle accounts with oneself; to ask whether more spiritual than physical, more philanthropic than hateful, more pure than impure feelings have gathered in our breast? To weigh whether the scale of the good sinks, or if it rises from the heavy weight of evil? To count the building blocks <note>original: "Bausteine" — often referring to the "ashlars" or stones a Mason carves to build the symbolic temple of humanity</note> that one has carried toward the completion of the sacred temple without fatigue or resentment: To round the line of truth with the compasses of reason: To search whether one could confidently offer neck and chest to the sword-stroke that threatens the breach of the Masonic oath? That is examination!

Soon the pulse of returning nature will beat—We still live! The light of the Godhead, which flows through all stars and elements, still beams down on us with streams of grace. She <note>The author personifies the Godhead or Nature as a feminine guiding force</note> still wields sublime scepters; and mighty allies protect us. The olive branch of peace still turns green. Sing, my brothers, sing your cloud-parting psalms of praise. But prepare yourselves for the future: Behind the gray curtain, the great scene awaits you; marveled at by all contemporaries! — Storms, how easily they can twist the rudder of unsteady fortune; how easily they can shake the strongest mast. Yet, even if everything is shattered, our Order remains standing: for it stands upon sacred, unshakable pillars.

<sig>IV.</sig>

*F. Bourdet, Engineer Officer & Inspector General of Hydraulics to His Majesty the King of Prussia, & Orator of the Lodge "Frederick of the Three Seraphim" in Berlin. For the benefit of the Poor. Berlin, published by G. J. Decker 1777. 28 pages, large octavo.* <note>original: "gr. 8." (gross octavo) refers to the size of the printed book</note>

The dedication is addressed to His Serene Highness Prince Frederick of Brunswick-Lüneburg. The author regards the Masonic Order as a single nation, which, though scattered across the globe by the particular interests of each individual, is nonetheless reunited in various places whenever the opportunity arises.

He does not, however, wish to write an apology <note>In this 18th-century context, an "apology" refers to a formal written defense of a doctrine or organization</note> for the Order, as the preface states; for this would only dim its luster. He merely wishes to tell the uninitiated <note>original: "Ungeweihten" — those who have not been formally admitted into the secrets of the Masonic lodge</note> that its meetings, which so greatly catch the eye of certain restless minds, are nothing more than a school of morals and reliable guides to the path of virtue.

This is not intended to gain proselytes <note>converts or new members</note>; for the Order imposes itself on no one; it must be sought out and requested. Rather, it is intended to open the eyes of those who harbor such distorted notions about it.

<summary>This page introduces a publication by F. Bourdet, an engineer and Masonic orator, who describes Freemasonry as a global nation and a school of virtue rather than a secretive or proselytizing group.</summary>
<keywords>F. Bourdet, Frederick of the Three Seraphim, Prince Frederick of Brunswick-Lüneburg, Masonic apology, school of morals, Berlin Freemasonry</keywords>
