### Circular Letter,
### concerning Basedow’s Philanthropinum.

When individual brothers, or lodges, seek to alleviate the misery of the needy and the suffering, they are indeed fulfilling one of the primary duties that characterize the spirit of our sublime <term>Order<note>The Masonic Order, a fraternal organization dedicated to moral improvement and charity.</note></term>, which is founded upon philanthropy and benevolence. However, for the most part, the Mason’s assistance is restricted only to mitigating the existing misery of mankind and making misfortune bearable for those who are already poor and oppressed. To few is it given to aid the human race in a more publicly beneficial and widespread manner, such that the number of the needy might be noticeably reduced in the future, fewer oppressed people might be seen, and that these might be properly raised and their hearts cultivated. Almost commonly, the years of youth are the time when a person is most neglected, and by this very means is prepared to be a burdensome load upon the state for their whole life, and to be a helpless, unhappy creature for themselves—who is all the more wretched the less the faculties of their soul were developed in the years of their bloom.

If, therefore, philanthropists and noble men hold it close to their hearts to help the human race as a whole in the most reliable way, let their entire care be directed toward <insert>improving</insert> the education of youth to

<summary>This document is a Masonic circular letter promoting Johann Bernhard Basedow’s "Philanthropinum," arguing that systemic education is a more effective form of charity than merely relieving existing poverty.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, Philanthropinum, Johann Bernhard Basedow, education reform, Enlightenment charity, social welfare, pedagogy</keywords>

--- Page 216 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the sentence from the previous page regarding the improvement of youth education, specifically advocating for the support of Johann Bernhard Basedow’s educational institution.</meta>

improve; and, if they cannot make their lives comfortable, they should nevertheless make it their concern to produce from them useful citizens, enriched with knowledge and thereby freed from want.

Our German fatherland is so fortunate to know of an institution within its borders that perfectly corresponds to this general ultimate purpose. Who here does not think of Father <term>Basedow<note>Johann Bernhard Basedow (1724–1790), a leading German educational reformer of the Enlightenment who sought to modernize teaching through practical experience and reason.</note></term>, of his orphaned <term>Philanthropinum<note>An experimental school founded in Dessau in 1774. Basedow’s "Philanthropinum" was designed to educate children of all classes according to "philanthropic" or humanitarian principles, though it often struggled with financial and administrative stability.</note></term>, whose caregiver, sustainer—everything—has withered away due to the inactivity and ineffectiveness of the citizens of the world, and for whose support the noble-minded Meyer so emphatically, so <term>Masonically<note>In this context, acting "Masonically" implies acting with the specific charitable and fraternal virtues associated with Freemasonry.</note></term> encourages?

The 3rd volume of the *Philanthropic Archive* gives those who were not acquainted with the institute a full explanation of what it could and should have become, and what—to the shame of our times—it currently is not.

Noble, upright Masons, recognize your duty to be active for the benefit of your fellow creatures—more active than the great multitude around you! It is your responsibility to prove to the world that being called a "Mason" is no empty title. Seize this beautiful opportunity to strengthen the honor of the <term>Order<note>The Masonic Order.</note></term>, to establish a cornerstone against which the malice of your adversaries shall shatter, and to leave behind for a happier posterity an indestructible monument of your kindness and <term>philanthropy<note>Original: "Menschenliebe," literally "love of human beings," a central tenet of Enlightenment thought.</note></term>. Consider if, in the future, even only ten souls emerge annually from this <term>nursery<note>Original: "Pflanzschule," a common metaphor for an educational institution where young minds are "cultivated" like plants.</note></term> who carry and spread the spirit of the true philanthropist into the world—what glorious fruits may not be hoped for from these new citizens of the world! How your memory will remain a blessing among them

<keywords>Basedow, Philanthropinum, Freemasonry, educational reform, philanthropy, Meyer, Enlightenment, citizens of the world</keywords>

--- Page 217 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues a speech or letter encouraging the Masonic lodge members to financially support Johann Bernhard Basedow’s Philanthropinum, following the previous page's call to action for the benefit of future generations.</meta>

...live among them, even if your perishable huts have long since rested softly in the silent valleys of death. Think that even a single good deed is worthy of its reward; how much more so when the welfare of the world occupies you.

Read the third issue of the *Archive* <note>original: *Archiv* - likely referring to the *Pedagogical Archive*, a journal documenting educational reforms of the time</note>, and see how Basedow’s protégé <note>The author uses the word *Zögling* (pupil/protégé) metaphorically here to refer to the educational project or the school itself</note> sighs in its last gasps, without any compassionate hand from the public rushing to its aid.

Consider what you can contribute annually without it burdening you, and—not with formal pomposity, but in your hearts—vow to yourselves that, as long as you live, you will repeat this contribution yearly, so that a certain amount results each year upon which one can reliably count.

I know well that our help will hardly flourish so much that something significant will be gathered by us toward the 30,000 <term>Reichsthalers<note>The Reichsthaler (Rthlr) was a standard silver coin used throughout the Holy Roman Empire; 30,000 was a massive sum at the time.</note></term> that Basedow requires for the completion of his work. However, annual contributions of 500 to 800 Reichsthalers, coming in so reliably—are they not just as good as a capital of 10,000 Reichsthalers? And do you not reckon that, encouraged by your example, God will already awaken the hearts of those who are outside our circle? And finally, consider: what are 500 to 800 Reichsthalers for such a widespread and—fortunate for us that I can say it—for such an enlightened philanthropic society?

->Tr., January 14th, 1777.<-

### Scattered
### Reflections on the Title-Vignette.

Soon, commentaries <note>original: *Glossen* - scholarly interpretations or marginalia that can sometimes obscure the original text</note> overwhelmed the high hymn of eternal generation out of ancient night, of the love of the fertilizing Spirit toward its principle <note>original: *Prinzipium*</note>, of strife and counter-strife. Here and there, individual, broken sounds and tones echoed from behind the veil <note>original: *Schleyer*</note> of holy mysteries; but nowhere did a pure triad <note>original: *Dreyklang*</note> in primal harmony resonate anymore from the holy seven-stringed lyre. — Who does not know the man who, full of high inspiration and with unshakable courage, gathered these scattered tones back into a pure spherical melody <note>This is a reference to the "Music of the Spheres," a celestial harmony Pythagoras believed was produced by the movement of heavenly bodies</note> — Most important period! Even after millennia, your light shines and illuminates for the seeker of truth the dark path to the innermost part of nature —

According to Bentley <note>Richard Bentley (1662–1742) was an English classical scholar and critic</note>, Pythagoras was born between the third and fourth year of the 43rd Olympiad <term>Olympiad<note>A period of four years used by the Ancient Greeks to reckon time, starting from 776 BCE.</note></term>, or 586 years before the birth of Christ; according to Lloyd <note>William Lloyd (1627–1717), an English bishop and chronologist</note> in the third year of the 48th, and according to Dodwell <note>Henry Dodwell (1641–1711), an Anglo-Irish scholar known for his works on ancient chronology</note> in the 52nd Olympiad. All cite reasons for their opinions, and Schrader 1) therefore considers it safest to assume that he was born neither before the 43rd nor after the 52nd Olympiad, but in the interval between the two.

1) Dissertation on Pythagoras <note>original: *Dissert. de Pythagora*</note>, Leipzig <note>original: *Lipsiæ*</note> 1708.

It contrasts the "broken sounds" of lost ancient wisdom with the harmonious system reconstructed by Pythagoras.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biographical sketch of Pythagoras, transitioning from a debate over his birth date (mentioned on the previous page) to a discussion regarding his birthplace and early education under Pherecydes. It explores the theory that Pythagorean wisdom originated with the Etruscans rather than the Greeks.</meta>

den. <note>The sentence continues from the previous page: "...at the age of eighty, he died." or similar.</note> Just as uncertain as the year of his birth is his homeland. Some say that he was born in Sidon and raised in Samos; still others consider him an Etruscan <note>The Etruscans were a powerful civilization in ancient Italy, predating the rise of Rome, known for their advanced religious and artistic culture.</note>, and more recently the famous Scipio Maffei 2) defended this opinion. He argued that arts and sciences flourished among this people long before, when the Greeks were still barbarians, so much so that even Plato 3) and Cicero 4) themselves confess that the Greeks adopted many religious customs from them. They were the first to possess pure concepts of God, and idolatry <note>The worship of physical idols or images, which the author suggests was a later corruption of a more "pure" ancient monotheism.</note> was an invention of later times; therefore, according to this opinion, Pythagoras also drew his first idea of the divine nature from them.

This much, meanwhile, is certain: that Samos was the place where he was raised, and where Pherecydes instructed him in symbolic theology, cosmogony <term>Cosmogony<note>The branch of science or philosophy dealing with the origin and evolution of the universe.</note></term>, and natural science—subjects which Orpheus had brought from Egypt to Greece, and whose dark, mystical sounds spread everywhere. Always and from the very beginning, Zeus existed alongside Time and Chaos; only then did Earth first come into being when Love breathed upon it, and Zeus blessed the new Earth with the gifts of his goodness. So goes the beginning of his lost book 5) on the origin of things. — Obscurity reigns everywhere in his history. Nearly two hundred years after his death

--- Page 220 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Pythagoras. The previous page discussed his early education under Pherecydes of Syros and his potential Etruscan origins. This page transitions to the historical sources of his life and his extensive travels to the East in search of ancient wisdom.</meta>

[...after his] death, his life was only first described by Heraclides Ponticus, Timaeus, and others, and from these, Porphyry and Iamblichus <note>Porphyry and Iamblichus were Neoplatonist philosophers of the 3rd and 4th centuries AD whose biographies of Pythagoras remain our primary, though often legendary, sources for his life.</note> later drew their accounts in the 4th century. Although they also consulted other sources, traditions, and secret writings, out of love for their founder they often embellished facts and invented occurrences; this cannot be denied and, despite their good intentions, cannot be excused in any way.

I therefore narrow down the most important events of his life and relate only that which withstands the touchstone of criticism. Not satisfied with the instruction of Pherecydes, his inquisitive spirit burned to drink from the source itself and to unravel the meaning of the symbols in which his teacher had left him in uncertainty. He therefore first traveled through the Greek islands, was initiated into the Samothracian mysteries <note>The Samothracian mysteries were secret religious rites held on the island of Samothrace, focused on the Great Gods (Cabiri) and promised protection at sea and a better afterlife.</note>, and visited Thales of Miletus, Bias, and Anaximander, the most famous natural philosophers of that time. From there he traveled to Phoenicia, visited Byblos, Tyre, and other famous cities, and was instructed by the priests and hierophants <note>Hierophants were high priests who interpreted sacred mysteries and esoteric principles.</note> in all holy sciences and customs. He then returned to Sidon and visited the school of the prophets there, who were disciples of Mochus or Moschus, called the natural philosopher by his contemporaries. Arcerius, Selden, and Huet <note>These 17th-century scholars were known for "Prisca Theologia," the attempt to prove that all ancient wisdom originated from Moses and the Hebrew Bible.</note> consider this Phoenician natural philosopher to be Moses; however, Scheffer, Stillingfleet, and Morhof have opposed this opinion with very strong arguments.

<summary>The author critiques the legendary biographies of Pythagoras by Porphyry and Iamblichus before detailing Pythagoras’s travels to Samothrace, Phoenicia, and Sidon to study under various sages and priests.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Porphyry, Iamblichus, Pherecydes, Samothracian mysteries, Thales of Miletus, Phoenicia, Sidon, Mochus, Moses, historiography</keywords>

--- Page 221 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Pythagoras, following his travels from Phoenicia to Egypt. The previous page ended with him visiting the school of the prophets in Sidon; this page describes his arrival in Egypt and his initiation into the priesthood.</meta>

After this preparation, he readied himself for his great Egyptian journey, the primary object of his burning thirst for knowledge. Even before this, he visited the temple on Mount Carmel <note>Mount Carmel, located in modern-day Israel, was considered a sacred site by many ancient cultures and is frequently associated with the prophet Elijah in the biblical tradition.</note> not far from Ptolemais in the borders of Asher and Zebulun, and called upon the deity for blessing and success for his important undertaking. This point in the history of Pythagoras has given many scholars occasion for bitter mockery, and yet it is one of the most important. Thrice-blessed place, where — — But may it never be profaned by me! — <note>The author adopts a reverent, almost mystical tone here, perhaps hinting at the esoteric or Masonic significance often attributed to these locations.</note> Polycrates, the ruler of Samos, had recommended him through letters to King Amasis of Egypt, and the latter sent him to the priests of Heliopolis <note>Heliopolis, the "City of the Sun," was a major religious center in ancient Egypt, famous for its temple to the sun god Ra.</note>. Since the Egyptians were extremely cautious regarding admission to their secrets, and expressed the greatest mistrust especially toward foreigners, he was sent from here to Memphis, and by the priests of that city again to Diospolis <note>Diospolis is the Greek name for Thebes, a capital of ancient Egypt and a center of the cult of Amun.</note>, in order to discourage him from his intention if possible. 

But when they recognized his resolute spirit, which distinguished itself so much from the usual frivolous character of his countrymen, he was finally admitted to the trials after he had first been circumcised <note>In antiquity, it was commonly believed that the Egyptian priests required candidates for the higher mysteries to undergo circumcision as a rite of purification.</note>. He endured everything with firm, masculine determination and was admitted to the innermost secrets of the priests. For a long time, he studied the most sublime sciences in their subterranean dwellings, until that epoch so tragic for Egypt, when their empire was overrun by Cambyses <note>Cambyses II, the Persian King who conquered Egypt in 525 BCE. Ancient traditions claim Pythagoras was then taken as a prisoner to Babylon.</note> and almost brought to ruin.

<summary>This page describes Pythagoras's journey to Egypt, his persistence in seeking initiation from wary Egyptian priests, and his eventual admission into their most sacred mysteries until the Persian conquest by Cambyses.</summary>

<keywords>Pythagoras, Mount Carmel, Egypt, Heliopolis, Memphis, Diospolis, King Amasis, Polycrates of Samos, Egyptian priesthood, initiation, Cambyses II, mysteries</keywords>

--- Page 222 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Pythagoras’s travels. Having been in Egypt during the Persian conquest (alluded to by his capture), he is now moved from Egypt to Babylon, transitioning from the study of Egyptian mysteries to the wisdom of the Chaldeans, Jews, and Persians.</meta>

Pythagoras was led into captivity to Babylon along with other priests. But this initially unfortunate accident took the best possible turn for him: he became acquainted with the wise men and priests of the land, and through their instruction, expanded his knowledge in astronomy and natural science <note>In the 18th and 19th centuries, "Naturlehre" or natural science referred to the study of the physical world, often encompassing physics and biology as part of "natural philosophy."</note>. He especially benefited from the teaching of a wise man whom Porphyry calls Zabratus, whereas Clement of Alexandria 6) calls him Nazaratus; Huet and Selden hold him to be the prophet Ezekiel, but since Clement of Alexandria explicitly contradicts this in the cited passage, Wendelin considers this wise man to be the prophet Daniel. This much is certain, that Pythagoras here, through interaction with Jews, became acquainted with the Mosaic Law and the sacred customs of this nation, several of which he also adopted into his system—for example, the practice of worshipping with bare feet, circumcision, and others. Josephus therefore claims that he drew his most sublime and important teachings from Jewish sources, and this is likewise claimed by Saint Origen, 7) Ambrose, 8) and Clement of Alexandria. 9)

Since the Babylonian Empire was now subject to the Persians, Pythagoras also found opportunity to explore the wisdom of the Magi <note>The Magi were the hereditary priestly class of ancient Persia, famous for their expertise in astronomy, astrology, and the religious teachings of Zoroaster.</note>. He finally regained his freedom after having lived in Babylon for several years, and the first use he made of it was to undertake a journey to India,

<meta>This page continues the biography of Pythagoras during his period of extensive travel. Following his time in Babylon, where he studied under Persian Magi and potentially encountered Jewish prophets, he now moves further east to India before beginning his return journey to Greece via Arabia and Delos.</meta>

...took, in order to learn the teachings of the Brahmins <note>The highest-ranking social class and priestly order in Hindu society.</note>. Even to this day, these teachings have been preserved; and as absurd as the mythological system of the common people may be, the doctrines of the wise are equally sublime. These are contained in their sacred books and written in the Sanskrit language, which is understood only by the priests. Holwell, 10) who spent thirty years in these countries and studied the sacred books himself, has given us the most complete account of the secret teachings of the Brahmins. He asserts that the tradition of Pythagoras’s travels to these regions has been preserved in Hindustan <note>A historical name for the northern part of the Indian subcontinent.</note> even until now. He further claims that by comparing the remaining fragments of Pythagoras's work with these Indian writings, it becomes quite clear that he drew his knowledge of the unity and providence of God, the immortality of the soul, and metempsychosis <term>Metempsychosis: the philosophical concept of the "transmigration of souls," where a soul is reborn into a new body after death</term> from these sacred books and brought them back to Greece.

On the return journey to his fatherland, he passed through Arabia and also made the acquaintance of the wise men and priests there. In Delos, he found his first teacher, Pherecydes, on his deathbed; he committed him to the earth, and after performing this duty of humanity, he traveled back to Samos and established a school there. Because of many frustrations, he soon left this place again and undertook new philosophical travels. In Crete, he studied the laws

He visited Lycurgus, Leontes, and other wise men, and at the same time was initiated into the mysteries of the Eleusinian Ceres <note>Ceres is the Roman equivalent of the Greek goddess Demeter; the Eleusinian Mysteries were the most famous and secret religious rites of ancient Greece.</note>. From here he went to Delphi, the oracle famous throughout all Greece, and was instructed in all the secrets of the Delphic deity by the High Priestess Themistoclea. Various writers of antiquity consider this priestess to be the sister of Pythagoras, though this is contradicted by others; Schrader <margin>11) In the place and book cited.</margin> has presented all these arguments in detail.

After this journey, he finally chose Crotone <note>A Greek colony in Southern Italy, famous for its physicians and athletes.</note> as his permanent place of residence and established a school in the house of the famous athlete Milo, which flourished greatly in a short time, and here he also died at a very advanced age. However, writers are very much at odds regarding both the manner of his death and his age; while some set his age at 99 years, <margin>12) Iamblichus, *Life of Pythagoras*.</margin> others assume a few years less. <margin>13) Eusebius, *Preparation for the Gospel* <note>Original: "Præparat. evangel."</note></margin>

Never, perhaps, were the opinions of scholars more divided and contradictory regarding the worth of an ancient sage than they are concerning Pythagoras. According to some, he is a prophet, seer, and miracle-worker; according to others

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the reputation of Pythagoras. The previous page ended with the observation that scholars are divided on whether he was a prophet or a miracle-worker.</meta>

...r, but rather a weak, superstitious mind and a fanatical monk; and a more recent writer <margin>14) Meiners' *Essay on the History of Religion of the Oldest Nations, Especially the Egyptians*. Göttingen, 1775. p. 313.</margin> considers him merely a shrewd politician and the head of a political party. This latter opinion has much in its favor, and the violent extermination of his followers gives it great weight. However, why all the arduous and dangerous journeys, which were undertaken solely with the intent of developing natural philosophy <term>Naturlehre: a term used historically to describe the study of nature and the physical world before the modern definition of "science" was established.</term> and the true meaning of the sacred legends and fables, if he had no further purpose than to be the head of a party opposed to despotism? Certainly, this latter was only a secondary aim, and scientific objects were the primary subjects with which Pythagoras occupied his students.

Knowledge and a view of the dual world—the visible and the invisible, the spiritual and the physical—the manifold levels and orders, and their interaction within and through one another, were the preeminent objects of Pythagorean wisdom. His students were divided into two classes: exoteric <note>From the Greek *exōterikos*, meaning "outer" or public; these students received only the general teachings.</note> and esoteric <note>From the Greek *esōterikos*, meaning "inner" or private; these were the inner circle of students initiated into deeper mysteries.</note>. Only the latter knew the true meaning of the symbols, under whose veil the most sublime doctrines of theology and natural philosophy lay hidden. However, they were bound by the most solemn oath to reveal nothing of them, but rather to endure death than to make these secrets known to a stranger. This very custom was also, according to the testimony of Jo-

--- Page 226 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the strict secrecy required of Pythagoras's followers, comparing their discipline to that of the Essenes and addressing the authenticity of writings attributed to Pythagoras.</meta>

Josephus <margin>15) *The Jewish War*, book II, chapter 7.</margin> notes regarding the Essenes <note>The Essenes were a Jewish sect known for their asceticism and communal lifestyle; early modern scholars often compared them to the Pythagoreans due to their shared emphasis on secrecy and hierarchy.</note>: Everyone who entered their society had to swear neither to conceal any secret from the initiated nor to reveal anything to the uninitiated; rather, they were to surrender their life sooner than the secret entrusted to them. Likewise, they were not permitted to reveal anything more to a member of their own society than what was appropriate for them to know according to the level <note>original: *Stuffe* (modern *Stufe*), referring to the hierarchical grades or degrees of initiation within the sect.</note> on which they stood.

To cut off all possible ways through which the secret doctrines might be spread, all Pythagoreans were forbidden from writing anything down about them; instead, everything was passed on orally. Although ancient writers cite various works supposedly written by Pythagoras, who does not see immediately that they must be forged <note>original: *untergeschoben*, meaning "foisted" or "fraudulently attributed."</note>? He surely would not have violated a law that constituted the very essence of his society. It has been demonstrated with strong arguments <margin>16) *The Sententious Works of the Most Ancient Gnomic Poets*, Edited and studied by E. G. Glandorf, Leipzig 1776, page 67 and following.</margin> that the so-called Pythagorean Golden Verses <note>A collection of ethical exhortations traditionally attributed to Pythagoras, but now believed by scholars to have been compiled by later followers.</note> are not the work of this sage, but rather contain at most only the collected teachings of his students or of the mysteries as a whole.

This gave rise to the many writings, absurd and dissolute tales with which antiquity amused itself at the expense of the Pythagoreans—such as the story of the golden thigh, which he is said to have shown to Abaris <note>Abaris the Hyperborean was a legendary priest and healer. Myth claims Pythagoras showed him his golden thigh to prove he was the god Apollo.</note>

<summary>This page compares the Pythagorean oath of secrecy to that of the Essenes and argues that Pythagoras never wrote down his teachings, suggesting that works like the "Golden Verses" are later attributions.</summary>
<keywords>Essenes, Josephus, secrecy, oral tradition, Pythagoras, Golden Verses, Abaris, authenticity, initiation levels</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page that writings attributed to Pythagoras are likely forgeries. It specifically addresses how later philosophers misrepresented or plagiarized Pythagorean thought because the original followers maintained a strict oral tradition.</meta>

should, etc., and which, as Crüger <margin>17) *Dissertation on the Golden Thigh of Pythagoras* <note>The "Golden Thigh" refers to a legend that Pythagoras possessed a thigh of gold; the author suggests this was an allegory for his advanced scientific knowledge rather than a physical claim.</note></margin> and Tollius <margin>18) *Fortuita* etc. Amsterdam 1687, p. 204.</margin> have likely demonstrated, should be nothing more than a symbolic representation of his knowledge in higher natural science. Porphyry <note>Porphyry (c. 234–305 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher and biographer of Pythagoras.</note> therefore warns that one must not take everything the common crowd recounts about Pythagoras as his actual teachings.

To this was added the fact that the other Greek sages of that time—such as Plato, Aristotle, Speusippus, Aristoxenus, and Xenocrates—took advantage of the circumstance that the Pythagoreans recorded nothing. They wrote down the doctrines of this school, which they believed they understood, in a one-sided, fragmented, and mutilated fashion as they encountered them, claiming these as their own. Afterward, to better conceal their plagiarism, they turned other doctrines that were less understandable to them into objects of ridicule, presenting them as the definitive teachings of this school. Perhaps the most authentic teachings of Pythagoras could still be separated out from the works of Plato, because he traveled to Tarentum <margin>19) Cicero, *On the Ends of Good and Evil* <note>original: *de finibus bonorum et malorum*</note>.</margin> specifically with the intention of being instructed by Pythagoras's students.

Many passages in Porphyry and Iamblichus <note>Iamblichus (c. 245–325 AD) was a Syrian Neoplatonist who wrote extensively on Pythagoreanism.</note> indicate that Pythagoras intended <term>metempsychosis</term> <note>The transmigration of souls; the belief that the soul inhabits different bodies (human or animal) over successive lifetimes.</note> in his secret doctrine to be understood symbolically rather than literally, and that through it, various degrees of spiritual purification were indicated before one could soar back to their ethereal homeland. The spirit is bound to the body, and all

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the soul according to Pythagorean and Neoplatonic thought, specifically how the soul is imprisoned within the body. losing its heavenly freedom; its clinging imperfections make these bonds even tighter; it must pass through many stages before they break and it regains its initial freedom. Heraclitus <note>Heraclitus of Ephesus (c. 535 – c. 475 BC) was a pre-Socratic Greek philosopher known for his doctrine of change being central to the universe.</note> therefore called life the death of the soul, and death its true life. To attain this life of the soul even before its final dissolution was the great purpose of the Alexandrian philosophy <note>The author refers here to the Neoplatonic school in Alexandria, which emphasized mystical union with the divine and the soul's ascent.</note>. —

I could say various other things here about the **symbols** <term>Symbola: These were short, cryptic maxims or "watchwords" used by the Pythagoreans to communicate moral and philosophical truths to initiates.</term> in which Pythagoras shrouded his teachings; however, I shall break off. For further reflection, I will only set down a few here:

> Remove all iron from yourself and cast it away. <note>original Latin: *Aciem omnem abs te removeto & avertite*; German: *Lege alles Eisen ab und thue es von dir*.</note>

> When putting on your shoes, put the right foot forward first. <note>original Latin: *Dum calceamenta pedibus subduntur pedem dextrum ante afferto*; German: *Sind die Schuhe abgelegt, so setze zuerst den rechten Fuß vor*.</note>

# I.
# O d e
### to His Serene Highness Duke Ferdinand
### of Brunswick-Lüneburg,
### Grand Master of the United Freemason Lodges, upon His
### presence in the Lodge of the Rue Garland in Gotha,
### by a member of the same. 1777.

---

Let others exalt the trophies of His battle-fame,
->How He avenged His fatherland,<-
And praise the daring spirit of His great deeds
->In many a victorious fray.<-

The Mason’s Muse flees those bloody scenes,
->Preferring to imagine him as the friend of mankind,<-
Whom even the enemy’s blessing follows, and His noble tears
->Which He wept at Krefeld;<-
<note>The Battle of Krefeld (June 23, 1758) was a major victory for Ferdinand during the Seven Years' War. The poet emphasizes Ferdinand's compassion for the suffering caused by war, a trait highly valued in Masonic circles.</note>

And deems itself happy, happy that of all
->Those initiated into Masonry<-
Ferdinand too, the Hero, has walked
->Within the sacred halls of her temple:<-

Ferdinand too, in the sacred halls of her temple,
->The hero, has walked:<-

<vocab>Duke Ferdinand of Brunswick-Lüneburg, Masonic Lodges, Gotha, Lodge of the Rue Garland, Krefeld, Masonry, Ode</vocab>

He who there boldly pulled back the manifold veil
->Of the hieroglyphs,<- <note>original: "Hieroglyphen." In the 18th century, Freemasons often viewed Egyptian hieroglyphs as symbols of ancient, hidden wisdom that only initiates could decode.</note>
And he, now familiar with the light, is no longer startled
->By the radiant fullness of the nearby sun.<-

Thus Solomon shone, and thus the mists vanished
->Before the Son of Wisdom;<- <note>King Solomon is a central figure in Masonic tradition, regarded as the builder of the Temple and a personification of divinely inspired wisdom.</note>
Kings came to see him, and stood
->Enchanted around his throne;<-

And returned wiser across their seas
->From his sanctuary,<-

->And the prosperity of their peoples:<-

The teaching of those divine feelings
->That first draws humanity together,<-
And which still blooms pure in our Lodges,
->As it once did by the Ganges and the Nile.<- <note>This reflects the 18th-century "prisca sapientia" (ancient wisdom) narrative, suggesting that Masonic truths are part of a universal tradition spanning from ancient India and Egypt to the present day.</note>

Hail to you, sublime Queen of the Arts,
->Hail to your proud structure—it stands!<- <note>The "Queen of the Arts" refers to the "Royal Art" (die Königliche Kunst), a common synonym for Freemasonry.</note>
A wonder to the profane, no mere flimsy web
->That every breath might blow away:<- <note>original: "Profan." This term refers to the uninitiated or the world outside the Masonic lodge.</note>

She sees the sons of princes walking her paths,
->Sees princes adorning her lap,<-
Not through the fame of their ancestors alone,
->No, great through their own merit;<-

And worthy that, on eternal wings,
->Their names shall speed toward all posterity,<-
And that the latest of our Brothers, full of silent awe,
->Shall linger at their urns:<- <note>original: "Urnen." A poetic reference to the monuments or the honored memory of deceased members of the order.</note>

# O d e
### to the Lodge Augusta of the Golden Crown in Stargard.

When misanthropy, an enemy of joy,
Commanded mortals to flee from me,
When virtue in the clothes of poverty,
And crime in purple was shown to me; <note>In 18th-century poetry, "purple" (Purpur) symbolizes royalty or high status, contrasting the outward wealth of the wicked with the inward "dress" of the virtuous poor.</note>

And when my gaze saw deception
Borne by high priests in the chariot of victory,
Saw war hunting across corpses,
And princes like Caligula; <note>Caligula was a Roman Emperor notorious for his tyranny and madness, often used as a literary archetype for a cruel ruler.</note>

Then descended—O what a sight, brothers!—
A square held in her hand,
Masonry down from the heavens;
Her magical garment was white. <term>square: Known as the "Winkelmaaß" in German, this tool is one of the most important symbols in Freemasonry, representing the regulation of one's actions and morality.</term>

Peace radiated from her eye,
And the harmony of beautiful souls,
And Pallas’s threatening Aegis,
And the belt of Venus adorned her. <note>Pallas (Athena) represents wisdom, while Venus represents love and beauty. The "Aegis" is Athena’s protective shield, suggesting that Masonic wisdom offers both protection and grace.</note>

> "Flee the wild laments of a Timon, <note>Timon refers to Timon of Athens, a legendary figure noted for his misanthropy (hatred of mankind). Masonry is instructing the young initiate to reject cynicism.</note>

> Not banish gentle brotherly love <note>original: "Bruderliebe." A central tenet of Freemasonry, emphasizing the bond between members and their duty to all humanity.</note>
> From within your sacred walls!

> So sadly, youth, and alone?

> Scatter flowers upon your path."

### 3.

### Regarding the monument which the Freemasons in Frankfurt an der Oder erected for the late Major von Kleist.
<note>Ewald Christian von Kleist (1715–1759) was a celebrated German poet and a Major in the Prussian army. He died following the Battle of Kunersdorf. His poem "Spring" (Der Frühling) was one of the most famous works of the era. The Freemasons of Frankfurt an der Oder, specifically the Lodge "Upright Heart" (Zum aufrichtigen Herzen), erected a monument to him in 1780.</note>

**To** exalt wisdom, strength, and beauty,
<note>In Masonic tradition, Wisdom, Strength, and Beauty are the "three pillars" that support a lodge. They represent the wisdom to contrive, the strength to support, and the beauty to adorn all great and important undertakings.</note>
Kleist must live forever in this marble;
Which the Masons raised to honor his virtues:
As a wise man he lived grandly, and died fighting as a hero!—
And even now, in every one of his songs,
We find the harmony of a most beautiful soul once more.

->v. Kahleke.<-

<summary>This page contains a brief commemorative poem honoring Ewald Christian von Kleist, a famous 18th-century poet and Prussian officer. It highlights the monument built for him by the Freemasons of Frankfurt an der Oder, celebrating his dual legacy as a literary "sage" and a military "hero."</summary>

<keywords>Ewald Christian von Kleist, Freemasonry, Frankfurt an der Oder, Enlightenment poetry, Masonic pillars, wisdom, strength, beauty, memorial, Kunersdorf</keywords>

### 4.
## Freemason’s Song.
->(From the Voss *Almanac of the Muses* of the year 1776.)<-
<note>The *Musen-Almanach* (Almanac of the Muses) was a highly influential annual literary journal in 18th-century Germany. The 1776 edition was edited by Johann Heinrich Voß, a famous poet and translator.</note>

Worthy brothers, strike up the song!
Arise! Greet the upright man,
Who into our free Order
Has just been admitted;
Who knows not what happened to him,
Because of the wonders that he saw!
<note>This refers to the Masonic initiation ritual, which involves symbolic ceremonies intended to surprise and enlighten the candidate—the "wonders" mentioned here.</note>

Dear brother, rejoice!
We too rejoice most heartily!
If you act as a Mason,
And walk the paths of wisdom;
As time goes by,
New truth shall unfold for you.

Do not lower, brother, your gaze
Back into the darkness!
Search deeper into the truth!
From the twilight go toward clarity!
Walk securely! Stumble not!
Until you fly from light to light!
<note>The movement from "darkness" or "twilight" to "light" and "clarity" is the central metaphor of the Enlightenment and Freemasonry, symbolizing the transition from ignorance to knowledge.</note>

Be of good cheer, and pay no heed
To what the fool and hypocrite says;
They, who judge us in the dark,
Weave lies into the truth!
What business has an upright man
With all the petty critics?
<term>petty critics</term> <note>original: "Splitterrichter." This is a biblical allusion to Matthew 7:3 regarding the "splinter" (mote) in one's neighbor's eye. It refers to censorious people who find small faults in others while ignoring their own major failings.</note>

<note>Vocabulary from the original text: Freymäurerlied (Freemason’s song), Freimaurer (Freemason), Orden (Order), Weisheit (Wisdom), Wahrheit (Truth), Musen-Almanach (Almanac of the Muses).</note>

<summary>This page features an initiation song for new Freemasons, originally published in 1776. The lyrics encourage the new "brother" to seek Enlightenment, leave ignorance behind, and ignore the criticisms of the uninitiated.</summary>

<keywords>Freemasonry, initiation song, Enlightenment, Johann Heinrich Voß, Musen-Almanach, Masonic ritual, wisdom, truth, light vs. darkness</keywords>

--- Page 238 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Freemason’s Song" from the previous page, concluding the celebratory lyrics with an exhortation to virtue and a ritual toast. The poem emphasizes the moral backbone required of a Mason and the communal bond shared by the brothers of the lodge.</meta>

->Hear what wisdom speaks:<-
->“Do right, and do not tremble!”<-
<note>This is a core Masonic tenet: the pursuit of integrity regardless of external pressure or fear.</note>
->Though a thousand enemies threaten him,<-
->The honest man shall fear nothing;<-
->He deviates neither to the left nor right,<-
->Feeling himself of divine lineage!<-
<note>The phrase "divine lineage" (göttliches Geschlechts) reflects the Enlightenment belief in the inherent dignity and "spark of divinity" within every human being, which is cultivated through virtue.</note>

->Brother, give us your hand,<-
->As a pledge of our friendship!<-
->To renew our covenant,<-
->Our brother shall rejoice:<-
->Masons, fill the glasses to the brim!<-
->Drink to our brother’s health!<-
<note>Ritual toasts, or "Table Lodges," were a standard part of Masonic gatherings, used to celebrate fellowship and the health of new members.</note>

->F. L. Count of Stolberg.<-
<note>Friedrich Leopold, Count of Stolberg-Stolberg (1750–1819), was a prominent German poet and diplomat. He was a member of the *Göttinger Hainbund*, a literary circle with many Masonic members.</note>

### 5.
## Bridal Song
### for three Freemason communities, upon the marriage of a brother.

->(From the Vossian Muse-Almanac of the year 1778.)<-
<note>The *Musen-Almanach* was a popular annual literary journal. This specific one was edited by Johann Heinrich Voss, a key figure in the *Göttinger Hainbund*, a group of poets who favored themes of nature, friendship, and virtue.</note>

God said: Let there be suns! The ether swelled,
And suns sprouted far around the Eternal One;
And behold, rushing like a storm of flames
Through infinity went God’s cedars.
<note>original: "Gottes Zedern." The poet uses the cedar, a tree often associated with the Temple of Solomon in Masonic tradition, as a metaphor for the majesty of the stars.</note>

God said: Let there be humans! His breath blew,
And humans sprouted—trees of life, they—
And bore fruit; in their shadow
The Son of the Eternal deigns to walk.

Whoever buds <term>wisdom</term>, and ripens <term>beauty</term> and <term>strength</term>,
<note>Wisdom, Beauty, and Strength are known as the "Three Lesser Lights" or the three pillars that support a Masonic lodge.</note>
Will one day take root by Eden’s crystal stream,
Transplanted by the Son, and rustles pure
Jubilation to the Lord, more than the sun-cedars.

Let us, <term>Jehovah</term>, Ruler in our grove’s
<note>In 18th-century Masonic poetry, "Jehovah" or the "Great Architect of the Universe" was frequently invoked as the supreme creator beyond specific denominational lines.</note>
Consecrated darkness, see your face shine,
And pour from your golden urn
Gentler blessing upon our brother,

Down upon our brother’s blooming bride:
That many fruitful offshoots may
Sprout for them, citizens of our
Solemn grove, worthy of the coming transplantation.
<note>The "grove" (Hain) refers to the lodge or the sacred space of the Masonic community. "Transplantation" is a metaphor for the soul being moved to the afterlife or the continuation of the lineage.</note>

->Voss.<-
<note>Johann Heinrich Voss (1751–1826) was a German poet and classicist, best known for his translation of Homer and his involvement in the Enlightenment literary scene.</note>

---

<summary>A Masonic poem by Johann Heinrich Voss celebrating the marriage of a lodge brother, using cosmic and botanical metaphors to link human creation to divine wisdom. The text emphasizes the Masonic virtues of Wisdom, Beauty, and Strength as the fruits of a well-lived life.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, Johann Heinrich Voss, Bridal Song, Wisdom, Beauty, Strength, Enlightenment poetry, Marriage, Creation imagery</keywords>

### 5.
## Bridal Song
### for three Freemason lodges, on the occasion of a brother’s marriage.

->(From the *Voss Musen-Almanach* of the year 1778.)<-
<note>The *Musen-Almanach* (Almanac of the Muses) was a highly influential annual literary journal edited by Johann Heinrich Voss. This specific song was likely performed during a "Loge de Table" or a festive joint meeting of three distinct Masonic lodges.</note>

God spoke: Let there be suns! The ether swelled,
And suns sprouted far around the Eternal One;
And behold, rushing like flaming storms
Through infinity, went God’s cedars.
<note>The poet uses the metaphor of "God's cedars" (original: *Gottes Zedern*) to describe the celestial bodies or suns, evoking the biblical cedars of Lebanon as symbols of strength and eternity.</note>

God spoke: Let there be humans! His breath blew,
And humans sprouted, they, the trees of life,
And bore fruit; in their shadow
The Son of the Eternal deigns to walk.

He who buds wisdom, ripens beauty and strength,
Shall one day take root by Eden’s crystal stream,
Transplanted by the Son, and rustle purer
Joy to the Lord than the sun-cedars.
<note>Here, Voss integrates the three "Pillars" of Freemasonry: <term>Wisdom, Beauty, and Strength</term> (original: *Weisheit, Schönheit und Stärke*). In Masonic ritual, these three qualities are necessary to complete any great work.</note>

Let us, Jehovah, Ruler in our grove’s
Consecrated darkness, see your face shine,
And pour from your golden urn
Milder blessings upon our brother,
<note>The "consecrated darkness" of the grove refers to the private, ritual space of the Masonic lodge, often associated in this era with the *Hain* (grove) imagery of the *Göttinger Hainbund* poets.</note>

Down upon our brother’s blooming bride:
So that many fruitful offspring
May sprout for them, citizens of our festive grove,
Worthy of the coming transplantation.
<note>The "transplantation" (original: *Verpflanzung*) is a poetic way of referring to the transition from this life to the next, or from the earthly lodge to the "Eternal Lodge."</note>

->Voß.<-
<note>Johann Heinrich Voss (1751–1826) was a German poet and translator famous for his German translation of Homer’s *Odyssey*. His work often emphasized virtue, family life, and the beauty of the natural world.</note>

---

<summary>This page contains a Masonic wedding hymn by the poet Johann Heinrich Voss, written for a joint celebration of three lodges. It uses arboreal imagery and the Masonic triad of Wisdom, Beauty, and Strength to bless the union of a brother and his bride.</summary>
<keywords>Bridal Song, Freemasonry, Johann Heinrich Voss, Wisdom, Beauty, Strength, Jehovah, Wedding, Musen-Almanach, Masonic Ritual</keywords>

### 6.

### The Bliss of Freemasonry.
->(From the appendix of the collected Masonic speeches of the year 1777.)<-

> Just as before Aurora’s <note>Aurora is the Roman goddess of the dawn; her "purple light" represents the rising sun of Enlightenment.</note> purple light
> The dark shadows give way:
> So can the face of Truth
> Frighten off the cowardly doubters.
> What makes us free from the tyranny
> Of powerful prejudices?
> ->Masonry.<-

> Oh see, how the clever ancients
> Depict the structure of the universe!
> They gathered the dew of wisdom,
> And hid it with images: <note>The "images" (original: *Bildern*) refer to the allegories and symbols used in Masonic rituals to convey moral truths.</note>
> They found miraculous power in Three. <note>The number three is foundational to Masonic symbolism, representing the three degrees (Entered Apprentice, Fellow Craft, Master Mason) and the three "Lesser Lights."</note>
> What teaches us what this mystery may be?
> ->Masonry.<-

> Highly we praise the Golden Age,
> When Saturn once reigned; <note>In Roman mythology, the reign of Saturn was a lost paradise of peace and equality. Masons often used this "Golden Age" as a metaphor for the fraternal harmony they sought to recreate in the lodge.</note>
> And led humanity to bliss
> With hands of peace.
> What restores for consecrated loyalty
> Destroyed monuments anew?
> ->Masonry.<-

<summary>This page contains a Masonic poem or song celebrating the "Bliss of Freemasonry," framing the craft as a source of Enlightenment and a restorer of ancient wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, Enlightenment, Aurora, Saturn, Golden Age, Symbolism, Sacred Number Three</keywords>
